<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:app-wpi-poly}
Fix $0\leq\beta<1$ and $D,M_0>0$.
There are constants $\Gamma>0$, depending only on $\beta$, and $C,B,N>0$, depending only on $(\beta,D,M_0)$, such that for $n\geq N$, with $\mathbb P_{0,n}$-probability at least $1-n^{-D}$,
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_A}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_A}\left[f\right]}\leq Cn^{7+B\Gamma}\varepsilon^{-\Gamma}\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu_A}\left(f,f\right)}+\varepsilon\operatorname{osc}(f)^2
\end{equation*}
simultaneously for every $\varepsilon\in[n^{-M_0},1/2]$ and every $f:\{\pm1\}^n\to\mathbb{R}$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:app-wpi-annealing}
Fix $0\leq\beta<1$ and $M,D>0$.
There is a randomized algorithm that starts from uniform product spins and uses Glauber dynamics along a deterministic increasing inverse-temperature schedule.
For all sufficiently large $n$, it runs in $n^{O_{\beta,M,D}(1)}$ time and, with $\mathbb P_{0,n}$-probability at least $1-n^{-D}$, produces a sample whose law is within $n^{-M}$ total variation of $\mu_A$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:intro-counting}
Fix $0\leq\beta<1$ and $M,D>0$.
There is a randomized algorithm that, given $A$ and $\eta\in(0,1/2]$, outputs an estimate $\widehat Z$ of $Z_A(0)$.
For all sufficiently large $n$, it runs in $n^{O_{\beta,M,D}(1)}\log(1/\eta)$ time and, with $\mathbb P_{0,n}$-probability at least $1-n^{-D}$, satisfies $\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left\vert\widehat Z/Z_A(0)-1\right\vert>n^{-M}\mid A\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{}\left[\left\vert\widehat Z/Z_A(0)-1\right\vert>n^{-M}\mid A\right]}\leq\eta$ simultaneously for every $\eta\in(0,1/2]$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-branches}
Let $\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\mathrm{sym},0}$ be the space of real symmetric zero-diagonal matrices.  
Fix $0<\beta_0<\beta_1<1$ and a constant $T>0$.
There is a constant $L$ such that, for every $n$ and every
$\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]$, there is a jointly Borel function
\begin{equation*}
 \overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}:
 \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\mathrm{sym},0}\times[0,T]
 \times\mathbb{R}^n\longrightarrow[0,4n]
\end{equation*}
with the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For every $A,t$ and $y_1,y_2\in\mathbb{R}^n$,
\begin{align*}
 \|m(A,y_1)-m(A,y_2)\|_2
 \le{}&L\|y_1-y_2\|_2
 +\sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;y_1)}
 +\sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;y_2)}.
\end{align*}

\item
{For every fixed $R>0$, there are $C_R,n_R>0$ such that,
for every $n\ge n_R$,
\begin{align*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \left\{
 \int_0^T
 \overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}
       (A,t;Y_t)\,\mathrm d t
 >C_R\log n
 \right\}
 &\le n^{-R},
\end{align*}
and
\begin{align*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \left\{
 \overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}
       (A,T;Y_T)
 >C_R\log n
 \right\}
 &\le n^{-R}.
\end{align*}
Here $\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}$ is the joint law where one draws the matrix $A\sim \mathbb P_{0,n}$, then draws $X \sim \mu_A$, then {sets} $Y_t=tX+B_t$ for an independent Brownian motion $B_t, t\geq0$.}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:polynomial-tap-residual}
Fix $0<\beta_*<1$ and $T>0$.
For every $H>0$, there are $C_H,n_H>0$, depending only on $(\beta_*,T,H)$, such that, for $n\ge n_H$,
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[0,\beta_*]}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
 \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}
 \left\{
 \left\|R_{\mathrm{TAP}}\bigl(m(A,Y_t);A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t,q_\beta(t)\bigr)\right\|_2^2
 >C_H\log n
 \right\}
 \le n^{-H}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:gaussian-enlargement-boosting}
Let $N\geq1$ be an integer, let $L>0$, and let $\xi\sim N(0,I_N)$.
Let $F:\mathbb{R}^N\to\mathbb{R}$ be an $L$-Lipschitz function, and let $q$ be a median of $F(\xi)$.
Then, for every $u\geq0$,
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[|F(\xi)-q|>u\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{}\left[|F(\xi)-q|>u\right]}\leq2\Phi(-u/L),
\end{equation*}
where $\Phi$ is the standard Gaussian distribution function.
Consequently, for every integer $p\geq1$, there is a constant $C_p>0$ depending only on $p$ such that
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|F(\xi)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[F(\xi)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[F(\xi)\right]}|^{2p}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|F(\xi)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[F(\xi)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[F(\xi)\right]}|^{2p}\right]}\leq C_pL^{2p}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:original-matrix-law}
For $n\geq1$ and $0\leq\beta<1$, let $\mathbb P_{0,n}$ be the law of a symmetric zero-diagonal matrix $A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ with independent entries $A_{ij}\sim N(0,1/n)$ for $i<j$.
Let $\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{diag}}$ be the product distribution on $\mathbb{R}^n$ with independent coordinates $N(\beta/n,2/n)$.
We draw $A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}$, $X\sim\mu_A$
and draw $d\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{diag}}$ independently of $(A,X)$ and set $Y_t=tX+B_t$ for $t\geq0$.

We denote the joint law of $(X,A,d,Y)$ by $\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}$, on the product space of spins $X\in\{\pm1\}^n$, zero-diagonal symmetric matrices $A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$, diagonals $d\in\mathbb{R}^n$, and paths $Y\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}^n)$.
Its $A$-marginal is $\mathbb P_{0,n}$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:tap-planted-model}
For $n\geq1$ and $0\leq\beta<1$, we draw $X\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm1\}^n)$ and a GOE matrix $W$, independently.
The entries $W_{ij}=W_{ji}\sim N(0,1/n)$ for $i<j$ and $W_{ii}\sim N(0,2/n)$ for $i\in[n]$ are independent over $i\leq j$.
Set $A^{\mathrm{full}}=W+\beta XX^\top/n$, let $A$ be its zero-diagonal part, write $d$ for its diagonal, and set $Y_t=tX+B_t$ for $t\geq0$.

We denote the joint law of $(X,A,d,Y)$ on the same product space as in \Cref{def:original-matrix-law} by $\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}$.
We denote its $A$-marginal by $\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl}}$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:planting-likelihood}
For $0\leq\beta<1$, we have
\begin{equation*}
  \sup_n\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^2\right]}
  \leq\left(1-\beta^2\right)^{-1/2},
  \qquad
  \sup_n\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-1}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-1}\right]}
  \leq\left(1-\beta^2\right)^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}
For every $n\geq1$ and every event $E$ determined by $(X,A,d,Y)$, we have
\begin{equation*}
  \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}\left(E\right)
  \leq(1-\beta^2)^{-1/4}\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}\left(E\right)^{1/2},
\qquad
  \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}\left(E\right)
  \leq(1-\beta^2)^{-1/4}\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}\left(E\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:gronwall-path-stability}
Let $a,L\geq0$ and let $d:[0,T]\to[0,\infty)$ be continuous.
If $d(t)\leq a+L\int_0^t d(s)\,\mathrm d s$ for every $t\in[0,T]$, then $d(t)\leq a\exp(Lt)$.
Consequently, for a globally $L$-Lipschitz function $b:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$, the solutions of
\begin{equation*}
 Y_t^\omega=\omega_t+\int_0^t b(Y_s^\omega)\,\mathrm d s
\end{equation*}
satisfy, for all $\omega,\omega'\in C_0([0,T];\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $h\in\mathbb H_T$,
\begin{equation*}
 \|Y^{\omega'}-Y^\omega\|_\infty
 \leq\exp(LT)\|\omega'-\omega\|_\infty,
 \qquad
 \|Y^{\omega+h}-Y^\omega\|_\infty
 \leq\sqrt T\exp(LT)\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Set $g(t)=a+L\int_0^t d(s)\,\mathrm d s$.
Since $g'(t)=Ld(t)\leq Lg(t)$ and $g(0)=a$, integration gives $d(t)\leq g(t)\leq a\exp(Lt)$.
For the path comparison, subtract the two integral equations and apply this bound to $d(t)=\sup_{s\leq t}\|Y_s^{\omega'}-Y_s^\omega\|_2$ with $a=\|\omega'-\omega\|_\infty$.
The second estimate follows from \cref{eq:cameron-martin-uniform-bound}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-two-set-gaussian}
Let $0<v\leq1/2$ and let $A_-,A_+$ be Borel subsets of $C_0([0,T];\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\gamma(A_-),\gamma(A_+)>v/4$.
There are $\omega\in A_-$ and $h\in\mathbb H_T$ such that $\omega+h\in A_+$ and $\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}\leq2\sqrt{2\log(4/v)}$.
In particular, for any Borel function $F:C_0([0,T];\mathbb{R}^n)\to\mathbb{R}$, writing $Q_F$ for its quantile function under $\gamma$, there are $\omega$ and $h\in\mathbb H_T$ such that
\begin{equation*}
 F(\omega)\leq Q_F(v),\qquad
 F(\omega+h)\geq Q_F(1-v),\qquad
 \|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}\leq2\sqrt{2\log(4/v)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let $\Phi$ be the standard Gaussian distribution function and set $r=\sqrt{2\log(4/v)}$, so that $\Phi(-r)<v/4$.
By \cite[Theorem~3.1]{Borell75}, the set $\{\omega+h:\omega\in A_-,\ h\in\mathbb H_T,\ \|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}\leq2r\}$ has inner Wiener measure at least $\Phi(r)>1-v/4$.
It therefore intersects $A_+$, which gives the first assertion.
For the quantile assertion, we take $A_-=\{F\leq Q_F(v)\}$ and $A_+=\{F\geq Q_F(1-v)\}$, each of Wiener measure at least $v$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:bdg-stochastic-integral}
Let $p\geq2$, let $(B_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}$ be a standard Brownian motion in $\mathbb{R}^n$ with respect to a filtration $(\mathcal F_t)$, and let $G_t\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}$ be predictable with respect to this filtration.
If $\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\int_0^T\|G_t\|_{\mathrm F}^2\,\mathrm d t)^{p/2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(\int_0^T\|G_t\|_{\mathrm F}^2\,\mathrm d t)^{p/2}\right]}<\infty$, then
\begin{equation*}
 \left(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\int_0^T G_t\,\mathrm d B_t\right\|_2^p\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left\|\int_0^T G_t\,\mathrm d B_t\right\|_2^p\right]}\right)^{1/p}
 \leq C_p\left(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\int_0^T\|G_t\|_{\mathrm F}^2\,\mathrm d t\right)^{p/2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left(\int_0^T\|G_t\|_{\mathrm F}^2\,\mathrm d t\right)^{p/2}\right]}\right)^{1/p},
\end{equation*}
where $C_p$ depends only on $p$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
We apply the upper bound in \cite[Theorem~1.1]{MarinelliRockner16} to the $\mathbb{R}^k$-valued continuous martingale $M_t=\int_0^tG_s\,\mathrm dB_s$.
Its scalar quadratic variation is $[M,M]_T=\int_0^T\|G_t\|_{\mathrm F}^2\,\mathrm d t$, and $\|M_T\|_2\leq\sup_{t\leq T}\|M_t\|_2$, giving the stated bound with a constant depending only on $p$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-observation-law}
For a fixed $A$, let $X\sim\mu_A$, and let $(B_t)_{0\leq t\leq T}$ be a standard Brownian motion in $\mathbb{R}^n$ with $B_0=0$, independent of $X$; set $Y_t=tX+B_t$.
Let $(\mathcal F_t)$ be the usual augmentation of the filtration $\sigma((Y_s)_{0\leq s\leq t})$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For every $t\in[0,T]$, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{Law}(X\mid\mathcal F_t)
 =\operatorname{Law}(X\mid Y_t)=\mu_{A,Y_t}.
\end{equation*}
In particular, $m(A,Y_t)=\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[X\mid\mathcal F_t\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[X\mid\mathcal F_t\right]}$ is a bounded martingale.
\item The innovation process $\widetilde B_t=Y_t-\int_0^t m(A,Y_s)\,\mathrm d s$ is a standard Brownian motion with respect to $(\mathcal F_t)$, and the field satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm dY_t=m(A,Y_t)\,\mathrm d t+\mathrm d\widetilde B_t.
\end{equation*}
The posterior mean satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d m(A,Y_t)=\Sigma(A,Y_t)\,\mathrm d\widetilde B_t.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, $Y$ has the same path distribution as $Y^\omega$ under $\omega\sim\gamma$.
All conclusions also hold for any full-support distribution $\mu$ on $\{\pm1\}^n$, replacing $\mu_A$ by $\mu$, $\mu_{A,y}$ by the normalized exponential tilt $\mu_y(x)\propto\mu(x)\exp(\langle y,x\rangle)$, and $m(A,y)$ and $\Sigma(A,y)$ by the mean and covariance of $\mu_y$, with the same replacement in the drift of \cref{eq:localization-path}.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
We apply \cite[Section~III]{ElAlaouiMontanari22} with $Q=I_n$: given $X=x$, the observed-path likelihood relative to Wiener measure is $\exp(\langle x,Y_t\rangle-t\|x\|_2^2/2)$, and the innovation representation makes $\widetilde B$ Brownian in $(\mathcal F_t)$.
Since $\|x\|_2^2=n$, Bayes' rule proves part~\textup{(i)}, and the definition of $\widetilde B$ gives the field SDE.
Summing the posterior-weight SDE in the same reference against $x$ gives the mean SDE in part~\textup{(ii)}.
Finally, uniqueness in \cref{eq:localization-path} gives $Y=Y^{\widetilde B}$, so the Brownian distribution of $\widetilde B$ proves the equality of path distributions.
The argument and the covariance bound above apply to every full-support base distribution on $\{\pm1\}^n$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:q-beta-regularity}
For every $0\leq\beta<1$ and $t\geq0$, the function $q\mapsto f(\beta^2q+t)$ takes $[0,1]$ into itself and is $\beta^2$-Lipschitz.
Hence \cref{eq:compact-rs-fixed-point} has a unique solution $q_\beta(t)\in[0,1]$.
Moreover, $q_\beta(0)=0$ and, for every $s,t\geq0$,
\begin{equation*}
 \left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\beta(s)\right\vert\leq\frac{\left\vertt-s\right\vert}{1-\beta^2}.
\end{equation*}
In particular, $q_\beta(t)\leq t/(1-\beta^2)$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
For the SK choice $\xi(q)=q^2/2$, \cite[Remark~2.3]{ElAlaouiMontanariSellke25} gives $\beta_1=1$, and its scalar function $\psi$ equals $f$.
Thus \cite[Lemmas~5.5--5.6]{ElAlaouiMontanariSellke25} gives the contraction and uniqueness statements, $q_\beta(0)=0$, and the $1$-Lipschitz property of $f$.
Subtracting the fixed-point equations at $s$ and $t$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\beta(s)\right\vert\leq\beta^2\left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\beta(s)\right\vert+\left\vertt-s\right\vert.
\end{equation*}
We rearrange this inequality to obtain the Lipschitz bound, then take $s=0$ to obtain the final bound.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:tap-cavity-pinning}
For any symmetric $J\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}$, external field $y\in\mathbb R^n$,
let $\mu=\mu_{J,y}$ have Gibbs weights proportional to $\exp(\langle \sigma,J\sigma\rangle/2+\langle y,\sigma\rangle)$, and write $m,\Sigma$ for its mean and covariance.
For coordinate $i\in[n]$, write $-i=[n]\setminus\{i\}$ for the surviving
coordinate set, $J^{\setminus i}=J_{-i,-i}$, and $a_i=J_{-i,i}$.
This deletion preserves $\ell$, and the surviving $M=n-1$ spins have inverse temperature $\beta_M=\sqrt{M\ell}=\beta\sqrt{M/n}$.

The cavity model is obtained by deleting coordinate $i$.  Its interaction
and external field are $J^{\setminus i}$ and $y_{-i}$, its Gibbs distribution is
denoted by $\mu^{\setminus i}$, and its mean and covariance are
$m^{\setminus i}$ and $\Sigma^{\setminus i}$.
In the planted model, we set
$
 \mathcal F=\sigma(J,y),\
 \mathcal F^{\setminus i}=\sigma(J^{\setminus i},y_{-i})
$,
and call $\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$ the cavity sigma-field.
Deleting coordinate $i$ discards $a_i$, $J_{ii}$, and $y_i$ without conditioning on the plant.
Under the planted law of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model}, given $\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$, the variables $X_{-i}\sim\mu^{\setminus i}$ and $X_i\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm1\})$ are independent.
The removed interaction vector satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 a_i=\ell X_iX_{-i}+\sqrt\ell\,G_i,
 \qquad G_i\sim N(0,I_{n-1}),
\end{equation*}
where $G_i$ is independent of $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},X)$.

We connect the cavity distribution to the conditional laws obtained by fixing $\sigma_i=\pm1$ through the exponential tilt $\exp(\gamma \langle a_i,\sigma_{-i}\rangle)$.
For the chosen $i$ and $\gamma\in[-1,1]$, define the cavity interpolation by
\begin{equation*}
 Z_\gamma
 \triangleq\sum_{\tau\in\{\pm1\}^{n-1}}\mu^{\setminus i}(\tau)
       \exp(\gamma\langle a_i,\tau\rangle),
 \qquad
 \mu_\gamma(\tau)
 \triangleq\frac{\mu^{\setminus i}(\tau)\exp(\gamma\langle a_i,\tau\rangle)}
 {Z_\gamma},
 \qquad \tau\in\{\pm1\}^{n-1}.
\end{equation*}
Thus the interaction remains $J^{\setminus i}$, while the external field at
parameter $\gamma$ is $y_{-i}+\gamma a_i$.
We write $m_\gamma$ and $\Sigma_\gamma$ for the mean and covariance under $\mu_\gamma$, and $\langle\cdot\rangle_\gamma$ for expectation under this distribution.
At $\gamma=0$, the interpolated law is the cavity law:
$Z_0=1$, $\mu_0=\mu^{\setminus i}$, $m_0=m^{\setminus i}$, and $\Sigma_0=\Sigma^{\setminus i}$.
For $b\in\{\pm1\}$, the endpoint $\gamma=b$ is the pinned law,
\begin{equation*}
 \mu_b(\tau)
 =\mu(\sigma_{-i}=\tau\mid\sigma_i=b),
 \qquad \tau\in\{\pm1\}^{n-1}.
\end{equation*}
To recover the marginal of $\mu$ on the surviving coordinates, we mix the pinned laws with weights $\pi_b=\mu(\sigma_i=b)=\frac{1+b(m)_i}{2}$.
If $m_{-i}$ denotes the restriction of the full mean $m$ to the
surviving coordinates, then
\[
 m_{-i}=\sum_{b=\pm1}\pi_b m_b,
 \qquad m^{\setminus i}\ne m_{-i}\quad\text{in general}.
\]
Given $i\in[n]$, external field $z\in\mathbb R^{n-1}$, and $u\in(-1,1)^{n-1}$, the empirical residual of the surviving system is
\begin{equation}
 R_{n-1}^{(Q)}(u;J^{\setminus i},z)
 =\operatorname{arctanh}(u)-z-J^{\setminus i}u+\ell\bigl(n-1-\left\Vertu\right\Vert_2^2\bigr)u.
 \label{eq:tap-empirical-residual}
\end{equation}
{For $n\ge2$, the empirical overlap here is $Q_{n-1}(u)=\|u\|_2^2/(n-1)$, and the Onsager coefficient equals $(n-1)\ell(1-Q_{n-1}(u))$.}
Here $\ell$ is held fixed after deletion, and the diagonal of $J^{\setminus i}$ is retained in the residual even though it cancels from the Gibbs distribution.
The cavity and pinned residuals are
\[
 \mathbf r^{\setminus i}
 =R_{n-1}^{(Q)}(m^{\setminus i};J^{\setminus i},y_{-i}),\qquad
 \mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^{i,b}
 =R_{n-1}^{(Q)}(m_b;J^{\setminus i},y_{-i}+ba_i).
\]
For fixed $i$, we abbreviate $\mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^b=\mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^{i,b}$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-overlap-mgf}
There are constants $\tau_0,K_0>0$, depending only on $\beta_*$, such that
the following statements hold for every $n\ge1$, $0\le \beta\le \beta_*$, and $t\ge0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The squared overlap satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle \exp\left(\tau_0n(R_{12}-q_{\beta}(t))^2\right)\right\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\left\langle \exp\left(\tau_0n(R_{12}-q_{\beta}(t))^2\right)\right\rangle\right]}
 \le K_0.
\end{equation*}
The same bound holds with $R_{12}$ replaced by $n^{-1}\sigma^1\cdot X$ or
$Q_n(m)=n^{-1}\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2^2$.

\item For every $p\geq1$,
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
   \left\langle|R_{12}-q_\beta(t)|^{2p}\right\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[
   \left\langle|R_{12}-q_\beta(t)|^{2p}\right\rangle\right]}
 \leq K_0\left(\frac p{\mathrm e\tau_0 n}\right)^p,
\end{equation*}
and the same moment bound holds for the replica--plant overlap and
$Q_n(m)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We use Gaussian interpolation with a decreasing quadratic coupling, as in Lata{\l}a's argument \cite[Corollary~1.4.7 and Proposition~1.4.8]{Talagrand11}.
In the planted model, we take a square root before averaging over the observations to cancel the posterior normalizing constant.
To compare the original planted model with an independent scalar-channel
model, we interpolate over $s\in[0,\beta^2]$.  Fix any $q\in[0,1]$ and
$\lambda_f\in(0,1-\beta^2)$, and define
\[
 t_s=t+(\beta^2-s)q,\qquad a = t_0,\qquad \lambda_s=\lambda_f+\beta^2-s,
 \qquad 0\le s\le \beta^2.
\]
Notice that
\[
 (s,t_s)=(\beta^2,t)\quad\text{at }s=\beta^2,
 \qquad (s,t_s)=(0,a)\quad\text{at }s=0,
 \qquad sq+t_s=a.
\]
For each $s$, let $\mathbb P_s$ be the law of the observations $(w,z)$
generated by first drawing $X\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm1\}^n)$, and then setting
\[
 w_{jk}=\sqrt{s/n}\,X_jX_k+G_{jk}\quad(j<k),\qquad
 z_j=\sqrt{t_s}\,X_j+g_j\quad(j\le n),
\]
where $(G_{jk})_{j<k}$ and $(g_j)_{j\le n}$ are mutually independent standard Gaussians, independent of $X$.
Let $\mu_s(\cdot\mid w,z)$ be the posterior distribution of $X$, and let
$\langle\cdot\rangle_s$ denote expectation over independent replicas from
$\mu_s(\cdot\mid w,z)$. Thus $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_s}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cdot\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_s}\left[\cdot\right]}$ averages the observations, while $\langle\cdot\rangle_s$ averages posterior replicas given those observations.

At $s=\beta^2$, we have $w_{jk}=\sqrt n\,A_{jk}$ and $\sqrt t\,z=Y_t$. So the interpolating model coincides with the fixed-time planted model and $\mu_{\beta^2}(\cdot\mid w,z)=\mu_{A,Y_t}$.
Thus, for every fixed-time observable \(F\) of finitely many replicas, \(\langle F\rangle\) is determined by \((A,Y_t)\) alone.
Averaging over $d$ and the other times of $Y$ therefore gives
$\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle F\rangle_{\beta^2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}\left[\langle F\rangle_{\beta^2}\right]}=\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle F\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\langle F\rangle\right]}$.

We now set
\[
 \Phi(s)
 \triangleq\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_s}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
 \left\langle\exp\left(\frac{\lambda_sn}{2}(R_{12}-q)^2\right)
 \right\rangle_s^{1/2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_s}\left[
 \left\langle\exp\left(\frac{\lambda_sn}{2}(R_{12}-q)^2\right)
 \right\rangle_s^{1/2}\right]}.
\]
Our goal is to prove that $\Phi(s)$ is nonincreasing for every $q\in[0,1]$, thereby bounding the
original endpoint by the independent endpoint.

\emph{Step 1: cancel the posterior normalizer.}
To cancel the posterior
normalizer, we compare $\mathbb P_s$ with the fixed reference law
$\mathbb Q$, under which all coordinates of $(w,z)$ are independent
standard Gaussians.

For each possible value $x\in\{\pm1\}^n$ of the planted spin $X$, the
standardized observation has, relative to $\mathbb Q$, mean-shift coordinates
\[
 \sqrt{s/n}\,x_jx_k\quad(j<k),\qquad
 \sqrt{t_s}\,x_j\quad(j\le n).
\]
The Gaussian shift formula gives the likelihood factor
\[
 L_{s,x}(w,z)
 \triangleq\exp(H_s(x)-v_s/2),
\]
where
\[
 H_s(x)=\sqrt{\frac sn}\sum_{j<k}w_{jk}x_jx_k
             +\sqrt{t_s}\sum_jz_jx_j.
\]
The configuration $x$ is
fixed in this likelihood calculation.  In particular,
$\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cdot\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\cdot\right]}$ below averages only
over the reference observation $(w,z)$ and does not average over either
$X$ or $x$.  By independence and centering, all cross terms have expectation zero,
so
\[
 v_s
 \triangleq\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_s(x)^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[H_s(x)^2\right]}
 =\frac{s}{n}\sum_{j<k}(x_jx_k)^2
   +t_s\sum_jx_j^2
 =\frac{s(n-1)}{2}+nt_s.
\]
This is independent of $x$ because every spin satisfies
$x_j^2=1$: all possible plants produce mean-shift vectors with the
same Euclidean norm.
$L_{s,x}$ is a likelihood factor, not a posterior mass.
Averaging it over the uniform prior $\pi(x)\triangleq2^{-n}$, $x\in\{\pm1\}^n$, we set $L_s=\sum_x\pi(x)L_{s,x}$.
The planted observation law defined above therefore satisfies
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d\mathbb P_s}{\mathrm d\mathbb Q}(w,z)=L_s(w,z).
\]
Bayes' formula also gives the explicit posterior distribution
\[
 \mu_s(\sigma\mid w,z)
 =\frac{\pi(\sigma)L_{s,\sigma}(w,z)}{L_s(w,z)}.
\]
We define the unnormalized overlap-tilted two-replica numerator by
\[
 \mathcal Z_s
 \triangleq\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\pi(\sigma)\pi(\tau)L_{s,\sigma}L_{s,\tau}
       \exp\left(\lambda_sn(R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2/2\right).
\]
We define the conditional exponential moment by
$
 \mathcal M_s
 \triangleq\left\langle
      \exp\left(\lambda_sn(R_{12}-q)^2/2\right)
    \right\rangle_s
$.
Using the preceding expression for $\mu_s$ in the two-replica sum, we have
\[
 \mathcal M_s
 =\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\mu_s(\sigma\mid w,z)\mu_s(\tau\mid w,z)
      \exp\left(\lambda_sn(R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2/2\right)
 =\frac{\mathcal Z_s}{L_s^2}.
\]
The square root is chosen specifically to remove the random normalizer:
the outer observation law contributes a factor $L_s$, whereas
$\sqrt{\mathcal M_s}=\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}/L_s$.  We substitute Bayes'
formula into the change of measure $\mathrm d\mathbb P_s=L_s\mathrm d\mathbb Q$
and cancel $L_s$ to obtain
\begin{equation}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_s}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_s}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_s}\right]}
 =\int L_s\frac{\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}}{L_s}\mathrm d\mathbb Q
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}.
 \label{eq:tap-bayes-cancellation}
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2: prove monotonicity of the square-root interpolation.}
We define
$\Psi(s)=\log\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}=\log\Phi(s)$ and prove that it is
nonincreasing on $[0,\beta^2]$.  For fixed reference observations, we define
the overlap-tilted replica-pair law
\[
 \rho_s(\sigma,\tau\mid w,z)
 \triangleq
 \frac{1}{\mathcal Z_s} \pi(\sigma)\pi(\tau)L_{s,\sigma}L_{s,\tau}
       \exp\left(\lambda_sn(R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2/2\right).
\]
For a function $F$ of one pair, we set
\[
 \langle F\rangle_{\rho_s}
 \triangleq\sum_{\sigma^1,\sigma^2}
       \rho_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^2\mid w,z)F(\sigma^1,\sigma^2).
\]
For a function $F$ of two pairs, we set
\[
 \langle F\rangle_{\rho_s^{\otimes2}}
 \triangleq\sum_{\sigma^1,\ldots,\sigma^4}
       \rho_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^2\mid w,z)
       \rho_s(\sigma^3,\sigma^4\mid w,z)
       F(\sigma^1,\sigma^2,\sigma^3,\sigma^4).
\]
Thus the two overlap-tilted pairs are conditionally independent given $(w,z)$.
We include the outer reweighting by defining
\[
 \mathopen{[\![}F\mathclose{]\!]}_s
 \triangleq\frac{\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}
       \langle F\rangle_{\rho_s^{\otimes2}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}
       \langle F\rangle_{\rho_s^{\otimes2}}\right]}}
      {\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}}.
\]
Under $\mathbb Q$, the collection $H_s=(H_s(\eta))_{\eta\in\{\pm1\}^n}$
is a centered $2^n$-dimensional Gaussian vector with covariance kernel
$\mathcal K_s(\sigma,\tau)=\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_s(\sigma)H_s(\tau)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[H_s(\sigma)H_s(\tau)\right]}$.
For $h=(h_\eta)_{\eta\in\{\pm1\}^n}$, we define the deterministic function
\[
 \mathcal Z_s(h)
 \triangleq
 \sum_{\sigma,\tau}\pi(\sigma)\pi(\tau)
 \exp\left(h_\sigma+h_\tau-v_s
       +\frac{\lambda_sn}{2}(R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2\right)
 .
\]
The random variable defined above is its evaluation
$\mathcal Z_s=\mathcal Z_s(H_s)$.
We write $\partial_\eta$ for differentiation with respect to $h_\eta$.
In every coordinate derivative below, we differentiate the corresponding
function of $h$ and then evaluate at $h=H_s$; we suppress this final
substitution from the notation.
Keeping $q$ and $a$ fixed, we apply the standard Gaussian interpolation
differentiation formula to the centered Gaussian vector $H_s$ and the
deterministic function $h\mapsto \sqrt{\mathcal Z_s(h)}$:
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s(h)}\big|_{h=H_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s(h)}\big|_{h=H_s}\right]}
  +\frac12\sum_{\eta,\xi}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\eta,\xi)
       \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\partial_\eta\partial_\xi\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\partial_\eta\partial_\xi\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}.
\]
The partial derivative holds $h$ fixed and differentiates only
$v_s$ and $\lambda_s$.  Its contribution is
\[
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}}
 \left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
      \sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right|_{h=H_s}
 =
   -\frac12\frac{\mathrm d v_s}{\mathrm d s}
   +\frac n4\frac{\mathrm d\lambda_s}{\mathrm d s}
      \langle(R_{12}-q)^2\rangle_{\rho_s}.
\]

For one overlap-tilted pair, we set
$n_\eta=\1_{\{\sigma^1=\eta\}}+\1_{\{\sigma^2=\eta\}}$.
{We differentiate the finite sum defining $\mathcal Z_s$ and its normalizing factor to obtain}
\begin{gather*}
 \partial_\eta\log\mathcal Z_s
 =\langle n_\eta\rangle_{\rho_s},\\
 \partial_\eta\partial_\xi\log\mathcal Z_s
 =\langle n_\eta n_\xi\rangle_{\rho_s}
   -\langle n_\eta\rangle_{\rho_s}\langle n_\xi\rangle_{\rho_s},\\
 \frac{\partial_\eta\partial_\xi\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}}
      {\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}}
 =\frac12\langle n_\eta n_\xi\rangle_{\rho_s}
   -\frac14\langle n_\eta\rangle_{\rho_s}
              \langle n_\xi\rangle_{\rho_s}.
\end{gather*}
The last identity follows from
$\partial_\eta\partial_\xi\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}/\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}
=\tfrac12\partial_\eta\partial_\xi\log\mathcal Z_s
+\tfrac14(\partial_\eta\log\mathcal Z_s)
            (\partial_\xi\log\mathcal Z_s)$.

Using $\mathcal K_s(\sigma,\sigma)=v_s$, we contract the two Hessian terms as
\begin{gather*}
 \sum_{\eta,\xi}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\eta,\xi)
       \langle n_\eta n_\xi\rangle_{\rho_s}
 =\left\langle\sum_{a,b=1}^2\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^a,\sigma^b)\right\rangle_{\rho_s}
  =2\frac{\mathrm d v_s}{\mathrm d s}+2\langle\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle_{\rho_s},\\
 \sum_{\eta,\xi}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\eta,\xi)
       \langle n_\eta\rangle_{\rho_s}\langle n_\xi\rangle_{\rho_s}
 =\left\langle\sum_{a=1}^2\sum_{b=3}^4
       \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^a,\sigma^b)\right\rangle_{\rho_s^{\otimes2}}
  =4\left\langle
       \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^3)\right\rangle_{\rho_s^{\otimes2}}.
\end{gather*}
For each fixed $(w,z)$, $\rho_s(\sigma,\tau\mid w,z)$ is symmetric in
$\sigma,\tau$, which gives the last equality by exchanging the two
coordinates within either pair.
Multiplying by $\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}$ and averaging under $\mathbb Q$ preserves
this identity.
Thus the second contraction, after outer reweighting, is
$4\mathopen{[\![}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^3)\mathclose{]\!]}_s$.
Dividing by $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb Q}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal Z_s}\right]}$ and substituting the preceding
identities, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\mathrm d\Psi(s)}{\mathrm d s}
 =&~-\frac12\frac{\mathrm d v_s}{\mathrm d s}
   +\frac n4\frac{\mathrm d\lambda_s}{\mathrm d s}
      \mathopen{[\![}(R_{12}-q)^2\mathclose{]\!]}_s\\
 &~+\frac14\left(2\frac{\mathrm d v_s}{\mathrm d s}
       +2\mathopen{[\![}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\mathclose{]\!]}_s\right)
       -\frac18\left(4
       \mathopen{[\![}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^3)\mathclose{]\!]}_s\right)\\
 =&~\frac12\mathopen{[\![}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\mathclose{]\!]}_s
   -\frac12\mathopen{[\![}\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma^1,\sigma^3)\mathclose{]\!]}_s
   +\frac n4\frac{\mathrm d\lambda_s}{\mathrm d s}
      \mathopen{[\![}(R_{12}-q)^2\mathclose{]\!]}_s.
\end{aligned}
 \label{eq:tap-gaussian-pair-derivative}
\end{equation}

We compute the covariance derivative by setting $u_j=\sigma_j\tau_j$.  Then
\[
 \sum_ju_j=nR(\sigma,\tau),\qquad
 \sum_{j<k}u_ju_k
 =\frac12\left(\left(\sum_ju_j\right)^2-n\right).
\]
Since $t_s=a-sq$, we have
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal K_s(\sigma,\tau)
 =&~\frac{s}{n}\sum_{j<k}u_ju_k+t_s\sum_ju_j
 =\frac{sn}{2}R(\sigma,\tau)^2-\frac s2
   +n(a-sq)R(\sigma,\tau),
\end{align*} 
\begin{align*}  
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d s}\mathcal K_s(\sigma,\tau)
 =&~\frac n2R(\sigma,\tau)^2-\frac12-nqR(\sigma,\tau)
 =\frac n2(R(\sigma,\tau)-q)^2-\frac{1+nq^2}{2}.
\end{align*}
Since $\frac{\mathrm d\lambda_s}{\mathrm d s}=-1$, we substitute the covariance derivative
into \cref{eq:tap-gaussian-pair-derivative}:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
 \Psi'(s)
 =&~\frac12\mathopen{[\![}\tfrac n2(R_{12}-q)^2-\tfrac{1+nq^2}{2}\mathclose{]\!]}_s
   -\frac12\mathopen{[\![}\tfrac n2(R_{13}-q)^2-\tfrac{1+nq^2}{2}\mathclose{]\!]}_s
   -\frac n4\mathopen{[\![}(R_{12}-q)^2\mathclose{]\!]}_s\\
 =&~-\frac n4\mathopen{[\![}(R_{13}-q)^2\mathclose{]\!]}_s\le0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
All derivatives above are justified on compact subintervals where $s>0$
and $t_s>0$: the sums over spins are finite, and their derivatives are
bounded by a polynomial in $(w,z)$ times $C\exp(C\|(w,z)\|_2)$,
which is integrable under $\mathbb Q$.  If $t_s$ is identically zero, the scalar
Gaussian term is absent.  At the endpoints, the same integrable bound
and continuity of the square-root coefficients justify passage to the
limit.  Thus $\Psi$ is nonincreasing on the entire interpolation interval
for every $q\in[0,1]$.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3: use the independent-coordinate endpoint.}
At $s=0$, the coefficient of the matrix term is zero, so the posterior distribution factors over coordinates and only independent scalar channels of strength $a$ remain.
For a scalar observation $z_j=\sqrt a\,X_j+g_j$, the posterior mean is
$\tanh(\sqrt a\,z_j)$.  Thus the conditional mean of the product of two
independent posterior replicas is $\tanh^2(\sqrt a\,z_j)$.  By symmetry,
$X_j\sqrt a\,z_j=a+\sqrt a\,X_jg_j$ has the same distribution as
$a+\sqrt a\,G$ for $G\sim N(0,1)$, and hence
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2\rangle_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\langle\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2\rangle_0\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\tanh^2(a+\sqrt a\,G)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,1)}\left[\tanh^2(a+\sqrt a\,G)\right]}=f(a).
\]
We choose $q=f(a)$.  Together with $a=\beta^2q+t$, this gives
$q=f(\beta^2q+t)$, and hence $q=q_\beta(t)$ by \Cref{lem:q-beta-regularity}.
Since both the observation channel and the posterior distribution factor over coordinates, the products $\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2$ are independent signs with mean $q$ after the outer planted average.
The variables $\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2-q$ are independent, centered,
and each lies in an interval of length two.  We factor their exponential
moment and apply Hoeffding's lemma with parameter $z/\sqrt n$ to each factor:
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle
   \exp\left(\frac z{\sqrt n}\sum_j(\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2-q)\right)
   \right\rangle_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\left\langle
   \exp\left(\frac z{\sqrt n}\sum_j(\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2-q)\right)
   \right\rangle_0\right]}
=\prod_{j=1}^n\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
   \left\langle\exp\left(\frac z{\sqrt n}(\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2-q)\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[
   \left\langle\exp\left(\frac z{\sqrt n}(\sigma_j^1\sigma_j^2-q)\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}
 \leq\prod_{j=1}^n\exp\left(\frac{z^2}{2n}\right)
 =\exp\left(\frac{z^2}{2}\right).
\end{align*}
Since $\lambda_0=\lambda_f+\beta^2<1$,
we set $W=\sqrt n(R_{12}-q)$ and introduce an independent
$G_0\sim N(0,1)$.  For every fixed value of $W$, we evaluate the standard
Gaussian moment-generating function (MGF) and obtain
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G_0\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\sqrt{\lambda_0}G_0W\right)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G_0\sim N(0,1)}\left[\exp\left(\sqrt{\lambda_0}G_0W\right)\right]}
 =\exp\left(\lambda_0W^2/2\right).
\]
Here the expectation over $W$ averages both the planted observations and the posterior replicas.
Since the integrands are nonnegative, we may average first over $W$ or first over
$G_0$. We condition on $G_0$ and apply the preceding sub-Gaussian estimate $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\exp(zW)\rangle_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\langle\exp(zW)\rangle_0\right]}\leq\exp(z^2/2)$ with $z=\sqrt{\lambda_0}G_0$; therefore
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal M_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\mathcal M_0\right]}
 =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle
       \exp\left(\lambda_0W^2/2\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\left\langle
       \exp\left(\lambda_0W^2/2\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{G_0\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
      \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle
       \exp\left(\sqrt{\lambda_0}G_0W\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\left\langle
       \exp\left(\sqrt{\lambda_0}G_0W\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G_0\sim N(0,1)}\left[
      \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle
       \exp\left(\sqrt{\lambda_0}G_0W\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\left\langle
       \exp\left(\sqrt{\lambda_0}G_0W\right)\right\rangle_0\right]}\right]}
 \leq~\ifthenelse{\isempty{G_0\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda_0G_0^2/2\right)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G_0\sim N(0,1)}\left[\exp\left(\lambda_0G_0^2/2\right)\right]}.
\end{align*}
Finally, we evaluate the Gaussian integral using $0<\lambda_0<1$ and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G_0\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda_0G_0^2/2\right)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G_0\sim N(0,1)}\left[\exp\left(\lambda_0G_0^2/2\right)\right]}
 =\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb R}
      \exp\left(-(1-\lambda_0)x^2/2\right)\mathrm d x
 =(1-\lambda_0)^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}

At the other endpoint, $\lambda_{\beta^2}=\lambda_f$.
We now transport the estimate from $s=0$ to $s=\beta^2$.  We combine monotonicity
of $\Psi$ with \cref{eq:tap-bayes-cancellation} and obtain
$
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_{\beta^2}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_{\beta^2}}\right]}
 \le \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_0}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_0}\right]}
$.
We apply Cauchy--Schwarz under $\mathbb P_0$ and the endpoint bound:
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_0}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_0}\right]}
 \leq\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_0}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal M_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_0}\left[\mathcal M_0\right]}^{1/2}
 \leq(1-\beta^2-\lambda_f)^{-1/4}.
\end{equation*}
On the other hand, for fixed observations we apply
Cauchy--Schwarz under the posterior distribution and obtain
\[
 \left\langle \exp\left(\lambda_fn(R_{12}-q)^2/4\right)\right\rangle_{\beta^2}
 \le\sqrt{\mathcal M_{\beta^2}}.
\]
We combine these estimates and obtain
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle \exp\left(\lambda_fn(R_{12}-q)^2/4\right)\right\rangle_{\beta^2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}\left[\left\langle \exp\left(\lambda_fn(R_{12}-q)^2/4\right)\right\rangle_{\beta^2}\right]}
 \le \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_{\beta^2}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{\beta^2}}\left[\sqrt{\mathcal M_{\beta^2}}\right]}
 \le (1-\beta^2-\lambda_f)^{-1/4}.
\]
We choose $\lambda_f=(1-\beta^2)/2$.  Then
$\lambda_f+\beta^2=(1+\beta^2)/2<1$, the exponent
$\lambda_f/4=(1-\beta^2)/8$ is at least
$\tau_0=(1-\beta_*^2)/8$, and the right side is bounded by $K_0$.
Because the exponent contains a square, decreasing it from
$\lambda_f/4$ to $\tau_0$ can only decrease the integrand.  Zero channel
strengths follow by continuity.

For completeness, given $(A,Y_t)$, the true spin $X$ and a fresh posterior replica have the same law $\mu_{A,Y_t}$.
We draw $\sigma^1$ independently of $X$ given these observations.  Then
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle F(\sigma^1,X)\rangle\mid A,Y_t\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\langle F(\sigma^1,X)\rangle\mid A,Y_t\right]}
 =\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\mu_{A,Y_t}(\sigma)\mu_{A,Y_t}(\tau)F(\sigma,\tau)
 =\langle F(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle.
\end{equation*}
We average over $(A,Y_t)$ to obtain the Nishimori identity
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle F(\sigma^1,X)\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\langle F(\sigma^1,X)\rangle\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle F(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\langle F(\sigma^1,\sigma^2)\rangle\right]}.
\]
This proves the replica--plant version.  Finally, we use the independence of two replicas given $(A,Y_t)$ to compute
$Q_n(m)=\frac1n\sum_jm_j^2=\langle R_{12}\rangle$.
For fixed $(A,Y_t)$, we apply Jensen's inequality to the convex function
$x\mapsto \exp(\tau_0nx^2)$ and obtain
\[
 \exp\left(\tau_0n(Q_n(m)-q)^2\right)
 \le\left\langle \exp\left(\tau_0n(R_{12}-q)^2\right)\right\rangle,
\]
which proves the posterior-mean version.  The independent auxiliary
diagonal changes none of these posterior identities.

Finally, for $z>0$,
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dz}\bigl(z^p\exp(-\tau_0z)\bigr)
 =z^{p-1}\exp(-\tau_0z)(p-\tau_0z).
\]
Thus $z^p\exp(-\tau_0z)$ is maximized at $z=p/\tau_0$, where its
value is $(p/(\mathrm e\tau_0))^p$.  The same bound is trivial at $z=0$,
and hence, for every $z\geq0$,
$
 z^p\leq\left(\frac{p}{\mathrm e\tau_0}\right)^p
 \exp(\tau_0z).
$
We apply this with $z=n(R_{12}-q_\beta(t))^2$, use the exponential bound,
and divide by $n^p$.  The replica--plant and posterior-mean moment bounds
follow from their corresponding exponential bounds in the same way.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:tap-covariance}
Consider the covariance $\Sigma$ in the fixed-time representation above, with $n\ell\leq\beta_*^2$ and $t\geq0$.
Given $(J,y)$, let $\sigma^1,\ldots,\sigma^4$ be independent samples from $\mu_{J,y}$ and write $\langle\cdot\rangle$ for their expectation.
We define the four-replica quantity $\mathcal D_{1234}$ and state its relation to the covariance as
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal D_{1234}=\frac14
 \bigl((\sigma^1-\sigma^2)\cdot(\sigma^3-\sigma^4)\bigr)^2,
 \qquad
 \left\langle\mathcal D_{1234}\right\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma^2.
\end{equation*}
There is a constant $K>0$, depending only on $\beta_*$, such that the following statements hold, where all $L^p$ norms and outer expectations are taken over $(J,y)$ under the same planted model.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The four-replica moments satisfy
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\rangle\right]}
 \leq(K\sqrt{np})^p\qquad(p\geq2).
\end{equation*}

\item Consequently, the covariance trace moments satisfy
\begin{equation*}
 \|\operatorname{Tr} \Sigma^2\|_{L^p}\le Knp\quad(p\ge1).
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
For fixed observations, we have
$\Sigma_{jk}=\langle(\sigma_j-m_j)(\sigma_k-m_k)\rangle$.  Two independent
replicas satisfy
\[
 \left\langle(\sigma_j^1-\sigma_j^2)
                 (\sigma_k^1-\sigma_k^2)\right\rangle
 =2\langle\sigma_j\sigma_k\rangle-2m_jm_k=2\Sigma_{jk}.
\]
We use the independence of the two replica pairs given the observations to compute
\begin{equation}
 \langle\mathcal D_{1234}\rangle
 =\frac14\sum_{j,k}
 \langle(\sigma_j^1-\sigma_j^2)(\sigma_k^1-\sigma_k^2)\rangle
 \langle(\sigma_j^3-\sigma_j^4)(\sigma_k^3-\sigma_k^4)\rangle
 =\sum_{j,k}\Sigma_{jk}^2=\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma^2.
\label{eq:tap-covariance-rectangle}
\end{equation}
Since $R_{ab}=n^{-1}\sigma^a\cdot\sigma^b$,
$\mathcal D_{1234}=\frac{n^2}{4}
(R_{13}-R_{14}-R_{23}+R_{24})^2$.
We write $q=q_{\beta}(t)$.
The common center $q$ cancels in this alternating sum, so taking the $r$th power gives the first identity below.
We then combine the elementary inequality $|x_1+\cdots+x_4|^r\le4^{r-1}\sum_{\alpha=1}^4|x_\alpha|^r$ and the overlap-moment conclusion of \Cref{lem:tap-overlap-mgf}-\textup{(ii)} to obtain, for $r\ge1$,
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{r}\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{r}\rangle\right]}
 =&~\frac{n^{2r}}{4^r}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
 \langle|(R_{13}-q)-(R_{14}-q)
 -(R_{23}-q)+(R_{24}-q)|^{2r}\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[
 \langle|(R_{13}-q)-(R_{14}-q)
 -(R_{23}-q)+(R_{24}-q)|^{2r}\rangle\right]}\\
 \leq&~\frac{n^{2r}}{4^r}4^{2r-1}
 \sum_{(a,b)\in\{(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4)\}}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle|R_{ab}-q|^{2r}\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\langle|R_{ab}-q|^{2r}\rangle\right]}\\
 \leq&~4^rK_0\left(\frac{nr}{\mathrm e\tau_0}\right)^r
 \leq(C n r)^r.
\end{align*}
Taking $r=p/2$ for $p\geq2$ proves part~\textup{(i)}:
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\rangle\right]}
 \leq(C\sqrt{np})^p.
\end{equation*}
For part~\textup{(ii)}, we fix the observations, apply Jensen's inequality with exponent $p\geq1$, and then average the observations:
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma^2)^p\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma^2)^p\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}\rangle^p\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}\rangle^p\right]}
 \le\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^p\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^p\rangle\right]},
\]
and the bound above with $r=p$ yields $\|\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma^2\|_{L^p}\le C n p$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-gaussian-cancellation}
For the cavity interpolation of \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning}, assume $M=n-1\geq1$ and $0\leq\ell M\leq1$.
We condition on $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$ and let $x$ be any $[-1,1]$-valued random variable that may depend on $G$ and additional randomness.
Under this conditioning, $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathrm{row}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cdot\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathrm{row}}\left[\cdot\right]}$ and $L^p(\mathrm{row})$ average over $G$ and the additional randomness used to construct $x$, while $\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cdot\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\cdot\right]}$ and $L^p(G)$ average $G$ alone.
We define
\[
 \overline m_x=\tfrac{1+x}{2}m_{+1}+\tfrac{1-x}{2}m_{-1},
\]
\[
r_{\mathrm{row}}
 =\tfrac12(\log Z_{+1}-\log Z_{-1})-a\cdot \overline m_x
       +\ell(M-\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2^2)x.
\]
Then, for every $p\geq2$, there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p$, such that
\begin{equation*}
 \|r_{\mathrm{row}}\|_{L^p(\mathrm{row})}\leq C_p\ell\sup_{|\gamma|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-complete-row}
For $\beta=\sqrt{n\ell}\leq\beta_*$ and $t\geq0$, the following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The conditional second moment of $r_i$ satisfies, almost surely,
\begin{equation*}
 e_i
 \le C\ell^2\bigl(1+\operatorname{Tr}[(\Sigma^{\setminus i})^2]\bigr)+4\ell.
\end{equation*}

\item For every $p\ge1$,
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_i\|e_i\|_{L^p}\le\frac{C\cdot p}{n}.
\end{equation*}

\item For every fixed $p\ge2$,
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_i\|r_i\|_{L^p}\le C_pn^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
Here $C$ depends only on $\beta_*$, and $C_p$ depends only on $p,\beta_*$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We write $J_{ii}=\ell+\zeta_i$, where $\zeta_i\sim N(0,2\ell)$ is independent of the plant and all other observations.
When $n=1$, we have
$m_1=\tanh y_1$ and $r_1=-\ell m_1^3-\zeta_1m_1$; independence
of $\zeta_1$ gives all three bounds directly.  We now assume $n\geq2$.
We set $M=n-1$, $x=m_i$, and $a=a_i$, and use the cavity interpolation of \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning}.
For $b\in\{\pm1\}$, we obtain
\[
 \tfrac{1+bx}{2}=\mu(\sigma_i=b)\propto\exp(by_i)Z_b.
\]
Their ratio is $(1+x)/(1-x)=\exp(2y_i)Z_{+1}/Z_{-1}$; taking half its logarithm, we obtain
\[
 \operatorname{arctanh}\left(x\right)-y_i
 =\tfrac12(\log Z_{+1}-\log Z_{-1}).
\]
By the mixture identity in \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning}, $\overline m_x=m_{-i}$, with $\overline m_x$ as in \Cref{lem:tap-gaussian-cancellation}.
Substituting $n=M+1$, $\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2^2=x^2+\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2^2$, and $J_{ii}=\ell+\zeta_i$ into the {empirical residual (with $q=Q_n(m)=\|m\|_2^2/n$)} in \cref{eq:tap-prescribed-residual}, we obtain
\[
 r_i
 =\tfrac12(\log Z_{+1}-\log Z_{-1})
   -a\cdot\overline m_x-(\ell+\zeta_i)x
   +\ell(M+1-x^2-\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2^2)x.
\]
Using $r_{\mathrm{row}}$ from \Cref{lem:tap-gaussian-cancellation}, we obtain
\begin{equation}
 r_i=r_{\mathrm{row}}-\ell x^3-\zeta_i x.
 \label{eq:tap-complete-row-identity}
\end{equation}

For every fixed $p\geq2$, we condition on $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$, apply \Cref{lem:tap-gaussian-cancellation} with $x=m_i$, and then apply \Cref{lem:tap-weak-tilt} to its covariance supremum.
The dependence of $x$ on $G$ and the removed scalar observation is allowed by \Cref{lem:tap-gaussian-cancellation}; since $\ell M\leq1$ and $\mu_0=\mu^{\setminus i}$, we obtain, almost surely,
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
 |r_{\mathrm{row}}|^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[
 |r_{\mathrm{row}}|^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
 \leq C_p\ell^p
 \left\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\right\rangle_{(\mu^{\setminus i})^{\otimes4}}.
\]
We first use this estimate at $p=2$, when $\langle\mathcal D_{1234}\rangle_{(\mu^{\setminus i})^{\otimes4}}=\operatorname{Tr}[(\Sigma^{\setminus i})^2]$.

The auxiliary diagonal contributes only a constant to the Hamiltonian, so $x$ and $r_{\mathrm{row}}$ do not depend on $\zeta_i$.
By independence, the cross term involving $\zeta_i$ in the square of \cref{eq:tap-complete-row-identity} has conditional expectation zero.
Using $|x|\le1$ and $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\zeta_i\sim N(0,2\ell)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\zeta_i^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\zeta_i\sim N(0,2\ell)}\left[\zeta_i^2\right]}=2\ell$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[r_i^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
 =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(r_{\mathrm{row}}-\ell x^3)^2
                 \mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[(r_{\mathrm{row}}-\ell x^3)^2
                 \mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
   +\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\zeta_i^2x^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\zeta_i^2x^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}\\
 \le&~2\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_{\mathrm{row}}^2
                 \mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[r_{\mathrm{row}}^2
                 \mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
      +2\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ell^2 x^6\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\ell^2 x^6\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
      +2\ell\\
 \le&~C\ell^2
       \operatorname{Tr}[(\Sigma^{\setminus i})^2]+2\ell^2+2\ell.
\end{align*}
The upper bound is $\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$-measurable and independent of $U$, so we average over $U$ and use $\ell\leq1$ to obtain
\[
 e_i = \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[r_i^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i}\right]}\leq C\ell^2\operatorname{Tr}[(\Sigma^{\setminus i})^2]+2\ell^2+2\ell
 \leq C\ell^2\bigl(1+\operatorname{Tr}[(\Sigma^{\setminus i})^2]\bigr)+4\ell.
\]
By \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning}, the cavity system has dimension $M$, inverse temperature $\sqrt{M\ell}\leq\beta_*$, and time $t$.
Taking $L^p$ norms, applying \Cref{cor:tap-covariance}-\textup{(ii)} to this system, and using $\ell\leq\beta_*^2/n$, we obtain, for every $p\geq1$,
\[
 \|e_i\|_{L^p}
 \le C\ell^2(1+np)+4\ell
 \le\frac{C\cdot p}{n}.
\]

For fixed $p\geq2$, we average the conditional $r_{\mathrm{row}}$ estimate above and apply \Cref{cor:tap-covariance}-\textup{(i)} to the same cavity system; the factor $\sqrt p$ is absorbed into $C_p$.
We obtain
\[
 \left\Vertr_{\mathrm{row}}\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq C_p\ell\sqrt n
 \leq C_pn^{-1/2}.
\]
Applying Minkowski to
\cref{eq:tap-complete-row-identity} and using the pointwise bounds
$|x^3|\leq1$ and $|\zeta_i x|\leq|\zeta_i|$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \|r_i\|_{L^p}
 &\leq\|r_{\mathrm{row}}\|_{L^p}
       +\ell\|x^3\|_{L^p}+\|\zeta_i x\|_{L^p}\\
 &\leq\|r_{\mathrm{row}}\|_{L^p}+\ell
       +\sqrt{2\ell}\,\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|G|^p\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,1)}\left[|G|^p\right]}^{1/p}\\
 &\leq C_p\ell\sqrt n+\ell+C_p\sqrt\ell
 \leq C_pn^{-1/2},
\end{align*}
where the last step uses $\ell\leq\beta_*^2/n$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:tap-energy-mgf}
Under the assumptions of \Cref{lem:tap-complete-row}, there are positive constants $C,\kappa$, depending only on $\beta_*$, such that
\begin{equation*}
 \|\mathcal E\|_{L^p}\le C\cdot p\quad(p\ge1),\qquad
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\kappa\mathcal E)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\exp(\kappa\mathcal E)\right]}\le 2.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
We use the moment bound for each $e_i$ in
\Cref{lem:tap-complete-row}-\textup{(ii)} and Minkowski's inequality to obtain
\[
 \|\mathcal E\|_{L^p}
 =\left\|\sum_i e_i\right\|_{L^p}
 \le\sum_i\|e_i\|_{L^p}\le C\cdot p.
\]
We choose $0<\kappa\le(2C\mathrm e)^{-1}$ to convert the linear moment bound into an exponential moment.
Since $\mathcal E\ge0$, its exponential series has nonnegative terms, so we apply Tonelli's theorem and $p!\ge(p/\mathrm e)^p$ to obtain
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\kappa\mathcal E)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\exp(\kappa\mathcal E)\right]}
 =1+\sum_{p=1}^\infty
        \frac{\kappa^p\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal E^p\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[\mathcal E^p\right]}}{p!}
 \le 1+\sum_{p=1}^\infty\frac{(\kappa C\cdot p)^p}{p!}
 \le 1+\sum_{p=1}^\infty(\kappa C\mathrm e)^p\le 2.
\]
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-parent-child}
Under the planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model}, set $J=\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}$.
If $n\geq2$ and $n\ell\leq\beta_*^2$, let $\mathbf r$ and
$\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$ be the residual before and after
deleting coordinate $i$.  Each $\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$ is
$\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$-measurable.  For every fixed integer $p\geq1$,
there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p,\beta_*,T$, such that,
for every $t\in[0,T]$,
\begin{equation*}
 \max_i\bigl\|\,\|\mathbf r\|_2-\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\,\bigr\|_{L^{4p}}\le C_pn^{-1/4}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We fix $H>0$.
At $\beta=0$, the posterior distribution factors over coordinates, so $m(A,Y_t)=\tanh(Y_t)$ and both TAP residuals vanish:
\[
 \mathbf r_t^{(q)}=\mathbf r_t^{(Q)}
 =\operatorname{arctanh}(m(A,Y_t))-Y_t=0.
\]
We now fix $0<\beta\leq\beta_*$ and $t\in(0,T]$, and take $\mathbf r=\mathbf r_t^{(Q)}$.
We write $\mathbb P=\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}$ for the outer law in this proof.
By \Cref{cor:tap-energy-mgf}, we have $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\kappa\mathcal E)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[\exp(\kappa\mathcal E)\right]}\le 2$ for some $\kappa>0$ depending only on $\beta_*$.
We apply \Cref{lem:tap-complete-row}-\textup{(iii)} with $p=4$ to obtain $\sum_i\|r_i\|_{L^4}^2\le C_4^2$.
By \Cref{lem:tap-parent-child}, for every fixed integer $p\geq1$, the $L^{4p}$ norm of $\|\mathbf r\|_2-\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2$ is at most $C_pn^{-1/4}$ for every $i$, with $C_p$ depending only on $p,\beta_*,T$.
Here $\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$ and $e_i$ use the same sigma-field $\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$.
We first prove the tail bound for $\mathbf r_t^{(Q)}$ with failure probability at most $n^{-(H+1)}$; the extra power of $n^{-1}$ leaves room for the final union bound with the overlap error.

\emph{Step 1: transfer the tail conditionally.}
We set $\eta_n=n^{-1/8}$ and define the events
\[
 E_i^{\mathrm{del}}=\{\bigl|\,\|\mathbf r\|_2-\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\,\bigr|>\eta_n\}.
\]
Outside $E_i^{\mathrm{del}}$, the two norms differ by at most $\eta_n$.
We set $\varepsilon_n\triangleq\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(r_i^2+e_i)\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[(r_i^2+e_i)\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}$.
For every $s>0$, we obtain
\begin{align}
 s^2\mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s)
 \le&~\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^2\1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[r_i^2\1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s\}}\right]}\notag\\
 \le&~\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^2
            \1_{\{\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\ge s-\eta_n\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[r_i^2
            \1_{\{\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\ge s-\eta_n\}}\right]}
                  +\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^2\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[r_i^2\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}\notag\\
 =&~\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[e_i
            \1_{\{\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\ge s-\eta_n\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[e_i
            \1_{\{\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\ge s-\eta_n\}}\right]}
                  +\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^2\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[r_i^2\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}\notag\\
 \le&~\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[e_i\1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[e_i\1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n\}}\right]}
       +\sum_i\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(r_i^2+e_i)\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[(r_i^2+e_i)\1_{E_i^{\mathrm{del}}}\right]}\notag\\
 =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal E\,
            \1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[\mathcal E\,
            \1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n\}}\right]}+\varepsilon_n.
 \label{eq:tap-tail-transfer}
\end{align}
For the first equality in \cref{eq:tap-tail-transfer}, $\{\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\ge s-\eta_n\}\in\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$ and $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[r_i^2\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i}\right]}=e_i$; thus conditioning leaves the indicator unchanged and replaces $r_i^2$ by $e_i$.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2: control the deletion error.}
By \Cref{lem:tap-parent-child} and Markov's inequality, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \max_i\mathbb P(E_i^{\mathrm{del}})\leq\eta_n^{-4p}C_p^{4p}n^{-p}=C_p'n^{-p/2}.
\end{equation*}
We condition on $\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$ and apply Jensen's inequality to obtain $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[e_i^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[e_i^2\right]}\leq\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[r_i^4\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[r_i^4\right]}$, so $\|e_i\|_{L^2}\leq\|r_i\|_{L^4}^2$.
We apply Cauchy--Schwarz and the fourth-moment bound above to obtain
\begin{align*}
 \varepsilon_n \le&~\sum_i\bigl(\|r_i\|_{L^4}^2+\|e_i\|_{L^2}\bigr)
             \mathbb P(E_i^{\mathrm{del}})^{1/2}\\
 \le&~2\left(\sum_i\|r_i\|_{L^4}^2\right)
             \max_i\mathbb P(E_i^{\mathrm{del}})^{1/2}\\
 \le&~2C_4^2\max_i\mathbb P(E_i^{\mathrm{del}})^{1/2}
 \le C_p''n^{-p/4}.
\end{align*}
We choose a fixed integer $p>4(H+5)$ before choosing the lower bound on $n$.
Then $\varepsilon_n\le n^{-(H+4)}$ for all sufficiently large $n$.

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3: truncate and iterate the threshold.}
By \Cref{cor:tap-energy-mgf} and Markov's inequality, we have $\mathbb P(\mathcal E>x)\le 2\exp(-\kappa x)$ for every $x\geq0$.
We integrate this tail bound to obtain
\begin{align}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal E\,\1_{\{\mathcal E>a\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[\mathcal E\,\1_{\{\mathcal E>a\}}\right]}
 =&~a\mathbb P(\mathcal E>a)
       +\int_a^\infty\mathbb P(\mathcal E>x)\,\-d x \le 2(a+\kappa^{-1})\exp(-\kappa a).
 \label{eq:tap-energy-cutoff}
\end{align}
We take $a=(H+5)\kappa^{-1}\log n$.
The last expression is at most $n^{-(H+4)}$ for large $n$.
We split $\mathcal E$ at $a$ to bound the expectation in \cref{eq:tap-tail-transfer} by
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal E\1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[\mathcal E\1_{\{\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n\}}\right]}
 \le&~ a\mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n)
       +\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal E\1_{\{\mathcal E>a\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[\mathcal E\1_{\{\mathcal E>a\}}\right]}.
\end{align*}
We bound $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal E\1_{\{\mathcal E>a\}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[\mathcal E\1_{\{\mathcal E>a\}}\right]}$ by
\cref{eq:tap-energy-cutoff} and substitute this estimate and
$\varepsilon_n\le n^{-(H+4)}$ into \cref{eq:tap-tail-transfer} to obtain
\begin{equation}
 s^2\mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s)
 \le a\mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s-2\eta_n)+2n^{-(H+4)}.
 \label{eq:tap-threshold-recursion}
\end{equation}

To iterate \cref{eq:tap-threshold-recursion}, we set
\[
 s_0=\sqrt{2a},\qquad s_j=s_0+2j\eta_n,\qquad
 L=\lceil(H+3)\log_2n\rceil.
\]
For $j\ge1$, we divide \cref{eq:tap-threshold-recursion} by $s_j^2\ge2a$
and use $s_j-2\eta_n=s_{j-1}$ to obtain
\[
 \mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s_j)
 \le\tfrac12\mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s_{j-1})+n^{-(H+4)}/a.
\]
Starting from $\mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\geq s_0)\leq1$, we iterate $L$ times
and sum the geometric error terms to obtain
\[
 \mathbb P(\left\Vert\mathbf r\right\Vert_2\ge s_L)
 \le2^{-L}+2n^{-(H+4)}/a\le n^{-(H+1)}
\]
for large $n$.  Since $L\eta_n=O_H(n^{-1/8}\log n)=o(1)$,
$s_L^2\le4a$ eventually, so
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb P\left\{\left\Vert\mathbf r_t^{(Q)}\right\Vert_2^2>4(H+5)\kappa^{-1}\log n\right\}\le n^{-(H+1)}.
\end{equation*}

\smallskip
\noindent\emph{Step 4: restore the prescribed overlap.}
We write $Q=Q_n(m_t)=\langle R_{12}\rangle$.
{We replace this empirical overlap $Q=\|m_t\|_2^2/n$ by the deterministic prescribed overlap $q_\beta(t)$ from \cref{eq:compact-rs-fixed-point}.}
The identity $\mathbf r_t^{(q)}-\mathbf r_t^{(Q)}=\beta^2(Q-q_\beta(t))m_t$ and $\|m_t\|_2^2\le n$ yield
\begin{equation}
 \left\Vert\mathbf r_t^{(q)}\right\Vert_2^2
    \le 2\left\Vert\mathbf r_t^{(Q)}\right\Vert_2^2
          +2\beta^4n(Q-q_\beta(t))^2.
 \label{eq:tap-restore-q}
\end{equation}
We apply the posterior-mean conclusion of \Cref{lem:tap-overlap-mgf} at dimension $n$, inverse temperature $\beta$, and time $t$ to obtain
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\tau_0n(Q-q_\beta(t))^2)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P}\left[\exp(\tau_0n(Q-q_\beta(t))^2)\right]}\le K_0.
\]
By Markov's inequality applied to this exponential, we have
\[
 \mathbb P\left\{n(Q-q_\beta(t))^2>
               \frac{H+2}{\tau_0}\log n\right\}
 \le K_0n^{-(H+2)}\le n^{-(H+1)}
\]
for large $n$.
We combine the two tail bounds with \cref{eq:tap-restore-q} and choose $C_H$ to absorb the two logarithmic thresholds.
The union bound is $2n^{-(H+1)}\le n^{-H}$ for $n\ge2$.

The constants in the overlap and conditional second-moment bounds depend only on $\beta_*$, not on $\beta\in[0,\beta_*]$ or $t\geq0$.
Once $H$ is fixed, all moment orders used to bound the residual coordinates and compare the cavity, pinned, and full residuals depend only on $H$.
The inverse-marginal estimates used in the deletion comparison are evaluated only at $t$ and $t+\ell\leq T+1$, while deleting coordinates keeps $\ell=\beta^2/n$ unchanged.
Thus the remaining stability constants depend only on $\beta_*$ and $T$.
We can therefore choose $C_H,n_H$ independently of $\beta,t$ and take both suprema in the theorem.
At $t=0$, we have $m_t=0$ by spin-flip symmetry, so the residual vanishes.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:amp-tap-geometry}
Fix $0<\beta<1$, $t>0$, and an integer $K\geq0$.
Let $A^{\mathrm{full}}$ be a real symmetric matrix.
{Let $A$ be its zero-diagonal part.}
{We use the depth-$K$ AMP output $\widehat m_K(y)$, computed with matrix $A^{\mathrm{full}}$, external field $y$, and inverse temperature $\beta$, as the center of the balls below.}
{We use the following conditions on $(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For $\varepsilon>0$, \emph{$\varepsilon$-small TAP residual} means
\begin{equation*}
 \left\VertR_{\mathrm{TAP}}\left(\widehat m_K(y);A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t)\right)\right\Vert_2
 \leq\varepsilon\sqrt n.
\end{equation*}
\item For $r,\kappa>0$, \emph{$(r,\kappa)$-local strong convexity}
means that, for every $u\in B(\widehat m_K(y),r\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$,
\begin{equation*}
 \nabla_u^2F_{\mathrm{TAP}}\left(u;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t)\right)
 \succeq\kappa I_n.
\end{equation*}
\item For $r>0$, \emph{$r$-posterior-mean proximity} means
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vertm(A,y)-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2\leq r\sqrt n.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:amp-good-events}
{Fix $0<\beta_0<\beta_1<1$ and a constant $T>0$.}
There exist $t_0\in(0,T]$, $\kappa_0>0$, a finite collection of
closed rectangles $I_1,\ldots,I_{\ell_{\max}}$ covering
$[\beta_0,\beta_1]\times[t_0,T]$, and constants
$K_\ell\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0}$ and $r_\ell,\kappa_\ell>0$.
For every $H>0$, there are $C_H,n_H$ such that, for all $n\geq n_H$,
the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For every $\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]$, with
$\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}$-probability at least
$1-C_Hn^{-H}$, we have $\left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq4$
and
\begin{equation*}
 I_n-\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}+\beta^2(1-q_\beta(t))I_n
 \succeq4\kappa_0I_n
 \qquad\text{for every }t\in[0,t_0].
\end{equation*}
\item For every $\ell$ and every
$(\beta,t)\in I_\ell\cap([\beta_0,\beta_1]\times[t_0,T])$, with
$\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}$-probability at least
$1-C_Hn^{-H}$, the pair $(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t)$ satisfies
$(\kappa_\ell r_\ell/8)$-small TAP residual,
$(4r_\ell,4\kappa_\ell)$-local strong convexity, and
$r_\ell$-posterior-mean proximity at inverse temperature $\beta$,
time $t$, and AMP depth $K_\ell$.
On the same event,
$\left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq4$ and
$\left\VertY_t\right\Vert_\infty\leq n^2$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:amp-spatial-lipschitz}
For every fixed integer $K\geq0$, there is a constant $L_K$ such that,
for every $n$, $0<\beta<1$, and symmetric matrix $A^{\mathrm{full}}$
with $\left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq4$,
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\widehat m_K(y)-\widehat m_K(z)\right\Vert_2
 \leq L_K\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2
 \qquad(y,z\in\mathbb{R}^n).
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Fix $y,z\in\mathbb{R}^n$.  We compare the two AMP recursions and prove the
Lipschitz property by induction on their depth.
Recall that $\widehat m_k(y)=\tanh(z_k(y))$, with
$\widehat m_{-1}(y)=z_0(y)=0$ and
\begin{equation*}
 z_{k+1}(y)=\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}\widehat m_k(y)+y
 -\beta^2\left(1-\frac{\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2^2}{n}\right)
   \widehat m_{k-1}(y).
\end{equation*}
The recursion at $z$ has the same matrix and inverse temperature.
Subtracting the two updates, and adding and subtracting the Onsager
coefficient at $y$ times $\widehat m_{k-1}(z)$, gives
\begin{align*}
 z_{k+1}(y)-z_{k+1}(z)
 =&~\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}(\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z))+(y-z)
 ~-\beta^2\left(1-\frac{\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2^2}{n}\right)
       (\widehat m_{k-1}(y)-\widehat m_{k-1}(z))\\
 &~+\frac{\beta^2}{n}
       (\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2^2-\left\Vert\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2^2)
       \widehat m_{k-1}(z).
\end{align*}
We bound the three terms other than $y-z$ separately.
The matrix term satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}(\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z))\right\Vert_2
 \leq4\beta\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2.
\end{equation*}
Since every coordinate of $\widehat m_k(y)$ lies in $[-1,1]$,
$0\leq1-\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2^2/n\leq1$.  Hence
\begin{align*}
 \left\|\beta^2\left(1-\frac{\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2^2}{n}\right)
       (\widehat m_{k-1}(y)-\widehat m_{k-1}(z))\right\|_2
 \leq \beta^2\left\Vert\widehat m_{k-1}(y)-\widehat m_{k-1}(z)\right\Vert_2.
\end{align*}
For the last term, the difference of squared norms satisfies
\begin{align*}
 \left\vert\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2^2-\left\Vert\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2^2\right\vert
 =&~\left\vert\langle \widehat m_k(y)+\widehat m_k(z),\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\rangle\right\vert\\
 \leq&~(\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2+\left\Vert\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2)
          \left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2\\
 \leq&~2\sqrt n\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2.
\end{align*}
Together with $\left\Vert\widehat m_{k-1}(z)\right\Vert_2\leq\sqrt n$, this gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\|\frac{\beta^2}{n}
       (\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)\right\Vert_2^2-\left\Vert\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2^2)
       \widehat m_{k-1}(z)\right\|_2
 \leq \frac{\beta^2}{n}\,2\sqrt n
       \left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2\,\sqrt n
 =2\beta^2\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2.
\end{align*}
Taking norms in the update difference and using these three bounds,
we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vertz_{k+1}(y)-z_{k+1}(z)\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~(4\beta+2\beta^2)\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2
       +\beta^2\left\Vert\widehat m_{k-1}(y)-\widehat m_{k-1}(z)\right\Vert_2
       +\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2\\
 \leq&~6\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2
       +\left\Vert\widehat m_{k-1}(y)-\widehat m_{k-1}(z)\right\Vert_2
       +\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2.
\end{align*}
The last inequality uses $0<\beta<1$.

Set $L_{-1}=L_0=0$ and $L_{k+1}=6L_k+L_{k-1}+1$.
We claim that, for every $k\geq-1$,
$\left\Vert\widehat m_k(y)-\widehat m_k(z)\right\Vert_2\leq L_k\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2$.
For $k=-1,0$, both AMP outputs are zero, so the claim holds.
Suppose it holds at depths $k-1$ and $k$.
Since $\tanh$ is one-Lipschitz, the preceding recursion implies
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\widehat m_{k+1}(y)-\widehat m_{k+1}(z)\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\left\Vertz_{k+1}(y)-z_{k+1}(z)\right\Vert_2
 \leq 6L_k\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2+L_{k-1}\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2+\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2\\
 =&~L_{k+1}\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2.
\end{align*}
This proves the claim at depth $k+1$.  Taking $k=K$ gives the
asserted bound, with $L_K$ depending only on $K$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-stationary-point}
Fix $0<\beta<1$, $t>0$, an integer $K\geq0$, a symmetric matrix
$A^{\mathrm{full}}$, and $r,\kappa>0$.  Write
$F_y(u)=F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(u;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t))$.
We have that
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item Fix $y\in\mathbb{R}^n$.  Suppose $(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$ satisfies
$(\kappa r/8)$-small TAP residual and
$(4r,4\kappa)$-local strong convexity
at time $t$ and AMP depth $K$, as defined in
\Cref{def:amp-tap-geometry}.
Then $F_y$ has a unique stationary point $m^\star$ in
$B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$,
with $\left\Vertm^\star-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2\leq r\sqrt n/32$.
For every $u\in B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$,
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vertu-m^\star\right\Vert_2\leq(4\kappa)^{-1}\left\Vert\nabla F_y(u)\right\Vert_2.
\end{equation*}
\item Let $D\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ and suppose the AMP center
$y\mapsto\widehat m_K(y)$ is $L$-Lipschitz on $D$.
Suppose the two properties in part~\textup{(i)} hold for every $y\in D$.
Write $m^\star(y)$ for its unique stationary point.
Then, for every $y,z\in D$,
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vertm^\star(y)-m^\star(z)\right\Vert_2
 \leq\max((4\kappa)^{-1},\,2L/(3r))\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
For part~\textup{(i)}, $F_y$ extends continuously to $[-1,1]^n$,
using $0\log0=0$ in $h_{\mathrm{bin}}$.
It attains its minimum on the nonempty compact set
$\overline B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)\cap[-1,1]^n$.
Let $u$ be a minimizer.  {We first show that $u$ lies in the interior of the cube $[-1,1]^n$.}
To the contrary, suppose $u_i=1$ for some $i$.  Decreasing only this coordinate by
$\varepsilon>0$ keeps the point in the cube, and
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vertu-\varepsilon e_i-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2^2-\left\Vertu-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2^2
 =-2\varepsilon(1-\widehat m_{K,i}(y))+\varepsilon^2<0{.}
\end{equation*}
Here $0<\varepsilon<\min(2,2(1-\widehat m_{K,i}(y)))$, so the perturbed point is feasible.
Keeping all other coordinates fixed, the derivative with respect
to the $i$th coordinate, at its value $s\in(-1,1)$, is
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{arctanh}(s)-y_i
 -\beta\sum_{j\ne i}A^{\mathrm{full}}_{ij}u_j
 +(\beta^2(1-q_\beta(t))-\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}_{ii})s.
\end{equation*}
This tends to $+\infty$ as $s\uparrow1$, so the one-variable
function is strictly increasing sufficiently near $1$.
By continuity at $1$, replacing $u_i=1$ by $1-\varepsilon$ strictly
decreases $F_y$ for sufficiently small $\varepsilon$, a contradiction.
If $u_i=-1$, increasing it by $\varepsilon$ changes the squared distance
to $\widehat m_K(y)$ by $-2\varepsilon(1+\widehat m_{K,i}(y))+\varepsilon^2<0$ for small $\varepsilon$.
