The same derivative tends to $-\infty$ as $s\downarrow-1$, so this
feasible move also strictly decreases $F_y$.
Hence no coordinate of a minimizer is on the cube boundary.

Next, we show that $u$ is not on the boundary of $B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)$.
Suppose $\left\Vertu-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2=4r\sqrt n$.
For $0<s<1$, the segment from $\widehat m_K(y)$ to $\widehat m_K(y)+s(u-\widehat m_K(y))$ lies in
$B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$, so the Hessian bound gives
\begin{equation*}
 F_y(\widehat m_K(y)+s(u-\widehat m_K(y)))-F_y(\widehat m_K(y))
 \geq s\langle \nabla F_y(\widehat m_K(y)),u-\widehat m_K(y)\rangle
       +2\kappa s^2\left\Vertu-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2^2.
\end{equation*}
Letting $s\uparrow1$ and using continuity, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 F_y(u)-F_y(\widehat m_K(y))
 \geq&~\langle \nabla F_y(\widehat m_K(y)),u-\widehat m_K(y)\rangle+2\kappa\left\Vertu-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2^2\\
 \geq&~-\frac{\kappa r\sqrt n}{8}(4r\sqrt n)
          +2\kappa(4r\sqrt n)^2>0.
\end{align*}
Since $\widehat m_K(y)$ is feasible, this contradicts the assumption that $u$ is the minimizer.
Thus a minimizer lies in $B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$.
{We denote it by $m^\star$; then} $\nabla F_y(m^\star)=0$.

For every $u\in B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$, integrating the Hessian bound
along the segment from $m^\star$ to $u$ and using the stationarity of $m^\star$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 4\kappa\left\Vertu-m^\star\right\Vert_2^2
 \leq\langle \nabla F_y(u)-\nabla F_y(m^\star),u-m^\star\rangle
 =\langle \nabla F_y(u),u-m^\star\rangle
 \leq\left\Vert\nabla F_y(u)\right\Vert_2\left\Vertu-m^\star\right\Vert_2.
\end{equation*}
Taking $u=\widehat m_K(y)$ gives
$\left\Vertm^\star-\widehat m_K(y)\right\Vert_2\leq(4\kappa)^{-1}\left\Vert\nabla F_y(\widehat m_K(y))\right\Vert_2
\leq r\sqrt n/32$.

For part~\textup{(ii)}, fix $y,z\in D$.
If $\left\Vert\widehat m_K(y)-\widehat m_K(z)\right\Vert_2\leq3r\sqrt n$,
both stationary points and their connecting segment lie in
$B(\widehat m_K(y),4r\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$.
{Since $\nabla F_y(m^\star(y))=0$ and $\nabla F_y(m^\star(z))=z-y$, the $4\kappa$-strong monotonicity of $\nabla F_y$ on this ball gives
\begin{equation*}
4\kappa\left\Vertm^\star(y)-m^\star(z)\right\Vert_2^2
\leq\langle y-z,m^\star(y)-m^\star(z)\rangle
\leq\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2\left\Vertm^\star(y)-m^\star(z)\right\Vert_2.
\end{equation*}
Thus $4\kappa\left\Vertm^\star(y)-m^\star(z)\right\Vert_2\leq\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2$.}

Otherwise, $\left\Vert\widehat m_K(y)-\widehat m_K(z)\right\Vert_2>3r\sqrt n$.
Since $y\mapsto\widehat m_K(y)$ is $L$-Lipschitz,
we have
\begin{equation*}
3r\sqrt n<\left\Vert\widehat m_K(y)-\widehat m_K(z)\right\Vert_2\leq L\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2.
\end{equation*}
Both stationary points belong to $(-1,1)^n$, so each coordinate
of their difference has absolute value at most two.  Hence
$\left\Vertm^\star(y)-m^\star(z)\right\Vert_2 \leq2\sqrt n \leq\frac{2L}{3r}\left\Verty-z\right\Vert_2$.
Then part~(ii) is proved by combining the above two cases.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:local-root-measurable-lipschitz}
There is a constant $L$, depending only on $(\beta_0,\beta_1,T)$, with the following properties.
The set $\{(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y):t\in[0,T], (A^{\mathrm{full}},y)\in\mathcal D_t\}$ is Borel, and the local stationary point $(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y)\mapsto a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$ is Borel on this domain.
For every fixed $A^{\mathrm{full}},t$ and every two certified fields $y,z$,
\begin{equation*}
 \|a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)-a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},z)\|_2
 \le L\|y-z\|_2.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We first prove that the set in the statement is Borel.
For $t\le t_0$, recall that $(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)\in\mathcal D_t$ means
\begin{equation*}
 \|A^{\mathrm{full}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le4,\qquad
 I_n-\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}+\beta^2(1-q_\beta(t))I_n\succeq4\kappa_0I_n.
\end{equation*}
Since $q_\beta(t)$, the operator norm, and the least eigenvalue are continuous, these inequalities together with $t\le t_0$ define a Borel set of triples $(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y)$.


For large times $t>t_0$, the least covering index $\ell_t$ gives a finite Borel partition, and on a part with index $\ell$ the depth-$K_\ell$ AMP center and its TAP residual are continuous in $(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y)$.
{The local strong-convexity condition is the only condition that must be checked at every point of a ball.
For each fixed $v\in\mathbb Q^n\cap(-1,1)^n$, this condition requires the following implication:}
\begin{equation*}
 \|v-\widehat m_\ell(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)\|_2<4r_\ell\sqrt n
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \nabla_v^2F_{\mathrm{TAP}}(v;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t))
 \succeq4\kappa_\ell I_n.
\end{equation*}
For fixed $v$, this implication is a Borel condition on $(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y)$.
The rational points are dense in the AMP-centered ball intersected with $(-1,1)^n$, and the Hessian is continuous in $v$, so the countable intersection of these conditions is exactly the required local strong convexity.
The matrix and field cutoffs $\|A^{\mathrm{full}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le4, \|y\|_\infty\le n^2$ and the AMP residual condition are Borel as well.
So the set is Borel.

We next show that $(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y)\mapsto a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$ is Borel on the certified domain.
It suffices to prove continuity relative to each part of the finite Borel partition above.
Fix a large-time part with index $\ell$ and $(A_k^{\mathrm{full}},t_k,y_k)\to(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y)$ in this part.
We set $c_k=\widehat m_\ell(A_k^{\mathrm{full}},y_k)$, $c=\widehat m_\ell(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$, $u_k=a_{t_k}(A_k^{\mathrm{full}},y_k)$, and $u=a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$.
By \Cref{lem:tap-stationary-point}-\textup{(i)}, we have $\|u_k-c_k\|_2,\|u-c\|_2\le r_\ell\sqrt n/32$, while continuity of the AMP center gives $c_k\to c$.
{For all sufficiently large $k$, $\|u-c_k\|_2\le\|u-c\|_2+\|c-c_k\|_2<4r_\ell\sqrt n$, so $u\in B(c_k,4r_\ell\sqrt n)\cap(-1,1)^n$.}

{We apply the residual estimate in \Cref{lem:tap-stationary-point}-\textup{(i)} to $u$ in this ball.}
Since $R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(u;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t))=0$ and $\|u\|_2\le\sqrt n$, subtracting the two residuals gives
\begin{align*}
 \|u_k-u\|_2
 \leq&~(4\kappa_\ell)^{-1}
   \|R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(u;A_k^{\mathrm{full}},y_k,q_\beta(t_k))\|_2\\
 \leq&~(4\kappa_\ell)^{-1}
   \bigl(\|y_k-y\|_2+\beta\sqrt n\|A_k^{\mathrm{full}}-A^{\mathrm{full}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
          +\beta^2\sqrt n|q_\beta(t_k)-q_\beta(t)|\bigr)\longrightarrow0.
\end{align*}
{At small times, the TAP free energy is continuous on the compact cube $[-1,1]^n$ and therefore attains a minimum.}
Its derivative in coordinate $i$ tends to $+\infty$ at $u_i=1$ and to $-\infty$ at $u_i=-1$, while the other terms remain bounded, so every minimizer lies in $(-1,1)^n$.
At this interior minimizer, $R_{\mathrm{TAP}}=\nabla F_{\mathrm{TAP}}=0$; the matrix condition defining $\mathcal D_t$ makes this zero unique, and we denote it by $a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$.
For certified small-time triples $(A_k^{\mathrm{full}},t_k,y_k)\to(A^{\mathrm{full}},t,y)$ with a certified limit, let $u_k=a_{t_k}(A_k^{\mathrm{full}},y_k)$ and $u=a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$.
{We integrate the Hessian lower bound $4\kappa_0I_n$ along the segment from $u_k$ to $u$ and use $R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(u_k;A_k^{\mathrm{full}},y_k,q_\beta(t_k))=0$ to obtain}
\begin{equation*}
 \|u_k-u\|_2
 \le(4\kappa_0)^{-1}\|R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(u;A_k^{\mathrm{full}},y_k,q_\beta(t_k))\|_2
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{equation*}
{The convergence follows from continuity of the residual at the limiting stationary point $u$.}
Hence the local stationary-point function is continuous relative to each part of the finite Borel partition and is therefore Borel on the certified domain.

We now prove the Lipschitz property.
Fix $A^{\mathrm{full}},t$ and two certified fields $y,z$, and set $u=a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},y)$ and $v=a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},z)$.

At small times, stationarity gives $R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(u;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t))=0$ and $R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(v;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t))=z-y$.
We apply the $4\kappa_0$-strong monotonicity of the first residual to these points and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 4\kappa_0\|u-v\|_2^2
 \le\langle y-z,u-v\rangle
 \le\|y-z\|_2\|u-v\|_2.
\end{equation*}
Thus $\|u-v\|_2\le(4\kappa_0)^{-1}\|y-z\|_2$.

At large times, we set $D=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^n:(A^{\mathrm{full}},w)\in\mathcal D_t\}$ and take $\ell=\ell_t$.
By \Cref{lem:amp-spatial-lipschitz}, the depth-$K_\ell$ AMP center is $L_{K_\ell}$-Lipschitz on $D$, and every $w\in D$ satisfies the hypotheses of \Cref{lem:tap-stationary-point}-\textup{(i)} with $r=r_\ell$ and $\kappa=\kappa_\ell$.
We apply \Cref{lem:tap-stationary-point}-\textup{(ii)} and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \|u-v\|_2
 \le\max\left((4\kappa_\ell)^{-1},\frac{2L_{K_\ell}}{3r_\ell}\right)\|y-z\|_2.
\end{equation*}
Combined, the lemma follows by setting  $L=\max\left\{(4\kappa_0)^{-1},\max_\ell\max\left((4\kappa_\ell)^{-1},\frac{2L_{K_\ell}}{3r_\ell}\right)\right\}$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:local-root-lipschitz-with-costs}
For every $A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\mathrm{sym},0}$, $s\in[0,T]$, and $y_1,y_2\in\mathbb{R}^n$, we have
\begin{align*}
 \|m(A,y_1)-m(A,y_2)\|_2
 \le{}&L\|y_1-y_2\|_2
 +\sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,s;y_1)}
 +\sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,s;y_2)}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
We fix $d\in\mathbb{R}^n$ and set $A^{\mathrm{full}}=A+\operatorname{diag}(d)$.
If $(A^{\mathrm{full}},y_1)$ and $(A^{\mathrm{full}},y_2)$ belong to $\mathcal D_s$, then by \Cref{lem:local-root-measurable-lipschitz} we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \|m(A,y_1)-m(A,y_2)\|_2
 \leq&~\|m(A,y_1)-a_s(A^{\mathrm{full}},y_1)\|_2
       +\|a_s(A^{\mathrm{full}},y_1)-a_s(A^{\mathrm{full}},y_2)\|_2\\
 &+\|a_s(A^{\mathrm{full}},y_2)-m(A,y_2)\|_2\\
 \leq&~\sqrt{\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,s}(y_1)}
       +L\|y_1-y_2\|_2
       +\sqrt{\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,s}(y_2)}.
\end{align*}
If either field $y_i$ is uncertified, its cost is $4n$, and its square root $2\sqrt n$ bounds the left side because both posterior means lie in $[-1,1]^n$.
Thus the inequality holds for every $d$.
Its left side and $L\|y_1-y_2\|_2$ do not depend on $d$.
We take expectation over $d$ and apply Cauchy--Schwarz to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{d\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{diag}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,s}(y_i)}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{d\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{diag}}}\left[\sqrt{\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,s}(y_i)}\right]}
 \le\sqrt{\ifthenelse{\isempty{d\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{diag}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,s}(y_i)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{d\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{diag}}}\left[\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,s}(y_i)\right]}}
 =\sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,s;y_i)}.\qedhere
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:scalar-reference-path-error}
For every $R>0$, there are constants $C_R,n_R>0$ such that, for every $n\ge n_R$,
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \{U_{n,\beta,A}(Y)>C_R\log n\}\le n^{-R}.
\end{equation*}
Moreover,
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \{E_{n,\beta,A}(Y)>C_R\log n\}\le n^{-R}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
We fix $R>0$ and set $H=2R+8$.
We first prove a fixed-time bound for the diagonal cost.
By \Cref{lem:amp-good-events,thm:polynomial-tap-residual}, after increasing a constant $K_H\ge1$, for every $\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]$ and $t\in[0,T]$, outside an event of $\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}$-probability at most $K_Hn^{-H}$, the following properties hold.
If $t\le t_0$, then $(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t)\in\mathcal D_t$ and
\begin{equation*}
 \|R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(m(A,Y_t);A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t,q_\beta(t))\|_2^2\le K_H\log n.
\end{equation*}
If $t>t_0$, then $(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t)\in\mathcal D_t$, the posterior-mean proximity condition holds at the index $\ell_t$, and the same residual bound holds.
For $t\le t_0$, strong convexity on the whole cube therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
 \|m(A,Y_t)-a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t)\|_2^2
 \le (4\kappa_0)^{-2}\|R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(m(A,Y_t);A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t,q_\beta(t))\|_2^2.
\end{equation*}
For $t>t_0$, posterior-mean proximity puts $m(A,Y_t)$ in the strongly convex ball from \Cref{lem:tap-stationary-point}-\textup{(i)}, and hence
\begin{equation*}
 \|m(A,Y_t)-a_t(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t)\|_2^2
 \le (4\kappa_{\ell_t})^{-2}\|R_{\mathrm{TAP}}(m(A,Y_t);A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_t,q_\beta(t))\|_2^2.
\end{equation*}
Since there are finitely many indices $\ell$, we can choose a constant $C_H$ so that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:fixed-time-raw-diagonal-cost}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
 \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}
 \left\{\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,t}(Y_t)>C_H\log n\right\}
 \le C_Hn^{-H}.
\end{equation}
We next average over the auxiliary diagonal.
For fixed $(A,Y)$ and $t\in[0,T]$, set
\begin{equation*}
 p_t(A,Y)
 =\mathbb P_{d\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{diag}}}
 \left\{\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,t}(Y_t)>C_H\log n\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Under $\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}$, the diagonal $d$ is independent of $(A,Y)$, so \cref{eq:fixed-time-raw-diagonal-cost} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{(A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[p_t(A,Y)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{(A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[p_t(A,Y)\right]}
 \le C_Hn^{-H}.
\end{equation*}
Using $0\le\mathfrak e_{\beta,A,d,t}\le4n$, we split the diagonal expectation according to the event defining $p_t(A,Y)$ and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;Y_t)
 \le C_H\log n+4n\,p_t(A,Y).
\end{equation*}
We apply Markov's inequality to $p_t(A,Y)$ and obtain, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
 \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}
 \left\{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;Y_t)>2C_H\log n\right\}
 \le4C_Hn^{-(H-1)}.
\end{equation*}
We apply the reverse probability comparison in \Cref{lem:planting-likelihood} and use $\beta\le\beta_1<1$ to obtain a constant $\widetilde C_H>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:fixed-time-averaged-diagonal-cost}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \left\{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;Y_t)>\widetilde C_H\log n\right\}
 \le \widetilde C_Hn^{-(H-1)/2}.
\end{equation}

We now integrate this fixed-time estimate.
By \Cref{lem:local-root-measurable-lipschitz} and the definition of the diagonal average, $\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}$ is jointly Borel.
Together with the continuity of $Y$, this implies that the function below is measurable.
We define
\begin{equation*}
 \Lambda_H(A,Y)
 =\int_0^T\mathbf 1_{\{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;Y_t)>\widetilde C_H\log n\}}\,\mathrm d t.
\end{equation*}
By \cref{eq:fixed-time-averaged-diagonal-cost} and Tonelli's theorem, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{(A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Lambda_H(A,Y)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{(A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}}\left[\Lambda_H(A,Y)\right]}
 =\int_0^T
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \left\{\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;Y_t)>\widetilde C_H\log n\right\}\,\mathrm d t
 \le T\widetilde C_Hn^{-(H-1)/2}.
\end{align*}
We apply Markov's inequality to $\Lambda_H(A,Y)$ and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \left\{\Lambda_H(A,Y)>\frac{\log n}{n}\right\}
 \le\frac{T\widetilde C_H}{\log n}n^{-(H-3)/2}.
\end{equation*}
On the event $\{\Lambda_H(A,Y)\le\log n/n\}$, we use $\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}\le4n$ to obtain $U_{n,\beta,A}(Y)\le T\widetilde C_H\log n+4n\Lambda_H(A,Y)\le(T\widetilde C_H+4)\log n$.
Since $H=2R+8$, after increasing $C_R$ and $n_R$ we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \{U_{n,\beta,A}(Y)>C_R\log n\}\le n^{-R}.
\end{equation*}
Finally, we apply \cref{eq:fixed-time-averaged-diagonal-cost} at $t=T$ and increase $C_R,n_R$ if necessary to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]}
 \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}
 \{E_{n,\beta,A}(Y)>C_R\log n\}\le n^{-R}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We take the cost function $\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}$ defined above and the constant $L$ from \Cref{lem:local-root-measurable-lipschitz}.
The Lipschitz property with cost functions in part~\textup{(i)} follows from \Cref{lem:local-root-lipschitz-with-costs}.
The continuous-time path-cost and terminal-cost bounds in part~\textup{(ii)} follow from \Cref{lem:scalar-reference-path-error}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion}
Fix constants $T,L,C_0,r>0$.
For each $n\ge 2$, let $\mu$ be a full-support distribution on
$\{\pm1\}^n$.
Suppose there is a jointly Borel function
$\overline{\mathfrak e}:
    [0,T]\times\mathbb R^n
    \longrightarrow [0,4n]$
such that, for every $t\in[0,T]$ and every
$y_1,y_2\in\mathbb R^n$,
\[
    \|m(y_1)-m(y_2)\|_2
    \le
    L\|y_1-y_2\|_2
    +
    \sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}(t;y_1)}
    +
    \sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}(t;y_2)}.
\]

For the localization paths defined above from $\mu$, define the
continuous-time path cost and terminal cost by
\[
    U(\omega)
    \triangleq
    \int_0^T
    \overline{\mathfrak e}
    \bigl(t;Y_t^\omega\bigr)\,\mathrm d t,
    \qquad
    E(\omega)
    \triangleq
    \overline{\mathfrak e}
    \bigl(T;Y_T^\omega\bigr).
\]
Assume that
\[
    \mathbb P_{\omega\sim\gamma}
    \left[
        U(\omega)>C_0\log n
        \ \text{or}\
        E(\omega)>C_0\log n
    \right]
    \le n^{-r}.
\]

Suppose there are numbers $a_n,\delta_n\ge0$ such that, for every
$f:\{\pm1\}^n\to\mathbb R$,
\[
    \mathbb E_{\omega\sim\gamma}
    \left[
        \operatorname{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}[f]
    \right]
    \le
    a_n\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu}\left(f,f\right)}
    +
    \delta_n\operatorname{osc}(f)^2.
\]
Then there are constants $\Gamma>0$, depending only on $T,L$, and
$C,B>0$, depending only on $T,L,C_0$, such that, for every $n\ge2$,
every $f:\{\pm1\}^n\to\mathbb R$, and every $8n^{-r}\le s\le \frac12$,
we have
\[
    \operatorname{Var}_\mu[f]
    \le
    \bigl(1+C n^B s^{-\Gamma}\bigr)
    a_n\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu}\left(f,f\right)}
    +
    \left(
        s+
        \bigl(1+C n^B s^{-\Gamma}\bigr)\delta_n
    \right)
    \operatorname{osc}(f)^2.
\]
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-path-distance}
For the {localization paths} $Y^\omega$ and path cost $U$ defined above, for every $\omega\in C_0([0,T];\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $h\in\mathbb H_T$ we have that
\begin{equation*}
 \|Y_T^{\omega+h}-Y_T^\omega\|_2
 \le\sqrt T\,\exp(LT)
 \bigl(\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}
       +\sqrt{U(\omega+h)}+\sqrt{U(\omega)}\bigr).
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
For $0\le t\le T$, we subtract the defining equations for $Y^{\omega+h}$ and $Y^\omega$ to obtain
\[
Y_t^{\omega+h}-Y_t^\omega
=
h_t
+
\int_0^t
\bigl(
m(Y_u^{\omega+h})-m(Y_u^\omega)
\bigr)\,\mathrm d u .
\]
By \cref{eq:cameron-martin-uniform-bound},
$\|h\|_\infty \le \sqrt T\,\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}$.
For each $u\in[0,T]$, we apply the Lipschitz property with cost functions assumed in \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion} at time $u$, taking $(y_1,y_2)=(Y_u^{\omega+h},Y_u^\omega)$.
We obtain
\begin{align*}
\|m(Y_u^{\omega+h})-m(Y_u^\omega)\|_2
\le{}
L\|Y_u^{\omega+h}-Y_u^\omega\|_2 
+
\sqrt{
\overline{\mathfrak e}
\bigl(u;Y_u^{\omega+h}\bigr)
}
+
\sqrt{
\overline{\mathfrak e}
\bigl(u;Y_u^\omega\bigr)
}.
\end{align*}
Thus, we have
\begin{align*}
\sup_{0\le s\le t}
\|Y_s^{\omega+h}-Y_s^\omega\|_2
\le{}&
\sqrt T\,\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}
+
L\int_0^t  \sup_{0\le s\le u}
\|Y_s^{\omega+h}-Y_s^\omega\|_2\,\mathrm d u
+
\int_0^t
\sqrt{
\overline{\mathfrak e}
\bigl(u;Y_u^{\omega+h}\bigr)
}\,\mathrm d u \\
&+
\int_0^t
\sqrt{
\overline{\mathfrak e}
\bigl(u;Y_u^\omega\bigr)
}\,\mathrm d u .
\end{align*}
By Cauchy--Schwarz and the definition of $U$, we have that 
\[
\int_0^t
\sqrt{
\overline{\mathfrak e}
\bigl(u;Y_u^{\omega+h}\bigr)
}\,\mathrm d u
\le
\sqrt{T\,U(\omega+h)},
\]
and similarly
\[
\int_0^t
\sqrt{
\overline{\mathfrak e}
\bigl(u;Y_u^\omega\bigr)
}\,\mathrm d u
\le
\sqrt{T\,U(\omega)}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\sup_{0\le s\le t}
\|Y_s^{\omega+h}-Y_s^\omega\|_2
\le
\sqrt T
\Bigl(
\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}
+\sqrt{U(\omega+h)}
+\sqrt{U(\omega)}
\Bigr)
+
L\int_0^t \sup_{0\le s\le u}
\|Y_s^{\omega+h}-Y_s^\omega\|_2\,\mathrm d u .
\]
By \Cref{lem:gronwall-path-stability}, we have that
\[
\|Y_T^{\omega+h}-Y_T^\omega\|_2
\le
\sup_{0\le s\le T}
\|Y_s^{\omega+h}-Y_s^\omega\|_2
\le
\sqrt T\mathrm{e}^{LT}
\Bigl(
\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}
+\sqrt{U(\omega+h)}
+\sqrt{U(\omega)}
\Bigr).
\]
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-posterior-comparison}
For the posterior distribution $\mu_y$ and cost function $\overline{\mathfrak e}$ defined above, for every $y,z\in\mathbb{R}^n$ and $f:\{\pm1\}^n\to\mathbb{R}$ we have that
\begin{align*}
 |\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_y}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_y}\left[f\right]}-\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_z}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_z}\left[f\right]}|^2
 \leq&~2\bigl(\exp([D(\mu_y\Vert\mu_z)
                         +D(\mu_z\Vert\mu_y)]/2)-1\bigr)
             (\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_y}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_y}\left[f\right]}+\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_z}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_z}\left[f\right]}),
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
 D(\mu_y\Vert\mu_z)+D(\mu_z\Vert\mu_y)
 \leq&~L\|y-z\|_2^2+
 \bigl(\sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}(T;y)}+\sqrt{\overline{\mathfrak e}(T;z)}\bigr)\|y-z\|_2.
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We first prove the first part of this lemma.
{We take full-support distributions $P,Q$ on the cube and set $a=\sum_x\sqrt{P(x)Q(x)}>0$, their Hellinger affinity.}
We have that
\begin{equation*}
 a(\ifthenelse{\isempty{P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[f\right]}-\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[f\right]})
 =\sum_x\sqrt{P(x)Q(x)}(f(x)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[f\right]})
  -\sum_x\sqrt{P(x)Q(x)}(f(x)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[f\right]}){.}
\end{equation*}
For the first sum, we use
$\sqrt{P(x)Q(x)}=Q(x)\sqrt{P(x)/Q(x)}$ and
$\sum_xQ(x)(f(x)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[f\right]})=0$.  Thus we may subtract
$aQ(x)(f(x)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[f\right]})$ and apply Cauchy--Schwarz:
\begin{equation*}
 \left|\sum_x\sqrt{P(x)Q(x)}(f(x)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[f\right]})\right|^2
 \le\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{Q}\left[f\right]}\sum_xQ(x)
       \left(\sqrt{P(x)/Q(x)}-a\right)^2
 =(1-a^2)\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{Q}\left[f\right]},
\end{equation*}
where the last equality follows from $\sum_xP(x)=\sum_xQ(x)=1$ and $\sum_x\sqrt{P(x)Q(x)}=a$.
Going through the calculation for the other centered sum with $P,Q$ exchanged and using $(u-v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 |\ifthenelse{\isempty{P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[f\right]}-\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[f\right]}|^2
 \le2(a^{-2}-1)(\ifthenelse{\isempty{P}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{P}\left[f\right]}+\ifthenelse{\isempty{Q}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{Q}\left[f\right]}).
\end{equation*}
Next, we provide a lower bound on $a$.
By Jensen's inequality, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 \log a=\log\ifthenelse{\isempty{P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{Q(X)/P(X)}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[\sqrt{Q(X)/P(X)}\right]}
 \ge\ifthenelse{\isempty{P}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\log\sqrt{Q(X)/P(X)}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[\log\sqrt{Q(X)/P(X)}\right]}=-D(P\Vert Q)/2,
\end{equation*}
and the same argument under $Q$ gives
$\log a\ge-D(Q\Vert P)/2$.
Taking the mean of these two lower bounds yields
$a^{-2}\le\exp([D(P\Vert Q)+D(Q\Vert P)]/2)$.
By setting $P = \mu_y$ and $Q = \mu_z$, we finish the proof of the first part.

For the second part, note that for
each $x\in\{\pm1\}^n$,
\begin{equation*}
 \log\frac{\mu_y(x)}{\mu_z(x)}
 =\langle y-z,x\rangle
  +\log\frac{\ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\langle z,X\rangle)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[\exp(\langle z,X\rangle)\right]}}
                  {\ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\langle y,X\rangle)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[\exp(\langle y,X\rangle)\right]}}.
\end{equation*}
Hence, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 D(\mu_y\Vert\mu_z)
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu_y}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\log\frac{\mu_y(X)}{\mu_z(X)}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu_y}\left[\log\frac{\mu_y(X)}{\mu_z(X)}\right]}
 =\langle y-z,m(y)\rangle
  +\log\frac{\ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\langle z,X\rangle)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[\exp(\langle z,X\rangle)\right]}}
                  {\ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(\langle y,X\rangle)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu}\left[\exp(\langle y,X\rangle)\right]}}.
\end{equation*}
Thus, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 D(\mu_y\Vert\mu_z)+D(\mu_z\Vert\mu_y)
 =\langle y-z,m(y)-m(z)\rangle.
\end{equation*}
We apply the Lipschitz property with cost functions in \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion} at time $T$ to $\|m(y)-m(z)\|_2$ and use Cauchy--Schwarz to obtain the second bound.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
We fix $f$.
By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-observation-law} and the law of total variance, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
  +\ifthenelse{\isempty{\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}.
\end{equation*}
The assumed bound on the averaged posterior variance controls the first term.
For the second term, we will prove that it is at most
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}\le Cn^Bs^{-\Gamma}\ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
 +s\operatorname{osc}(f)^2{.}
\end{equation*}
{Here $\Gamma$ depends only on $T,L$, while $C,B$ may also depend on $C_0$.}


{For $0<v<1$, we define the quantile of $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}$ under $\omega\sim\gamma$ by
\begin{equation*}
 Q_f(v)=\inf\left\{a\in\mathbb{R}:\ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\le a\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\le a\right]}\ge v\right\}.
\end{equation*}
}
For $s\le v\le1/2$, we claim that
\begin{equation*}
 [Q_f(1-v)-Q_f(v)]^2
 \le 16\cdot4^\Gamma n^Bv^{-1-\Gamma}
      \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}.
\end{equation*}
Later, we will integrate this estimate over $v\in[s,1/2]$; the remaining
interval $(0,s)$ contributes at most $s\operatorname{osc}(f)^2$.

To prove the above claim, we use Gaussian isoperimetry to choose a lower-quantile path and an upper-quantile path whose Cameron--Martin distance is small.
The lower event
$\{\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\le Q_f(v)\}$ and the upper event $\{\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\ge Q_f(1-v)\}$ each have Wiener measure at least $v$.
{We discard paths for which $U>C_0\log n$ or $E>C_0\log n$.}
{By the cost bound, the discarded paths have Wiener measure at most $n^{-r}\le v/8$.}
{We also discard paths for which}
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}>
 \frac4v\,\ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega'\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega'\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}\left[f\right]}\right]}.
\end{equation*}
{By Markov's inequality, these paths have Wiener measure at most $v/4$.}
Combined, each of the lower and upper quantile sets still has measure at least $v-v/8-v/4=5v/8$.

These events are Borel because $\omega\mapsto Y^\omega$ is continuous, the cost functions are Borel, and the posterior expectations and variances are continuous. By
\Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-set-gaussian}, there are a retained lower path
$\omega$ and a retained upper path $\omega+h$ with
$\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}\le2\sqrt{2\log(4/v)}$.

Let $y=Y_T^\omega$ and $z=Y_T^{\omega+h}$.
Note that by the above construction, both paths have $U\le C_0\log n$.  
By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-path-distance} and
$\|h\|_{\mathbb H_T}\le2\sqrt{2\log(4/v)}$, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 \|y-z\|_2
 \le\sqrt T\exp(LT)
   \bigl(2\sqrt{2\log(4/v)}+2\sqrt{C_0\log n}\bigr),\quad
 \|y-z\|_2^2
 \le T\exp(2LT)\bigl(16\log(4/v)+8C_0\log n\bigr).
\end{equation*}
\begingroup
We set $\Gamma=8(L+1)T\exp(2LT)$ and $B=(4(L+1)T\exp(2LT)+1/2)C_0$.
By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-posterior-comparison} and $2\sqrt{C_0\log n}\|y-z\|_2\leq C_0\log n+\|y-z\|_2^2$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 D(\mu_y\Vert\mu_z)+D(\mu_z\Vert\mu_y)
 \le(L+1)T\exp(2LT)
   \bigl(16\log(4/v)+8C_0\log n\bigr)+C_0\log n
 =2\Gamma\log(4/v)+2B\log n.
\end{equation*}
Thus $\Gamma$ is independent of the cost threshold $C_0$.
\endgroup
Also by the above construction, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 \max(\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_y}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_y}\left[f\right]},\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_z}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_z}\left[f\right]})
 \le\frac4v\ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega'\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega'\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^{\omega'}}}\left[f\right]}\right]}.
\end{equation*}
Combined with  \Cref{lem:app-wpi-posterior-comparison}, we have that
\begingroup
\begin{align*}
 [Q_f(1-v)-Q_f(v)]^2
 \leq&~\frac{16}{v}\exp(\Gamma\log(4/v)+B\log n)
      \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}\\
 =&~16\cdot4^\Gamma n^Bv^{-1-\Gamma}
      \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}{.}
\end{align*}
\endgroup

We set $q\triangleq Q_f(1/2)$ and let $V\sim\operatorname{Unif}(0,1)$.
The random variable $Q_f(V)$ has the same law as $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}$ under $\omega\sim\gamma$, so using $Q_f(u)\leq q\leq Q_f(1-u)$ for $0<u<1/2$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
 \leq&~ \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bigl(\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}-q\bigr)^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\bigl(\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}-q\bigr)^2\right]}
 = \int_0^{1/2}\bigl((Q_f(u)-q)^2+(Q_f(1-u)-q)^2\bigr)\,\mathrm d u\\
 \leq&~ \int_0^{1/2}[Q_f(1-u)-Q_f(u)]^2\,\mathrm d u.
\end{align*}
The part with $u<s$ is at most $s\operatorname{osc}(f)^2$.
On $[s,1/2]$ we have that 
\begingroup
\begin{equation*}
 \int_s^{1/2}u^{-1-\Gamma}\,\mathrm d u
 =\frac{s^{-\Gamma}-2^\Gamma}{\Gamma}
 \le\frac1\Gamma s^{-\Gamma}.
\end{equation*}
Taking $C=16\cdot4^\Gamma/\Gamma$, we obtain
\endgroup
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
 \le Cn^Bs^{-\Gamma}
       \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
       +s\operatorname{osc}(f)^2.
\end{equation*}
Hence, we have that
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]}
 =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    +\ifthenelse{\isempty{\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}\\
 \leq&~(1+Cn^Bs^{-\Gamma})
          \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{Y_T^\omega}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
          +s\operatorname{osc}(f)^2\\
 \leq&~(1+Cn^Bs^{-\Gamma})a_n\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu}\left(f,f\right)}
    +(s+(1+Cn^Bs^{-\Gamma})\delta_n)
          \operatorname{osc}(f)^2.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-two-path-terminal}
Fix $0<\beta_0<\beta_1<1$.
There are constants $T_0,C,c,N>0$, depending only on $(\beta_0,\beta_1)$, such that, for every $n\geq N$, there is an event $\Omega_n^{\mathrm{end}}$ of $\mathbb P_{0,n}$-probability at least $1-C\exp(-cn)$ with the following property.
For every $A\in\Omega_n^{\mathrm{end}}$ and every $\beta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]$, let $X\sim\mu_{\beta,A}$, let $(B_t)_{0\leq t\leq T_0}$ be an independent standard Brownian motion in $\mathbb{R}^n$ started at $0$, and set $Y_t=tX+B_t$.
Then, for every $f:\{\pm1\}^n\to\mathbb{R}$,
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu_{\beta,A},\,B}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{A,Y_{T_0}}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{A,Y_{T_0}}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu_{\beta,A},\,B}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{A,Y_{T_0}}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{A,Y_{T_0}}}\left[f\right]}\right]}
 \leq Cn^7\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu_{\beta,A}}\left(f,f\right)}
       +\tfrac14\exp(-cn)\operatorname{osc}(f)^2,
\end{equation*}
where $\mu_{A,y}$ is the Gibbs distribution at inverse temperature $\beta$ and external field $y$.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
At $\beta=0$, tensorization for product distributions gives $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_A}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_A}\left[f\right]}\le n\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu_A}\left(f,f\right)}$.

{We fix $0<\beta<1$ and $D,M_0>0$, and choose $0<\beta_0<\beta<\beta_1<1$ depending only on $\beta$.}
We take $T=T_0$ from \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-terminal} on $[\beta_0,\beta_1]$.
We will apply \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion} on a high-probability event of interaction matrices.
We next verify each assumption of this lemma.

By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-branches} with $T$ and
$R=M_0+D+6$, we take the cost function
$\overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}$.
For fixed $A$, define
\[
    \overline{\mathfrak e}(t;y)
    \triangleq
    \overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}(A,t;y),
    \qquad
    t\in[0,T],\quad y\in\mathbb{R}^n.
\]
By part~\textup{(i)} of \Cref{lem:app-wpi-branches},
$\overline{\mathfrak e}$ satisfies the Lipschitz property with cost functions
assumed in \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion}.
Let
\[
    U_{\beta,A}(\omega)
    \triangleq
    \int_0^T
    \overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}
    \bigl(A,t;Y_t^\omega\bigr)\,\mathrm d t, \quad
    E_{\beta,A}(\omega)
    \triangleq
    \overline{\mathfrak e}_{n,\beta}
    \bigl(A,T;Y_T^\omega\bigr).
\]


{Next, we bound the probability that either $U_{\beta,A}$ or $E_{\beta,A}$ exceeds $C_R\log n$, as required by \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion}.}
By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-branches}, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
  \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[U_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n
                 \text{ or }E_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[U_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n
                 \text{ or }E_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[
  \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[U_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n
                 \text{ or }E_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[U_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n
                 \text{ or }E_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n\right]}\right]}
 \le2n^{-R}.
\end{equation*}
Taking $r=M_0+3$, by Markov's inequality, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb P_{0,n}\left\{A:
  \ifthenelse{\isempty{\omega\sim\gamma}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[U_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n
                 \text{ or }E_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega\sim\gamma}\left[U_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n
                 \text{ or }E_{\beta,A}(\omega)>C_R\log n\right]}>n^{-r}\right\}
 \le2n^{r-R}.
\end{equation*}
Finally, on the event $\Omega_n^{\mathrm{end}}$, we apply \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-terminal,lem:app-wpi-observation-law} to obtain the averaged terminal posterior variance bound in \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion} with $a_n=Cn^7$ and $\delta_n=\exp(-cn)/4$.
For every $\varepsilon\in[n^{-M_0},1/2]$, we take $s=\varepsilon/2$.
Since $r=M_0+3$, for all sufficiently large $n$,
$8n^{-r}\le\varepsilon/2\le1/4$.
\begingroup
By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion} with $a_n=Cn^7$ and $\delta_n=\exp(-cn)/4$, there are constants $C,b,\Gamma>0$ such that
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_A}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_A}\left[f\right]}
 \le Cn^7(1+Cn^b(\varepsilon/2)^{-\Gamma})
       \ensuremath{\+E_{\mu_A}\left(f,f\right)}
 +\left(\frac\varepsilon2
       +\frac{1+Cn^b(\varepsilon/2)^{-\Gamma}}4
         \exp(-cn)\right)\operatorname{osc}(f)^2.
\end{equation*}
Here $\Gamma=8(L+1)T\exp(2LT)$ depends only on $\beta$, since $T,L$ depend only on the chosen interval $[\beta_0,\beta_1]$.
Since $\varepsilon\ge n^{-M_0}$, for sufficiently large $n$, we have that 
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{1+C2^\Gamma n^{b+M_0\Gamma}}4
       \exp(-cn)
 \le\frac12n^{-M_0}\le\frac\varepsilon2.
\end{equation*}
Also, by $n\ge2$ and $\varepsilon\le1/2$, we have $Cn^7(1+Cn^b(\varepsilon/2)^{-\Gamma})\leq C'n^{7+b}\varepsilon^{-\Gamma}$.
Taking $B=b/\Gamma$ gives the coefficient in \Cref{thm:app-wpi-poly}, with $C,B,N$ depending only on $(\beta,D,M_0)$.
\endgroup
Finally, the union bound gives a failure probability of at most $2n^{r-R}+C\exp(-cn)=2n^{-D-3}+C\exp(-cn)\le n^{-D}$ for all sufficiently large $n$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:app-wpi-schedule}
Fix $0\leq\beta_*<1$ and $D,M_0,C_{\mathrm{sch}}>0$.
{There are constants $\Gamma>0$, depending only on $\beta_*$, and $C,a,N>0$, depending only on $(\beta_*,D,M_0,C_{\mathrm{sch}})$, such that every deterministic}
$\mathcal B_n\subseteq [0,\beta_*]$ with
$|\mathcal B_n|\leq n^{C_{\mathrm{sch}}}$ has an event of
$\mathbb P_{0,n}$-probability at least $1-n^{-D}$ on which
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{\beta,A}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{\beta,A}}\left[f\right]}
 \leq Cn^a\varepsilon^{-\Gamma}\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu_{\beta,A}}\left(f,f\right)}
       +\varepsilon\operatorname{osc}(f)^2
\end{equation*}
for all $\beta\in\mathcal B_n$, all
$\varepsilon\in[n^{-M_0},1/2]$, and all $f:\{\pm1\}^n\to\mathbb{R}$, when $n\geq N$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
We fix $0\leq\beta_*<1$ and a deterministic schedule $\mathcal B_n\subseteq[0,\beta_*]$ with $|\mathcal B_n|\leq n^{C_{\mathrm{sch}}}$.
We take $r=M_0+3$ and choose $R>D+C_{\mathrm{sch}}+r+3$.
We prove this corollary by considering two cases, small $\beta$ and large $\beta$.

We first handle the large $\beta$.
Suppose $\beta_*>1/15$.
By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-terminal} with $(\beta_0,\beta_1)=(1/15,\beta_*)$, we choose one terminal time $T_0$ and {the matrix event $\Omega_n^{\mathrm{end}}$} for every $\beta\in[1/15,\beta_*]$.
By \Cref{lem:app-wpi-branches} on this interval with $T=T_0$ and the chosen $R$, we use the same Lipschitz constant $L$, error threshold $C_R\log n$, and lower bound on $n$ for every such $\beta$.

{In \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-criterion}, the exponent $\Gamma$ depends only on $T_0,L$, hence only on $\beta_*$, while $C,B$ may also depend on $C_R$.}
The constants in the averaged terminal posterior variance bound of \Cref{lem:app-wpi-two-path-terminal} are also common to the interval.
Going through the proof of \Cref{thm:app-wpi-poly} with these choices therefore gives constants $C,a,\Gamma,N$, independent of $\beta\in[1/15,\beta_*]$, such that for each fixed $\beta$ the claimed WPI holds for all $f$ and all $\varepsilon\in[n^{-M_0},1/2]$ outside a matrix event of probability at most $2n^{r-R}+C\exp(-cn)$.

For $0\leq\beta\leq1/15$, the event $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq3$ gives $0\preceq\beta A+3\beta I_n\preceq(2/5)I_n$.
The diagonal shift leaves the Gibbs distribution unchanged, so \cite[Theorem~1]{EldanKoehlerZeitouni22} gives $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_{\beta,A}}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_{\beta,A}}\left[f\right]}\leq(5n/3)\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu_{\beta,A}}\left(f,f\right)}$ for every $f$.
If $\beta_*\leq1/15$, we take $\Gamma=1$, $a=1$, and $C=5/3$.
By \Cref{lem:terminal-disorder-tail} with $\beta_1=1/2$, the probability that $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}>3$ is at most $C\exp(-cn)$.
After increasing $C,a,N$ if necessary, the same WPI constants and the same failure bound $2n^{r-R}+C\exp(-cn)$ thus apply to each fixed $\beta\in[0,\beta_*]$.

We now take the union over the prescribed schedule:
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb P_{0,n}\left\{\text{the WPI fails for some }\beta\in\mathcal B_n\right\}
 \leq |\mathcal B_n|(2n^{r-R}+C\exp(-cn))
 \leq n^{-D}
\end{equation*}
for all sufficiently large $n$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-warm-decay}
Let $P$ be a reversible Markov kernel on a finite space, with
stationary law $\mu$ and spectrum in $[0,1]$.
Suppose $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu}\left[f\right]}\leq A_0\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu}\left(f,f\right)}
+\delta\operatorname{osc}(f)^2$, with $A_0\geq1$.
For every $B_0$-warm initial law $\mu^{\mathrm{init}}$ and integer $k\geq0$,
\begin{equation*}
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu^{\mathrm{init}} P^k},{\mu}\right)
 \leq\frac12\left(\mathrm{e}^{-k/A_0}(B_0-1)+\delta B_0^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
We set $f_k=\,\-d(\mu^{\mathrm{init}}P^k)/\,\-d\mu$ and
$v_k=\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f_k\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu}\left[f_k\right]}=\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(f_k-1)^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[(f_k-1)^2\right]}$.
For every state $y$ with $\mu(y)>0$, by detailed balance we have that
\begin{equation*}
 f_{k+1}(y)
 =\frac{\sum_x\mu(x)f_k(x)P(x,y)}{\mu(y)}
 =\sum_xP(y,x)f_k(x)=(Pf_k)(y).
\end{equation*}
Thus $f_k=P^kf_0$.  Since a Markov kernel preserves the interval $[0,B_0]$, we have $0\leq f_k\leq B_0$ and
$\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f_k\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f_k\right]}=1$.  
In particular, $v_0=\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f_0^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f_0^2\right]}-1\leq B_0\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[f_0\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[f_0\right]}-1=B_0-1$.

For every spectral value $\lambda\in[0,1]$,
$1-\lambda^2\geq1-\lambda$.  By self-adjointness of $P$,
$I-P^2\succeq I-P$ on $L^2(\mu)$, and hence
\begin{align*}
 v_k-v_{k+1}
 =&~\langle f_k,(I-P^2)f_k\rangle_\mu
 \geq\ensuremath{\+E_{\mu}\left(f_k,f_k\right)}
 \geq A_0^{-1}(v_k-\delta B_0^2),
\end{align*}
where the last inequality holds due to the WPI and $\operatorname{osc}(f_k)\leq B_0$.
Writing the resulting recurrence as
$v_{k+1}\leq(1-A_0^{-1})v_k+A_0^{-1}\delta B_0^2$ and iterating,
we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 v_k\leq(1-A_0^{-1})^kv_0
       +\delta B_0^2(1-(1-A_0^{-1})^k)
 \leq\mathrm{e}^{-k/A_0}(B_0-1)+\delta B_0^2.
\end{equation*}
Finally, by Cauchy--Schwarz,
$d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu^{\mathrm{init}}P^k},{\mu}\right)
=\frac12\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|f_k-1|\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[|f_k-1|\right]}\leq\frac12\sqrt{v_k}${,} which finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:app-wpi-annealing-composition}
Let $\mu_0,\ldots,\mu_L$ be probability measures on the same finite
space.  For $1\leq j\leq L$, let $P_j$ be reversible with stationary
law $\mu_j$ and nonnegative spectrum, and suppose
\begin{equation*}
 \mu_{j-1}\leq B_j\mu_j,\qquad
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\mu_j}}
    {\operatorname*{Var}\left[f\right]}
    {\operatorname*{Var}_{\mu_j}\left[f\right]}\leq A_j\langle f,(I-P_j)f\rangle_{\mu_j}
          +\delta_j\operatorname{osc}(f)^2,\qquad A_j\geq1.
\end{equation*}
Then, for nonnegative integers $k_1,\ldots,k_L$,
\begin{equation*}
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu_0P_1^{k_1}\cdots P_L^{k_L}},{\mu_L}\right)
 \leq\frac12\sum_{j=1}^L
      \left(\mathrm{e}^{-k_j/A_j}(B_j-1)+\delta_jB_j^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
{We write $\widehat\mu_{j-1}=\mu_0P_1^{k_1}\cdots P_{j-1}^{k_{j-1}}$ for the distribution at the start of stage $j$.}
{We compare $\widehat\mu_{j-1}$ with $\mu_{j-1}$, which is $B_j$-warm with respect to $\mu_j$.}
By total-variation contraction under $P_j^{k_j}$ and the triangle inequality we have
\begin{align*}
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\widehat\mu_{j-1}P_j^{k_j}},{\mu_j}\right)
 \leq&~d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\widehat\mu_{j-1}P_j^{k_j}},{\mu_{j-1}P_j^{k_j}}\right)
       +d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu_{j-1}P_j^{k_j}},{\mu_j}\right)\\
 \leq&~d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\widehat\mu_{j-1}},{\mu_{j-1}}\right)
       +d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu_{j-1}P_j^{k_j}},{\mu_j}\right).
\end{align*}
Starting from $\widehat\mu_0=\mu_0$, we iterate this inequality and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu_0P_1^{k_1}\cdots P_L^{k_L}},{\mu_L}\right)
 \leq\sum_{j=1}^Ld_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu_{j-1}P_j^{k_j}},{\mu_j}\right).
\end{equation*}
{We apply \Cref{lem:app-wpi-warm-decay} to each term to finish the proof.}
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
If $\beta=0$, we directly sample from the uniform product distribution. 
We assume that $\beta > 0$ and  we use \Cref{alg:app-wpi-annealing} and \Cref{lem:app-wpi-annealing-composition} to prove this theorem.
There are $L\leq n$ stages in \Cref{alg:app-wpi-annealing}.  


We first verify warmness between consecutive Gibbs {distributions}.
On $\left\VertA\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq3$, the energy $H_A(x)=\langle x,Ax\rangle/2$ satisfies $|H_A(x)|\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|x\|_2^2/2\leq3n/2$ for every spin $x$.  
Therefore $\sup_{x} H_A(x)-\inf_{x} H_A(x)\leq3n$.
For every spin $x$, the normalized Gibbs probabilities satisfy
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{\mu_{\beta_{j-1},A}(x)}{\mu_{\beta_j,A}(x)}
 =\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta_j-\beta_{j-1})H_A(x)}
   \ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu_{\beta_{j-1},A}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
        \mathrm{e}^{(\beta_j-\beta_{j-1})H_A(X)}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu_{\beta_{j-1},A}}\left[
        \mathrm{e}^{(\beta_j-\beta_{j-1})H_A(X)}\right]}.
\end{equation*}
We bound the first factor using $\inf H_A$ and the second factor using $\sup H_A$.  
Since $\beta_j-\beta_{j-1}\leq1/n$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\|\frac{\,\-d\mu_{\beta_{j-1},A}}
                  {\,\-d\mu_{\beta_j,A}}\right\|_\infty
 \leq\exp(3n(\beta_j-\beta_{j-1}))\leq B_0\triangleq\mathrm{e}^3.
\end{equation*}

Next, we apply \Cref{cor:app-wpi-schedule} to $\mathcal B_n=\{\beta_j:0\leq j\leq L\}$, which has at most $n+1\leq n^2$ points for large $n$, with $C_{\mathrm{sch}}=2$,  $D+1$, and $M_0=2M+5$.
Let $C\geq1$ and $a,\Gamma\geq0$ be constants in \Cref{cor:app-wpi-schedule}.
Set $\varepsilon=\mathrm{e}^{-6}n^{-(2M+4)}$ and $p=\bigl\lceil a+(2M+4)\Gamma\bigr\rceil+2$.
For all sufficiently large $n$, $\varepsilon/n^{-M_0}=\mathrm{e}^{-6}n\geq1$ and $\varepsilon\leq1/2$.
{We write $\mu_j=\mu_{\beta_j,A}$, and let $P_j$ be its random-scan heat-bath Glauber transition matrix.}
Combined with \Cref{lem:app-wpi-annealing-composition}, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\mu_0P_1^{\lceil n^p \rceil}\cdots P_L^{\lceil n^p \rceil}},{\mu_L}\right)
 \leq\frac12\sum_{j=1}^L
      \left(\mathrm{e}^{- \lceil n^p \rceil / (C n^a \varepsilon^{-\Gamma})}(\mathrm{e}^3-1)+\varepsilon \mathrm{e}^6\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation*}
Since $C n^a\varepsilon^{-\Gamma}=C\mathrm{e}^{6\Gamma}n^{a+(2M+4)\Gamma}\geq1$ and $p\geq a+(2M+4)\Gamma+2$, the exponent ratio in the first term is at least $n^2/(C\mathrm{e}^{6\Gamma})$.
Thus, for all sufficiently large $n$, both terms inside the parentheses are at most $n^{-2(M+2)}$, and the total variation distance is at most $L n^{-(M+2)}\leq n^{-(M+1)}\leq n^{-M}$.

By a union bound, the common WPI event and $\{A:\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq3\}$ hold simultaneously with probability at least $1-n^{-(D+1)}-C\mathrm{e}^{-cn}\geq1-n^{-D}$ for all sufficiently large $n$.
Each of the $L\lceil n^p\rceil$ updates costs $O(n)$ exact-real operations, so including the input matrix and initial spins, the running time is $O(n^2+nL\lceil n^p\rceil)=n^{O_{\beta,M,D}(1)}$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
If $\beta=0$, we output $\widehat Z=2^n$.
We assume that $\beta>0$ and estimate the ratios of consecutive partition functions along the schedule in \Cref{alg:app-wpi-annealing}.
We recall that $L=\lceil \beta n\rceil\leq n$, $\beta_j=j\beta/L$, and $H_A(x)=\langle x,Ax\rangle/2$.
We write $Z_j=\sum_{x\in\{\pm1\}^n}\mathrm{e}^{\beta_jH_A(x)}$ and set $g_j(x)=\mathrm{e}^{(\beta_j-\beta_{j-1})H_A(x)}$ for $1\leq j\leq L$.
Then $Z_0=2^n$, $Z_L=Z_A(0)$, and
\begin{equation*}
 r_j\triangleq\frac{Z_j}{Z_{j-1}}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{X\sim\mu_{\beta_{j-1},A}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_j(X)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mu_{\beta_{j-1},A}}\left[g_j(X)\right]}.
\end{equation*}
Thus we can estimate $Z_A(0)=2^n\prod_{j=1}^Lr_j$ by estimating these expectations.

We first choose one sampler that works at every temperature in the schedule.
We use the proof of \Cref{thm:app-wpi-annealing} with $M+3$ in place of $M$ and the common event from \Cref{cor:app-wpi-schedule}.
We obtain a fixed update exponent $p$ and an event of $\mathbb P_{0,n}$-probability at least $1-n^{-D}$ on which $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq3$ and $d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left({\nu_j},{\mu_{\beta_j,A}}\right)\leq n^{-(M+3)}$ for every $0\leq j\leq L$.
Here $\nu_j$ is the output law of \Cref{alg:app-wpi-annealing} stopped after stage $j$; by \Cref{lem:app-wpi-annealing-composition}, its error is bounded by the sum of the errors of the first $j$ stages.
We fix $A$ in this event for the rest of the proof.
Since $|H_A(x)|\leq3n/2$ and $\beta_j-\beta_{j-1}\leq1/n$, we have $\mathrm{e}^{-3/2}\leq g_j(x)\leq\mathrm{e}^{3/2}$ and hence $r_j\geq\mathrm{e}^{-3/2}$.

We set $\varepsilon=n^{-M}/(4L)$ and $s=\lceil 2\mathrm{e}^6\varepsilon^{-2}\log(2L/\eta)\rceil$.
For each $j$, we use independent runs of the sampler stopped after stage $j-1$ to draw $X_{j,1},\ldots,X_{j,s}$ with law $\nu_{j-1}$, and set $\widehat r_j=s^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^s g_j(X_{j,k})$.
We output $\widehat Z=2^n\prod_{j=1}^L\widehat r_j$.
By the total-variation bound and the bounds on $g_j$ and $r_j$, for all sufficiently large $n$ we have
\begin{equation*}
 \left|\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat r_j\mid A\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\widehat r_j\mid A\right]}-r_j\right|
 \leq\mathrm{e}^{3/2}n^{-(M+3)}
 \leq\frac{\varepsilon r_j}{2}.
\end{equation*}
Applying Hoeffding's inequality to the independent variables $g_j(X_{j,k})$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left|\widehat r_j/r_j-1\right|>\varepsilon\mid A\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{}\left[\left|\widehat r_j/r_j-1\right|>\varepsilon\mid A\right]}
 \leq2\exp\left(-\frac{s\varepsilon^2}{2\mathrm{e}^6}\right)
 \leq\frac{\eta}{L}.
\end{equation*}
By a union bound, with probability at least $1-\eta$, all $L$ relative errors are at most $\varepsilon$.
Since $\varepsilon\leq1/4$, on this event we have
\begin{equation*}
 \left|\log\frac{\widehat Z}{Z_A(0)}\right|
 \leq\sum_{j=1}^L\left|\log\frac{\widehat r_j}{r_j}\right|
 \leq2L\varepsilon=\frac{n^{-M}}{2},
\end{equation*}
so $|\widehat Z/Z_A(0)-1|\leq\exp(n^{-M}/2)-1\leq n^{-M}$.

Finally, $L\leq n$, $s=O(n^{2M+2}\log(2n/\eta))$, and every sample takes $n^{O_{\beta,M,D}(1)}$ time.
Combined, the total running time is $n^{O_{\beta,M,D}(1)}\log(1/\eta)$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
We compute $L_n(A)$ by averaging the conditional Gaussian density of $A$ over the uniform spin in the planted model:
\begin{align*}
  L_n(A)
  =&~2^{-n}\sum_{x\in\{\pm1\}^n}
  \exp\left(\beta\sum_{i<j}A_{ij}x_ix_j-\frac{\beta^2(n-1)}4\right)\\
  =&~2^{-n}Z_A(0)\exp(-\beta^2(n-1)/4).
\end{align*}
Thus $L_n(A)=\exp(\log Z_A(0)-n\log2-\beta^2(n-1)/4)$.
This is the Rademacher specialization of \cite[Eq.~(3)]{ElAlaouiKrzakalaJordan20}, with $\lambda=\beta^2${, $\sigma=+\infty$,} and $Y_{ij}=\sqrt n A_{ij}$.
The corresponding asymptotic mutual contiguity for $\beta<1$ follows from \cite[Theorem~2 and Corollary~3]{ElAlaouiKrzakalaJordan20}.
Since $L_n$ is the density of $\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl}}$ relative to $\mathbb P_{0,n}$, we have $\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n(A)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n(A)\right]}=1$.

To bound the second moment, we take independent uniform spins $x,x'$ and set $\xi_i=x_ix_i'$.
Integrating over $A$ and then applying the Hubbard--Stratonovich identity with $G\sim N(0,1)$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
  \ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n(A)^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n(A)^2\right]}
  =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{\xi\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm1\}^n)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta^2}{2n}\left((\sum_i\xi_i)^2-n\right)\right)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\{\pm1\}^n)}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\beta^2}{2n}\left((\sum_i\xi_i)^2-n\right)\right)\right]}\\
  =&~\mathrm{e}^{-\beta^2/2}\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cosh(\beta G/\sqrt n)^n\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,1)}\left[\cosh(\beta G/\sqrt n)^n\right]}\\
  \leq&~\mathrm{e}^{-\beta^2/2}(1-\beta^2)^{-1/2}
  \leq(1-\beta^2)^{-1/2}.
\end{align*}
Here we used $\log\cosh z\leq z^2/2$ and $\beta<1$ in the last line.

To control the inverse moment, let $g=(\sqrt n A_{ij})_{i<j}$ and $F(g)=\log L_n(A)$.
This finite log-sum-exp of affine functions of the standard Gaussian vector $g$ is convex and Lipschitz with constant $\beta\sqrt{(n-1)/2}$, so all its exponential moments are finite.
Set $\Theta(\lambda)=\lambda^{-1}\log\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n(A)^\lambda\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n(A)^\lambda\right]}$ for $\lambda\ne0$, with $\Theta(0)=\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\log L_n(A)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[\log L_n(A)\right]}$.
By \cite[Theorem~1]{Chen23}, $\Theta$ is convex on $\mathbb{R}$.
Since $\Theta(1)=0$, its successive secant slopes satisfy $\Theta(0)-\Theta(-1)\leq\Theta(1)-\Theta(0)\leq\Theta(2)-\Theta(1)$, and therefore
\begin{equation*}
  \log\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n(A)^{-1}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n(A)^{-1}\right]}
  =-\Theta(-1)\leq2(\Theta(2)-\Theta(1))
  =\log\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n(A)^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n(A)^2\right]}.
\end{equation*}
The second-moment bound now also bounds the inverse moment.

For every event $E$ determined by $(X,A,d,Y)$, we apply Cauchy--Schwarz to \cref{eq:planting-density-ratio} and use the second-moment bound to obtain
\begin{align*}
  \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}\left(E\right)
  =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{(X,A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)\1_E\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{(X,A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)\1_E\right]}\\
  \leq&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^2\right]}^{1/2}
    \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}\left(E\right)^{1/2}\\
  \leq&~(1-\beta^2)^{-1/4}\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}\left(E\right)^{1/2}.
\end{align*}
For the reverse direction, we first use the density ratio and the inverse-moment bound to compute
\begin{equation*}
  \ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl}}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-2}\right]}
  =\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-1}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{0,n}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-1}\right]}
  \leq(1-\beta^2)^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}
We then apply Cauchy--Schwarz to the reciprocal density and obtain
\begin{align*}
  \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}\left(E\right)
  =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{(X,A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-1}\1_E\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{(X,A,d,Y)\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-1}\1_E\right]}\\
  \leq&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{A\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{A\sim\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl}}}\left[L_n\left(A\right)^{-2}\right]}^{1/2}
    \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}\left(E\right)^{1/2}\\
  \leq&~(1-\beta^2)^{-1/4}\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}\left(E\right)^{1/2}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-gaussian-poincare-p}
Let $M,k\geq1$, $p\geq2$, and $G\sim N(0,I_M)$.
All $L^p(G)$ norms are taken under this Gaussian law, using the Euclidean
norm for vectors and the Frobenius norm for matrices.
There is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p$, for which the following
statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For every smooth $F:\mathbb R^M\to\mathbb R^k$ such that $F(G)$ and
$D_GF(G)$ belong to $L^p(G)$,
\begin{equation*}
 \|F(G)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[F(G)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[F(G)\right]}\|_{L^p(G)}
 \leq C_p\left\VertD_GF(G)\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{equation*}
For $p=2$, we may take $C_2=1$.

\item For every smooth $F:\mathbb R^M\to\mathbb R^M$ such that $F(G)$ and
$D_GF(G)$ belong to $L^p(G)$,
\begin{equation*}
 \|\delta_GF\|_{L^p(G)}
 \leq C_p\left(
  \left\VertF(G)\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
  +\left\VertD_GF(G)\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\right).
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
For part~\textup{(i)}, we apply Pisier's inequality with the convex
function $z\mapsto\|z\|_2^p$ and an independent $G'\sim N(0,I_M)$:
\begin{equation*}
 \|F(G)-\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[F(G)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[F(G)\right]}\|_{L^p(G)}
 \leq\frac\pi2\left\|D_GF(G)G'\right\|_{L^p(G,G')}
 \leq C_p\left\|D_GF(G)\right\|_{L^p(G)}.
\end{equation*}
For the last inequality, we condition on $G$ and apply Minkowski in
$L^{p/2}(G')$ to the sum of squared coordinates; each coordinate is
Gaussian with variance equal to the squared norm of the corresponding
Jacobian row.  At $p=2$, summing the scalar Gaussian Poincar\'e
inequalities over the coordinates of $F$ yields $C_2=1$.
Part~\textup{(ii)} is Meyer's inequality for the Gaussian divergence.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-weak-tilt}
For the cavity interpolation of \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning} with $M=n-1\geq1$, given $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$, the following bound holds for every $p\ge2$:
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_{|\gamma|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
 \leq\mathrm e^{40\ell M/p}
 \left\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\right\rangle_{\mu_0^{\otimes4}}^{1/p}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
For four replicas, the Radon--Nikodym derivative is
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d\mu_\gamma^{\otimes4}}
      {\mathrm d\mu_0^{\otimes4}}(\sigma^1,\ldots,\sigma^4)
 =\prod_{r=1}^4\frac{\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot\sigma^r}}{Z_\gamma}
 =Z_\gamma^{-4}\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot(\sigma^1+\cdots+\sigma^4)}.
\]
We set $S=\sigma^1+\cdots+\sigma^4$.  Jensen's inequality gives $Z_\gamma=\langle \mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot\sigma}\rangle_0
 \ge \mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot m_0}$, so
\[
 Z_\gamma^{-4}\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot S}
 \le \mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot(S-4m_0)}.
\]
We fix the replicas and set $W=S-4m_0$.  Since every coordinate of $W$ lies
in $[-8,8]$, we have
\[
 |U\cdot W|\le8M,\qquad \left\VertW\right\Vert_2^2\le64M.
\]
We use $a=\ell U+\sqrt\ell G$ and evaluate the Gaussian MGF:
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot W}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot W}\right]}
 =&~\exp\left(\gamma\ell U\cdot W
              +\frac{\gamma^2\ell}{2}\left\VertW\right\Vert_2^2\right)
 \le \mathrm e^{8\ell M+32\ell M}=\mathrm e^{40\ell M}.
\end{align*}
For any nonnegative function $F$ of the four replicas, with no explicit dependence on $G$, we multiply by $F$ and interchange the finite replica sum and the Gaussian expectation:
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle F\rangle_{\mu_\gamma^{\otimes4}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\langle F\rangle_{\mu_\gamma^{\otimes4}}\right]}
 =\left\langle F\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
       Z_\gamma^{-4}\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot S}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[
       Z_\gamma^{-4}\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot S}\right]}\right\rangle_{\mu_0^{\otimes4}}
 \leq\mathrm e^{40\ell M}\langle F\rangle_{\mu_0^{\otimes4}}.
\end{equation*}
To bound the tilted covariance, we apply
\cref{eq:tap-covariance-rectangle} under $\mu_\gamma$ and obtain
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_\gamma^2
 =\langle\mathcal D_{1234}\rangle_{\mu_\gamma^{\otimes4}}.
\]
For each fixed Gaussian vector $G$ and $p\ge2$, we apply Jensen's inequality
under $\mu_\gamma^{\otimes4}$ and obtain
\[
 \left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^{p}
 =(\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_\gamma^2)^{p/2}
 \le\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\rangle_{\mu_\gamma^{\otimes4}}.
\]
We apply the nonnegative comparison just proved with $F=\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}$ and obtain, for every $|\gamma|\leq1$,
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^{p}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^{p}\right]}
 \leq\mathrm e^{40\ell M}\left\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\right\rangle_{\mu_0^{\otimes4}}.
\end{equation*}
Taking $p$th roots and then the supremum over $|\gamma|\leq1$ proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-tilted-mean-displacement}
For the cavity interpolation of \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning}, assume $M=n-1\geq1$ and $0\leq\ell M\leq1$.
Given $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$, for every fixed $p\geq2$ and $|\gamma|\leq1$, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vertm_\gamma-m_0\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\leq C_p|\gamma|\sqrt\ell\sup_{|r|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_r\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
We first collect the derivatives with respect to $\gamma$.
With $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U,G)$ fixed, we have $\mu_\gamma(\sigma)=\mu_0(\sigma)\mathrm e^{\gamma a\cdot\sigma}/Z_\gamma$.
Differentiating this identity, we obtain
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\gamma}\log Z_\gamma=a\cdot m_\gamma,\qquad
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\gamma} \mu_\gamma(\sigma)
 =\mu_\gamma(\sigma)a\cdot(\sigma-m_\gamma).
\]
Multiplying the second identity by $\sigma_j$ and summing gives
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\gamma}(m_\gamma)_j
 =\left\langle\sigma_j\,a\cdot(\sigma-m_\gamma)\right\rangle_\gamma
 =\sum_k a_k
   \left\langle\sigma_j(\sigma_k-(m_\gamma)_k)\right\rangle_\gamma
 =\sum_k a_k(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}=(\Sigma_\gamma a)_j.
\]

We next collect the derivatives with respect to $G$.  Since
$\partial_{G_k}a_j=\sqrt\ell\,\1_{\{j=k\}}$, we have
\[
 \partial_{G_k}\log Z_\gamma
 =\gamma\sqrt\ell\,(m_\gamma)_k,\qquad
 \partial_{G_k}\mu_\gamma(\sigma)
 =\gamma\sqrt\ell\,\mu_\gamma(\sigma)
   (\sigma_k-(m_\gamma)_k).
\]
Consequently,
\[
 \partial_{G_k}(m_\gamma)_j
 =\gamma\sqrt\ell\,
   \left\langle\sigma_j(\sigma_k-(m_\gamma)_k)\right\rangle_\gamma
 =\gamma\sqrt\ell\,(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}.
\]
For a function $F=F(G,\sigma)$ differentiable in $G$, differentiating
both the Gibbs weight and $F$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \partial_{G_k}\langle F\rangle_\gamma
 =&~\sum_\sigma \partial_{G_k}\mu_\gamma(\sigma)F(G,\sigma)
   +\sum_\sigma\mu_\gamma(\sigma)\partial_{G_k}F(G,\sigma)\\
 =&~\gamma\sqrt\ell\,
   \left\langle F(\sigma_k-(m_\gamma)_k)\right\rangle_\gamma
   +\left\langle\partial_{G_k}F\right\rangle_\gamma.
\end{align*}
For
$F=(\sigma_j-(m_\gamma)_j)(\sigma_k-(m_\gamma)_k)$, we have
\[
 \partial_{G_k}F
 =-(\partial_{G_k}(m_\gamma)_j)(\sigma_k-(m_\gamma)_k)
  -(\partial_{G_k}(m_\gamma)_k)(\sigma_j-(m_\gamma)_j),
\]
and hence $\langle\partial_{G_k}F\rangle_\gamma=0$.  Using
$\sigma_k^2=1$, we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \partial_{G_k}(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}
 =&~\gamma\sqrt\ell
   \left\langle(\sigma_j-(m_\gamma)_j)
       (\sigma_k-(m_\gamma)_k)^2\right\rangle_\gamma\\
 =&~\gamma\sqrt\ell\bigl(1+(m_\gamma)_k^2\bigr)
       \left\langle\sigma_j-(m_\gamma)_j\right\rangle_\gamma
   -2\gamma\sqrt\ell\,(m_\gamma)_k
       \left\langle(\sigma_j-(m_\gamma)_j)\sigma_k\right\rangle_\gamma\\
 =&~-2\gamma\sqrt\ell\,(m_\gamma)_k(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Every entry of $\Sigma_\gamma$ is bounded, and the derivatives just
computed are bounded by constants depending on $M,\ell$.  The
$\gamma$-derivative is dominated by a constant times $1+\left\VertG\right\Vert_2$,
so differentiation under the Gaussian expectation and Gaussian
integration by parts are valid.

We now combine the two groups of derivatives by Gaussian integration by
parts.  Writing
$a=\ell U+\sqrt\ell G$ and integrating the Gaussian term by parts, we
obtain, component by component,
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\gamma}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(m_\gamma)_j\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[(m_\gamma)_j\right]}
 =&~\ell\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\Sigma_\gamma U)_j\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[(\Sigma_\gamma U)_j\right]}
   +\sqrt\ell\sum_k
      \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[G_k(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[G_k(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}\right]}\\
 =&~\ell\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\Sigma_\gamma U)_j\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[(\Sigma_\gamma U)_j\right]}
   +\sqrt\ell\sum_k
      \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
        \partial_{G_k}(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[
        \partial_{G_k}(\Sigma_\gamma)_{jk}\right]}\\
 =&~\ell\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
      (\Sigma_\gamma(U-2\gamma m_\gamma))_j\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[
      (\Sigma_\gamma(U-2\gamma m_\gamma))_j\right]}.
\end{align*}
Thus, in vector form,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\gamma}\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[m_\gamma\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[m_\gamma\right]}
 =\ell\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
      \Sigma_\gamma(U-2\gamma m_\gamma)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[
      \Sigma_\gamma(U-2\gamma m_\gamma)\right]}.
 \label{eq:tap-mean-derivative}
\end{equation}

Since $m_0$ does not depend on $G$, integrating
\cref{eq:tap-mean-derivative}, and applying the triangle and Jensen
inequalities, gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[m_\gamma\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[m_\gamma\right]}-m_0\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\ell\int_{\min(0,\gamma)}^{\max(0,\gamma)}
       \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
          \left\Vert\Sigma_r(U-2rm_r)\right\Vert_2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[
          \left\Vert\Sigma_r(U-2rm_r)\right\Vert_2\right]}\mathrm d r\\
 \leq&~3\ell\sqrt M
       \int_{\min(0,\gamma)}^{\max(0,\gamma)}
       \left\Vert\Sigma_r\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\mathrm d r\\
 \leq&~3|\gamma|\ell\sqrt M
       \sup_{|r|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_r\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{align*}
For the centered fluctuation, \Cref{lem:tap-gaussian-poincare-p}-\textup{(i)} yields
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vertm_\gamma-\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[m_\gamma\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[m_\gamma\right]}\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
 \leq C_p|\gamma|\sqrt\ell\,
       \left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{equation*}
Combining the two preceding inequalities with the triangle inequality,
and using $\ell M\leq1$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vertm_\gamma-m_0\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
 \leq&~\left\Vertm_\gamma-
       \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[m_\gamma\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[m_\gamma\right]}\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
       +\left\Vert\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[m_\gamma\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[m_\gamma\right]}-m_0\right\Vert_2\\
 \leq&~C_p|\gamma|\sqrt\ell
       \sup_{|r|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_r\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{align*}
This proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- restatedlemma-1: body_md -->
For the cavity interpolation of \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning}, assume $M=n-1\geq1$ and $0\leq\ell M\leq1$.
We condition on $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$ and let $x$ be any $[-1,1]$-valued random variable that may depend on $G$ and additional randomness.
Under this conditioning, $\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathrm{row}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cdot\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathrm{row}}\left[\cdot\right]}$ and $L^p(\mathrm{row})$ average over $G$ and the additional randomness used to construct $x$, while $\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cdot\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\cdot\right]}$ and $L^p(G)$ average $G$ alone.
We define
\[
 \overline m_x=\tfrac{1+x}{2}m_{+1}+\tfrac{1-x}{2}m_{-1},
\]
\[
r_{\mathrm{row}}
 =\tfrac12(\log Z_{+1}-\log Z_{-1})-a\cdot \overline m_x
       +\ell(M-\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2^2)x.
\]
Then, for every $p\geq2$, there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p$, such that
\begin{equation*}
 \|r_{\mathrm{row}}\|_{L^p(\mathrm{row})}\leq C_p\ell\sup_{|\gamma|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end restatedlemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
We fix $p\geq2$ and condition on $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$.
Under this conditioning, $m_0$ does not depend on $G$, and we have $a=\ell U+\sqrt\ell\,G$ and $\left\VertU\right\Vert_2=\sqrt M$.
Throughout the proof, $C_p$ denotes a constant depending only on $p$ that may change from line to line.
