Using the definition of $\overline m_x$, we decompose the residual as
\[
 r_{\mathrm{row}}=A_i+xB_i,
\]
where
\[
 A_i \triangleq\frac12(\log Z_{+1}-\log Z_{-1})
              -\frac12a\cdot(m_{+1}+m_{-1}),
\]
\[
 B_i \triangleq\ell(M-\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2^2)
              -\frac12a\cdot(m_{+1}-m_{-1}).
\]
We estimate these two errors separately by subtracting the fixed cavity trace from the row response.

For $|\gamma|\leq1$, we set $F_\gamma\triangleq m_\gamma-m_0$ and define
\[
 \Delta_\gamma\triangleq a\cdot F_\gamma-\gamma\ell\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_0.
\]
Since $D_GF_\gamma=\gamma\sqrt\ell\,\Sigma_\gamma$, we expand the Gaussian divergence as
\[
 \delta_GF_\gamma
 =G\cdot F_\gamma-\gamma\sqrt\ell\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_\gamma.
\]
Substituting $a=\ell U+\sqrt\ell\,G$ into the definition of $\Delta_\gamma$, we obtain the exact decomposition
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_\gamma=\ell U\cdot F_\gamma+\sqrt\ell\,\delta_GF_\gamma
       +\gamma\ell(\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_\gamma-\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_0).
 \label{eq:tap-divergence-cancellation}
\end{equation}
For Ising spins, we have $\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_\gamma=M-\left\Vertm_\gamma\right\Vert_2^2$.
Using $\left\Vertm_\gamma\right\Vert_2,\left\Vertm_0\right\Vert_2\leq\sqrt M$, we obtain
\[
 |\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_\gamma-\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_0|
 =|\left\Vertm_\gamma\right\Vert_2^2-\left\Vertm_0\right\Vert_2^2|
 \leq2\sqrt M\,\left\VertF_\gamma\right\Vert_2.
\]
We apply the Gaussian divergence estimate in \Cref{lem:tap-gaussian-poincare-p}-\textup{(ii)} to $F_\gamma$ in \cref{eq:tap-divergence-cancellation}.
Together with $|\gamma|\leq1$ and the trace-difference bound, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\Delta_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
 \leq&~\ell\sqrt M\,\left\VertF_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
       +C_p\sqrt\ell\left(\left\VertF_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
              +|\gamma|\sqrt\ell\,\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\right)
 +2|\gamma|\ell\sqrt M\,\left\VertF_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\\
 \leq&~C_p(\sqrt\ell+\ell\sqrt M)\left\VertF_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
       +C_p|\gamma|\ell\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{align*}
By \Cref{lem:tap-tilted-mean-displacement}, we can upper bound the mean displacement by
\[
 \left\VertF_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
 \leq C_p|\gamma|\sqrt\ell
       \sup_{|r|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_r\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\]
Substituting this bound into the preceding estimate and using $M\ell\leq1$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \left\Vert\Delta_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
 \leq C_p|\gamma|\ell(1+\sqrt{M\ell})
       \sup_{|r|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_r\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
 \leq C_p|\gamma|\ell
       \sup_{|r|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_r\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{aligned}
 \label{eq:tap-Delta-p}
\end{equation}

We now express $A_i$ and $B_i$ in terms of $\Delta_\gamma$.
By the definition of $\Delta_\gamma$, we have
\[
 a\cdot m_\gamma=a\cdot m_0+\gamma\ell\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_0+\Delta_\gamma.
\]
We integrate $\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\gamma}\log Z_\gamma=a\cdot m_\gamma$ over $[-1,1]$ and use $\int_{-1}^1\gamma\,\mathrm d\gamma=0$ to obtain
\[
 \frac12(\log Z_{+1}-\log Z_{-1})
 =a\cdot m_0+\frac12\int_{-1}^1\Delta_\gamma\,\mathrm d\gamma.
\]
Taking the average of the two endpoint identities, we obtain
\[
 \frac12a\cdot(m_{+1}+m_{-1})
 =a\cdot m_0+\frac12(\Delta_1+\Delta_{-1}).
\]
Taking half their difference, we obtain
\[
 \frac12a\cdot(m_{+1}-m_{-1})
 =\ell\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_0+\frac12(\Delta_1-\Delta_{-1}).
\]
Substituting these identities and $\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma_0=M-\left\Vertm_0\right\Vert_2^2$ into the definitions of the two errors, we obtain
\[
A_i =\frac12\int_{-1}^1\Delta_\gamma\,\mathrm d\gamma
       -\frac12(\Delta_1+\Delta_{-1}),
\]
\[
B_i =\ell(\left\Vertm_0\right\Vert_2^2-\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2^2)
       -\frac12(\Delta_1-\Delta_{-1}).
\]
Since $A_i$ depends only on $G$ under our conditioning, we apply Minkowski's inequality and \cref{eq:tap-Delta-p} to obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\VertA_i\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
\leq \frac12\int_{-1}^1\left\Vert\Delta_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\,\mathrm d\gamma
       +\frac12\left(\left\Vert\Delta_1\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
                     +\left\Vert\Delta_{-1}\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\right)\leq C_p\ell\sup_{|\gamma|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{align*}
To estimate $B_i$, we use $\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2,\left\Vertm_0\right\Vert_2\leq\sqrt M$ to obtain the pointwise bound
\[
 |\left\Vert\overline m_x\right\Vert_2^2-\left\Vertm_0\right\Vert_2^2|
 \leq2\sqrt M\,\left\Vert\overline m_x-m_0\right\Vert_2.
\]
By convexity of the endpoint mixture, we also have
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\overline m_x-m_0\right\Vert_2
 \leq \frac{1+x}{2}\left\VertF_1\right\Vert_2
       +\frac{1-x}{2}\left\VertF_{-1}\right\Vert_2
 \leq \left\VertF_1\right\Vert_2+\left\VertF_{-1}\right\Vert_2. 
\end{align*}
These pointwise inequalities use only $|x|\leq1$, so they remain valid when $x$ depends on $G$ and additional randomness.
Since $F_{\pm1}$ and $\Delta_{\pm1}$ depend only on $G$ under our conditioning, their $L^p(\mathrm{row})$ norms equal their $L^p(G)$ norms.
Using the pointwise bounds, \Cref{lem:tap-tilted-mean-displacement}, and \cref{eq:tap-Delta-p}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\VertB_i\right\Vert_{L^p(\mathrm{row})}
 \leq&~2\ell\sqrt M\left(\left\VertF_1\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
                        +\left\VertF_{-1}\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\right)
 \mathbin{+}\frac12\left(\left\Vert\Delta_1\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}
               +\left\Vert\Delta_{-1}\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\right)\\
 \leq&~C_p\ell(1+\sqrt{M\ell})
       \sup_{|\gamma|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}\\
 \leq&~C_p\ell\sup_{|\gamma|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)},
\end{align*}
where the last inequality uses $M\ell\leq1$.
Finally, using $r_{\mathrm{row}}=A_i+xB_i$ and $|x|\leq1$, we combine the two error estimates to obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vertr_{\mathrm{row}}\right\Vert_{L^p(\mathrm{row})}
 \leq\left\VertA_i\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}+\left\VertB_i\right\Vert_{L^p(\mathrm{row})}
 \leq C_p\ell\sup_{|\gamma|\leq1}\left\Vert\Sigma_\gamma\right\Vert_{L^p(G)}.
\end{align*}
This proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- restatedlemma-2: body_md -->
Under the planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model}, set $J=\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}$.
If $n\geq2$ and $n\ell\leq\beta_*^2$, let $\mathbf r$ and
$\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$ be the residual before and after
deleting coordinate $i$.  Each $\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$ is
$\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$-measurable.  For every fixed integer $p\geq1$,
there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p,\beta_*,T$, such that,
for every $t\in[0,T]$,
\begin{equation*}
 \max_i\bigl\|\,\|\mathbf r\|_2-\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\,\bigr\|_{L^{4p}}\le C_pn^{-1/4}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end restatedlemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-residual-sde}
Fix a symmetric matrix $J\in\mathbb R^{M\times M}$, $\ell\geq0$, and
an initial external field $y\in\mathbb R^M$.  Let $\mu_{J,y}$ be the Gibbs {distribution} with
interaction $J$ and external field $y$, with mean ${m_J}(y)$ and covariance $\Sigma(y)$,
and let $\mathbf r$ be defined by \cref{eq:tap-M-residual}.
Sample $\xi\sim\mu_{J,y}$ and independently a standard Brownian motion
$(\widetilde B_s)_{s\geq0}$ in $\mathbb R^M$, starting from zero.
Set $y_s=y+s\xi+\widetilde B_s$ and
$\mathcal F_s=\sigma((y_u)_{0\leq u\leq s})$.
Then $\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}
=y_s-y-\int_0^s {m_J}(y_u)\mathrm d u$ is Brownian motion in this filtration, and
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d\mathbf r(y_s)=\left.\frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}\right|_{v=y_s}\mathrm d\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}
                    +\mathbf b(y_s)\mathrm d s.
\end{equation*}
For every external field $v\in\mathbb R^M$, with
$D(v)=\operatorname{diag}((1-{m_{J,j}}(v)^2)^{-1})$, the coefficients are
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}=&~(D(v)-J+\ell(M-\left\Vert{m_J}(v)\right\Vert_2^2)I_M
                   -2\ell {m_J}(v){m_J}(v)^\top)\Sigma(v)-I_M,\\
 \mathbf b(v)=&~{m_J}(v)\mathbin{\odot}
   (\operatorname{diag}(D(v)\Sigma(v)^2D(v))-\1) -\ell\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma(v)^2){m_J}(v)-2\ell \Sigma(v)^2{m_J}(v).
\end{align*}
Here $\odot$ denotes coordinatewise multiplication and
$\operatorname{diag}$ applied to a matrix denotes its diagonal vector.
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
We apply \Cref{lem:app-wpi-observation-law} in dimension $M$ with base distribution $\mu=\mu_{J,y}$ and additional observation $y_s-y=s\xi+\widetilde B_s$.
The tilt of this base distribution by a field $v$ is $\mu_{J,y+v}$, so its mean and covariance at $v=y_s-y$ are ${m_J}(y_s)$ and $\Sigma(y_s)$.
Thus the innovation process is precisely $\widetilde B^{\mathrm{loc}}$ from the statement.
We set $m_s={m_J}(y_s)$, $\Sigma_s=\Sigma(y_s)$, and $D_s=\operatorname{diag}((1-m_{s,j}^2)^{-1})$.
By the field SDE in \Cref{lem:app-wpi-observation-law}, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d y_s=m_s\,\mathrm d s+\mathrm d\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}.
\end{equation*}
By the mean SDE in the same lemma, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d m_s=\Sigma_s\,\mathrm d\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}.
\end{equation*}
Multiplying the Brownian coefficients, we obtain the quadratic covariation
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d[m_j,m_k]_s=(\Sigma_s^2)_{jk}\,\mathrm d s.
\end{equation*}
The mean ${m_J}(v)$ is a ratio of finite sums of exponentials with positive denominator, so it is smooth in $v$; full support of the Gibbs distribution gives ${m_J}(v)\in(-1,1)^M$ for every $v$.
We can therefore apply It\^o's formula to the three terms of the residual along the continuous field path.

We now apply these identities to the residual
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbf r(y_s)=\operatorname{arctanh}\left(m_s\right)
                 -(y_s+Jm_s)+\ell(M-\left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2)m_s.
\end{equation*}
We use $(\operatorname{arctanh})'(v)=(1-v^2)^{-1}$ and
$(\operatorname{arctanh})''(v)=2v(1-v^2)^{-2}$ in It\^o's formula to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d \operatorname{arctanh}\left(m_{s,j}\right)
 =\frac{\mathrm d m_{s,j}}{1-m_{s,j}^2}
   +\frac{m_{s,j}(\Sigma_s^2)_{jj}}{(1-m_{s,j}^2)^2}\mathrm d s.
\end{equation*}
We write the resulting vector SDE as
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d \operatorname{arctanh}\left(m_s\right)
 =D_s\Sigma_s\mathrm d \widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}
   +m_s\mathbin{\odot}\operatorname{diag}(D_s\Sigma_s^2D_s)\mathrm d s.
\end{equation*}
For $-y_s-Jm_s$, we substitute the field and mean SDEs:
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d (-y_s-Jm_s)=-\mathrm d y_s-J\mathrm d m_s
 =-(I_M+J\Sigma_s)\mathrm d \widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}-m_s\mathrm d s.
\end{equation*}
For $\ell(M-\left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2)m_s$, we first compute
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d \left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2
 =2m_s^\top \Sigma_s\mathrm d \widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}
   +\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma_s^2)\mathrm d s.
\end{equation*}
The Brownian coefficient of $\left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2$ is $2m_s^\top \Sigma_s$.
We multiply it by the Brownian coefficient of $m_{s,j}$ to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d[\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2^2,m_j]_s
 =2\sum_k(\Sigma_sm_s)_k(\Sigma_s)_{jk}\mathrm d s
 =2(\Sigma_s^2m_s)_j\mathrm d s.
\end{equation*}
We substitute these two identities in It\^o's product formula:
\begin{align*}
 \mathrm d (\ell(M-\left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2)m_s)
 =&~\ell(M-\left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2)\mathrm d m_s
    -\ell m_s\mathrm d \left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2-\ell\mathrm d [\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2^2,m]_s\\
 =&~(\ell(M-\left\Vertm_s\right\Vert_2^2)I_M-2\ell m_sm_s^\top)\Sigma_s
            \mathrm d \widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}} -\ell(\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma_s^2)m_s+2\Sigma_s^2m_s)\mathrm d s.
\end{align*}
Adding the three drift coefficients, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 m_s\mathbin{\odot}\operatorname{diag}(D_s\Sigma_s^2D_s)-m_s
 -\ell\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma_s^2)m_s-2\ell \Sigma_s^2m_s=\mathbf b(y_s).
\end{equation*}
For the Brownian coefficient, we differentiate the residual in $y$
using $\partial {m_J}(y)/\partial y=\Sigma(y)$ and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{\partial\mathbf r(y)}{\partial y}
 =D(y)\Sigma(y)-I_M-J\Sigma(y)
   +(\ell(M-\left\Vert{m_J}(y)\right\Vert_2^2)I_M-2\ell {m_J}(y){m_J}(y)^\top)\Sigma(y),
\end{equation*}
and $\mathrm d\mathbf r(y_s)=(\partial\mathbf r/\partial y)(y_s)
\mathrm d\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}+\mathbf b(y_s)\mathrm d s$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-29: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-influence}
The following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item {Let $\mu$ be a probability distribution on $\{\pm1\}^n$ with mean $m$ satisfying $|m_j|<1$ for every $j$, and define the covariance $\Sigma$, and $D=\operatorname{diag}((1-m_j^2)^{-1})$.}
For each $i$, define the influence $\delta^{(i)}=(\delta_j^{(i)})_{j\ne i}$ by
\begin{equation*}
 \delta_j^{(i)}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sigma_j=1\mid\sigma_i=1\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_j=1\mid\sigma_i=1\right]}
 -\ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sigma_j=1\mid\sigma_i=-1\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_j=1\mid\sigma_i=-1\right]}.
\end{equation*}
Then we have that $(D\Sigma^2D)_{ii}-1=\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^2$ for every $i$.
\item Under the planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model}, set $J=\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}$.
If $n\ell\leq\beta_*^2$ and $t\geq0$, then for every fixed $p\geq2$
there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p,\beta_*$, such that
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_i\|\delta^{(i)}\|_{L^p}\leq C_p,
 \qquad \sup_i\|\Sigma_{i,\cdot}\|_{L^p}\leq C_p.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-29 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
For part~\textup{(i)}, a function of a binary spin is affine in that
spin, so
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sigma_j\mid\sigma_i\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\sim\mu}\left[\sigma_j\mid\sigma_i\right]}-m_j
 =\delta_j^{(i)}(\sigma_i-m_i)\qquad(j\ne i).
\end{equation*}
We multiply by $\sigma_i-m_i$ and take expectation to obtain
$\Sigma_{ij}=(1-m_i^2)\delta_j^{(i)}$.
Since $\Sigma_{ii}=1-m_i^2$ and $\Sigma$ is symmetric, we have
\begin{equation*}
 (D\Sigma^2D)_{ii}
 =\frac{\sum_j\Sigma_{ij}^2}{(1-m_i^2)^2}
 =1+\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^2.
\end{equation*}

{For part~\textup{(ii)}, we have that $|m_j|<1$ for every $j$ and the coefficients in part~\textup{(i)} are defined.}
For $n=1$, the influence vector is empty and $|\Sigma_{11}|=1-m_1^2\leq1$, so both bounds hold.
We now assume $n\geq2$.
For the pinned means of \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning}, we have $\delta^{(i)}=(m_{+1}-m_{-1})/2$ and $m_0=m^{\setminus i}$.
By the triangle inequality, we obtain
\[\|\delta^{(i)}\|_{L^p}
 \leq\tfrac12\bigl(\|m_{+1}-m^{\setminus i}\|_{L^p}+\|m_{-1}-m^{\setminus i}\|_{L^p}\bigr).\]
We use the cavity interpolation to bound both mean differences.
{
We fix $i$ and set $M=n-1$, $U=X_iX_{-i}$, and $G=G_i$.
We condition on $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$.
Then $m_0$ is fixed, and each $m_b$ depends on the remaining randomness only through $G$.
Here $L^p(G)$ averages only over $G$ with $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U)$ fixed, whereas the unsubscripted $L^p$ norm below averages over the full planted law.
Since $M\ell\leq n\ell\leq\beta_*^2<1$, we have that }
\begingroup

\begin{align*}
 \left(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}\right)^{1/p}
 =&~\|m_b-m_0\|_{L^p(G)}\\
 (\text{by \Cref{lem:tap-tilted-mean-displacement}})\quad\leq&~C_p\sqrt\ell\sup_{|r|\leq1}\|\Sigma_r\|_{L^p(G)}\\
 (\text{by \Cref{lem:tap-weak-tilt}})\quad\leq&~C_p\sqrt\ell\,\mathrm e^{40\ell M/p}
 \left\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\right\rangle_{(\mu^{\setminus i})^{\otimes4}}^{1/p}.
\end{align*}
\endgroup
\begingroup

Next, we take expectations over $\+F^{\setminus i}$ and $U$,
\begin{align*}
 \|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_{L^p}^p
 =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid\mathcal F^{\setminus i},U\right]}\right]}\\
 \leq&~C_p^p\ell^{p/2}\mathrm e^{40\ell M}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\right\rangle_{(\mu^{\setminus i})^{\otimes4}}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left\langle\mathcal D_{1234}^{p/2}\right\rangle_{(\mu^{\setminus i})^{\otimes4}}\right]}\\
 (\text{by \Cref{cor:tap-covariance}-\textup{(i)}})\quad\leq&~C_p^p\ell^{p/2}\mathrm e^{40\ell M}(K\sqrt{Mp})^p
 \leq\bigl(C_p\sqrt{\ell M}\bigr)^p.
\end{align*}
\endgroup
{In the last inequality, we absorb $\mathrm e^{40\ell M}$ and the fixed $p$-dependent factors into $C_p^p$.  Taking $p$th roots and substituting the resulting bound for $b=1,-1$ into the triangle inequality above, we obtain that}
\begingroup

\begin{equation*}
 \|\delta^{(i)}\|_{L^p}\leq C_p\sqrt{\ell M}\leq C_p.
\end{equation*}
\endgroup
Finally, the covariance identity from part~\textup{(i)} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \|\Sigma_{i,\cdot}\|_2
 =(1-m_i^2)\sqrt{1+\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^2}
 \leq1+\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2.\qedhere
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-30: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-residual-dynamics}
{Consider the $M$-spin planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model} at inverse temperature $\beta_M=\sqrt{M\ell}$, with $J=\beta_MA^{\mathrm{full}}$ and $y=Y_t$.}
{Given $(J,y)$, draw $\xi\sim\mu_{J,y}$.  Independently of $(J,y,\xi)$, draw a standard Brownian motion $(\widetilde B_s)_{s\geq0}$ in $\mathbb R^M$ starting from zero, and set} ${y_s=y+s\xi+\widetilde B_s}$.

{We use $\mathbf r$, $\mathbf b$, and $\Sigma$ from \Cref{lem:tap-residual-sde} with dimension $M$, interaction matrix $J$, and the same $\ell$.}
{Suppose that $M\ell\leq\beta_*^2$.}
There is a constant $K>0$, depending only on $\beta_*$, such that, for every fixed $p\geq2$, there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p,\beta_*$, such that, for all $t,s\geq0$,
\begin{equation*}
 \|\mathbf r(y_s)\|_{L^p}\le C_p,\qquad
 \left\Vert\Sigma(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}\le K\sqrt{Mp},\qquad
 \|\mathbf b(y_s)\|_{L^p}\le C_p\sqrt M{.}
\end{equation*}
Here, $L^p$ norms involving this path average jointly over $(J,y,\xi,\widetilde B)$.
<!-- end lemma-30 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
We fix $t,s\geq0$.
By the posterior identity, $(J,y,\xi)$ and $(J,y,X)$ have the same joint law.
In the representation below, $g\sim N(0,I_M)$ is independent of $X$, the interaction noises, and the Brownian motion $(\widetilde B_s)_{s\geq0}$.
Since $\widetilde B$ is independent, we have
\begin{equation}
 (J,y_s)\overset{\mathrm d}{=}
 (J,(t+s)X+\sqrt t\,g+\widetilde B_s).
 \label{eq:tap-path-planted-marginal}
\end{equation}
{Given $X$, the noise on the right has law $N(0,(t+s)I_M)$ and is independent of the interaction noises.}
Thus we can apply the planted-model estimates at time $t+s$.

By \Cref{lem:tap-complete-row} at dimension $M$ and time $t+s$,
we have $\left\Vertr_i(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}\leq C_pM^{-1/2}$.
Since $p/2\geq1$, we apply Minkowski's inequality in $L^{p/2}$:
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\mathbf r(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}^2
 =\left\|\sum_{i=1}^M r_i(y_s)^2\right\|_{L^{p/2}}
 \leq\sum_{i=1}^M\left\Vertr_i(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}^2
 \leq M(C_pM^{-1/2})^2=C_p^2.
\end{equation*}
For the covariance matrix $\Sigma(y_s)$, we use $\left\Vert\Sigma(y_s)\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^2=\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma(y_s)^2)$ and apply \Cref{cor:tap-covariance}-\textup{(ii)} at moment order $p/2$.
After increasing $K$ if necessary, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\Sigma(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}
 =\bigl\|\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma(y_s)^2)\bigr\|_{L^{p/2}}^{1/2}
 \leq K\sqrt{Mp}.
\end{equation*}

For the drift $\mathbf b(y_s)$, we write $m={m_J}(y_s)$, $\Sigma=\Sigma(y_s)$,
and $D=\operatorname{diag}((1-m_i^2)^{-1})$.
{For each $i$, we use the coefficients $\delta^{(i)}=(m_{+1}-m_{-1})/2$ from \Cref{lem:tap-influence}, where $m_{\pm1}$ are the pinned means obtained by deleting coordinate $i$ from the model with interaction $J$ and external field $y_s$.}
Substituting $(D\Sigma^2D)_{ii}-1=\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^2$
into the drift formula in \Cref{lem:tap-residual-sde}, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 b_i(y_s)
 =m_i\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^2
   -\ell\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2)m_i-2\ell(\Sigma^2m)_i.
\end{equation*}
We next bound the contribution of $m_i\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^2$.
Since $|m_i|\leq1$, by Minkowski's inequality in $L^{p/2}$ and
\Cref{lem:tap-influence}-\textup{(ii)}, we have
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^M m_i^2\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^4\right)^{p/2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^M m_i^2\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^4\right)^{p/2}\right]}^{2/p}
 = \left\|\sum_{i=1}^M m_i^2\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^4
                                      \right\|_{L^{p/2}}
 \leq \sum_{i=1}^M
        \bigl\|m_i^2\|\delta^{(i)}\|_2^4\bigr\|_{L^{p/2}}
 \leq \sum_{i=1}^M\|\delta^{(i)}\|_{L^{2p}}^4
 \leq M C_{2p}^4.
\end{align*}
Taking square roots bounds this part by $C_p\sqrt M$.

Then, we bound $\ell\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2)m$ and $2\ell \Sigma^2m$.
Since $\Sigma$ is symmetric, $\Sigma^2\succeq0$ and
$\left\Vert\Sigma^2\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2)$.
By $\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2\leq\sqrt M$ and
$\left\Vert\Sigma^2m\right\Vert_2\leq\left\Vert\Sigma^2\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2$, we have
\begin{align*}
 \ell\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2)\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2+2\ell\left\Vert\Sigma^2m\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\ell\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2)\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2
       +2\ell\left\Vert\Sigma^2\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\left\Vertm\right\Vert_2
 \leq 3\ell\sqrt M\,\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2).
\end{align*}
By \Cref{cor:tap-covariance}-\textup{(ii)}, we have
$\left\Vert\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2)\right\Vert_{L^p}\leq KMp$.
We apply the triangle inequality to the three drift terms and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\mathbf b(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq C_p\sqrt M+3\ell\sqrt M\,\left\Vert\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma^2)\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq C_p\sqrt M+3Kp(\ell M)\sqrt M
 \leq C'_p\sqrt M,
\end{equation*}
where we used $\ell M\leq\beta_*^2<1$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-31: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-susceptibility}
Let $(J,y)$ follow the $M$-spin planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model} at inverse temperature $\sqrt{M\ell}$, with $J=\sqrt{M\ell}A^{\mathrm{full}}$ and $y=Y_t$.
Suppose that $M\ell\leq\beta_*^2$ and $t\geq0$.
{Here $n=M$ specifies the dimension in that definition, and $\mathbf r$ is the residual in \cref{eq:tap-M-residual} for the resulting $M\times M$ matrix $J$.}
For every fixed $p\geq2$, there is a constant $C_p$,
depending only on $p,\beta_*$, such that
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial\mathbf r(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}\leq C_pM^{1/4}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-31 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
We use the path $y_s$ from \Cref{lem:tap-residual-dynamics}.
For a function $F$ of the external field and $J$, we set
$(P_sF)(y)=\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[F(y_s)\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[F(y_s)\mid J,y\right]}$.
Integrating the SDE in \Cref{lem:tap-residual-sde} to time $0<h\leq1$
and taking expectation given $(J,y)$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
 \mathbf r(y)=(P_h\mathbf r)(y)-\int_0^h(P_s\mathbf b)(y)\,\-d s.
 \label{eq:tap-smoothing-residual-identity}
\end{equation}
We will differentiate these two averages to bound the residual Jacobian;
the required integrability and differentiation justifications are given below.

We claim that, if $F$ is measurable and bounded as a function of the external field
for each fixed $J$, and $\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^{2p}}$ is finite, then
\begin{equation}
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_sF)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq s^{-1/2}\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}
    +\bigl\|\,\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\bigr\|_{L^p}^{1/2}
       \left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^{2p}}.
 \label{eq:tap-smoothing-gradient}
\end{equation}

Assuming \cref{eq:tap-smoothing-gradient}, we first bound the residual Jacobian.
For $0<s\leq h$, we apply \Cref{lem:tap-residual-dynamics} at time $t+s$.
Using $\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm F}$, we substitute its bounds at orders $p$ and $2p$ and obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_h\mathbf r)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq&~C_p h^{-1/2}
       +(K\sqrt{Mp})^{1/2}C_{2p}
 \leq C_p(h^{-1/2}+M^{1/4}),\\
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_s\mathbf b)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq&~C_p\sqrt M\,s^{-1/2}
       +(K\sqrt{Mp})^{1/2}C_{2p}\sqrt M
 \leq C_p\sqrt M(s^{-1/2}+M^{1/4}).
\end{align*}
We differentiate \cref{eq:tap-smoothing-residual-identity}, take
$L^p$ norms, and apply Minkowski to the integral:
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial\mathbf r(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq&~\left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_h\mathbf r)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}
       +\int_0^h
         \left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_s\mathbf b)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}\,\-d s
 \leq C_p(h^{-1/2}+M^{1/4}
                +\sqrt M(2\sqrt h+hM^{1/4})).
\end{align*}
We choose $h=M^{-1/2}\leq1$.  Then
$h^{-1/2}=\sqrt M\sqrt h=\sqrt M hM^{1/4}=M^{1/4}$,
so the right side is at most $C_pM^{1/4}$.

It remains to prove \cref{eq:tap-smoothing-gradient} and justify its
application to $\mathbf r$ and $\mathbf b$.
We fix $J$ and $s>0$, and let $p_s(y,y')$ be the conditional
density of $y_s$ evaluated at $y'\in\mathbb R^M$.
We write the conditional average as
\begin{equation*}
 (P_sF)(y)=\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[F(y_s)\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[F(y_s)\mid J,y\right]}
          =\int_{\mathbb R^M}F(y')p_s(y,y')\,\-d y'.
\end{equation*}
When differentiating in the initial field $y$, we hold $J,s,y'$ fixed,
so $F(y')$ does not change.  We compute the density and its derivative
before justifying the exchange with the integral.  We set
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal Z_J(y)=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm1\}^M}
      \exp(\langle \sigma,J\sigma\rangle/2+\langle y,\sigma\rangle).
\end{equation*}
{Given $(J,y,\xi=\sigma)$, we have $y_s\sim N(y+s\sigma,sI_M)$.}  We average these Gaussian densities
with the Gibbs probabilities of $\sigma$ to obtain
\begin{equation} \label{eq:tap-smoothing-kernel}
\begin{aligned}
 p_s(y,y')
 =&~\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm1\}^M}
 \frac{\exp(\langle \sigma,J\sigma\rangle/2+\langle y,\sigma\rangle)}{\mathcal Z_J(y)}
 (2\pi s)^{-M/2}
       \exp\left(-\frac{\left\Verty'-y-s\sigma\right\Vert_2^2}{2s}\right)\\
 =&~(2\pi s)^{-M/2}
       \exp\left(-\frac{\left\Verty'-y\right\Vert_2^2}{2s}-\frac{Ms}{2}\right)
       \frac{\mathcal Z_J(y')}{\mathcal Z_J(y)},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the second equality uses $\left\Vert\sigma\right\Vert_2^2=M$ and $\langle y,\sigma\rangle+\langle y'-y,\sigma\rangle=\langle y',\sigma\rangle$.

We differentiate in $y$, using $\nabla_y\log\mathcal Z_J(y)={m_J}(y)$:
\begin{equation*}
 \nabla_y\log p_s(y,y')
 =-\nabla_y\frac{\|y'-y\|_2^2}{2s}-\nabla_y\log\mathcal Z_J(y)
 =\frac{y'-y}{s}-{m_J}(y).
\end{equation*}
Multiplying by $p_s(y,y')$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \nabla_yp_s(y,y')=p_s(y,y')
                 \left(\frac{y'-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right).
\end{equation*}

Then, we have
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\partial(P_sF)(y)}{\partial y}
 =&~\int_{\mathbb R^M}F(y')\bigl(\nabla_yp_s(y,y')\bigr)^\top\,\-d y'\\
 =&~\int_{\mathbb R^M}F(y')
        \left(\frac{y'-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right)^\top p_s(y,y')\,\-d y'\\
 =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[F(y_s)\left(\frac{y_s-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right)^\top\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[F(y_s)\left(\frac{y_s-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right)^\top\mid J,y\right]}.
\end{align*}
{We will apply Cauchy--Schwarz with $(J,y)$ fixed, taking $U=F(y_s)$ and $V=(y_s-y)/s-{m_J}(y)$.}
For any square-integrable $U,V$, with expectations taken under
their joint law, its rowwise form is
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[U_jV\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[U_jV\right]}\right\Vert_2^2
 =&~\sup_{\left\Vertv\right\Vert_2=1}\left\vert\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[U_j\langle v,V\rangle\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[U_j\langle v,V\rangle\right]}\right\vert^2
 \leq \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[U_j^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[U_j^2\right]}\sup_{\left\Vertv\right\Vert_2=1}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle v,V\rangle^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\langle v,V\rangle^2\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[U_j^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[U_j^2\right]}\left\Vert\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[VV^\top\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[VV^\top\right]}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}.
\end{align*}
Summing over $j$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[UV^\top\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[UV^\top\right]}\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^2
 \leq\left\Vert\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[VV^\top\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[VV^\top\right]}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\VertU\right\Vert_2^2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left\VertU\right\Vert_2^2\right]}.
\end{equation*}
Under the conditional law given $(J,y)$, we have $V=\xi-{m_J}(y)+\widetilde B_s/s$.
By the definitions of ${m_J}(y)$ and $\Sigma(y)$, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi-{m_J}(y)\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\xi-{m_J}(y)\mid J,y\right]}=0,\qquad
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\xi-{m_J}(y))(\xi-{m_J}(y))^\top\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(\xi-{m_J}(y))(\xi-{m_J}(y))^\top\mid J,y\right]}=\Sigma(y).
\end{equation*}
Under the same fixed $(J,y)$, we use $\widetilde B_s\sim N(0,sI_M)$ to compute
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widetilde B_s/s\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\widetilde B_s/s\mid J,y\right]}=0,\qquad
 s^{-2}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widetilde B_s\widetilde B_s^\top\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\widetilde B_s\widetilde B_s^\top\mid J,y\right]}
 =s^{-2}sI_M=s^{-1}I_M.
\end{equation*}
By independence of $\xi$ and $\widetilde B_s$ given $(J,y)$,
we compute the cross term as
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\xi-{m_J}(y))(\widetilde B_s/s)^\top\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(\xi-{m_J}(y))(\widetilde B_s/s)^\top\mid J,y\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi-{m_J}(y)\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\xi-{m_J}(y)\mid J,y\right]}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widetilde B_s/s\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\widetilde B_s/s\mid J,y\right]}^\top=0.
\end{equation*}
The other cross term is its transpose and also vanishes.
Expanding the product, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 &~\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\frac{y_s-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right)
       \left(\frac{y_s-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right)^\top\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left(\frac{y_s-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right)
       \left(\frac{y_s-y}{s}-{m_J}(y)\right)^\top\mid J,y\right]}\\
 =&~\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\xi-{m_J}(y))(\xi-{m_J}(y))^\top\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(\xi-{m_J}(y))(\xi-{m_J}(y))^\top\mid J,y\right]}
   +s^{-2}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widetilde B_s\widetilde B_s^\top\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\widetilde B_s\widetilde B_s^\top\mid J,y\right]}
 =\Sigma(y)+s^{-1}I_M.
\end{align*}
Substituting into the Cauchy--Schwarz bound, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_sF)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}
 \leq&~\left\Vert\Sigma(y)+s^{-1}I_M\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2}
       \bigl(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_2^2\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_2^2\mid J,y\right]}\bigr)^{1/2}\\
 \leq&~(s^{-1/2}+\left\Vert\Sigma(y)\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2})
       \bigl(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_2^2\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_2^2\mid J,y\right]}\bigr)^{1/2}.
\end{align*}
For any $q\geq2$, we fix $(J,y)$ and apply Jensen's inequality with exponent $q/2$:
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bigl(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^2\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^2\mid J,y\right]}\bigr)^{q/2}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\bigl(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^2\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^2\mid J,y\right]}\bigr)^{q/2}\right]}
 \leq\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^q\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^q\mid J,y\right]}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^q\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^q\mid J,y\right]}\right]}
 =\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^q\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|F(y_s)\|_2^q\right]}.
\end{equation*}
Taking $q$th roots, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\|\bigl(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_2^2\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_2^2\mid J,y\right]}\bigr)^{1/2}\right\|_{L^q}
 \leq\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^q}.
\end{equation*}
We now take $L^p$ norms over $(J,y)$.  We use this inequality with $q=p$
for the term with coefficient $s^{-1/2}$, and Hölder with exponents
$2,2$, followed by the same inequality with $q=2p$, for the other term.
We obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_sF)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq s^{-1/2}\left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^p}
    +\bigl\|\,\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\bigr\|_{L^p}^{1/2}
       \left\VertF(y_s)\right\Vert_{L^{2p}},
\end{equation*}
which proves \cref{eq:tap-smoothing-gradient}.

We now justify the steps deferred above.
We first establish the estimates needed for these steps.
We fix $J,M$.  Each probability $\mu_J(y)(\sigma)$
is a ratio of finite sums of exponentials in $y$, with a strictly
positive denominator.  Thus ${m_{J,j}}(y)=\sum_\sigma\sigma_j\mu_J(y)(\sigma)$
and $\Sigma_{jk}(y)=\sum_\sigma\sigma_j\sigma_k\mu_J(y)(\sigma)-{m_{J,j}}(y){m_{J,k}}(y)$
are infinitely differentiable in $y$.  

We next bound $\operatorname{arctanh}\left({m_{J,j}}(y)\right)-y_j$.
We write $\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})$ for the Gibbs distribution on
$\{\pm1\}^{M-1}$ with interaction $J^{\setminus j}$ and external field $y_{-j}$.
{Here we delete $j$ from the $M$-spin system of this lemma, so $J^{\setminus j}$ has size $(M-1)\times(M-1)$.}
By summing the Gibbs weights separately over $\sigma_j=1$ and
$\sigma_j=-1$, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{1+{m_{J,j}}(y)}{1-{m_{J,j}}(y)}
 =\frac{\mu_J(y)\{\sigma_j=1\}}{\mu_J(y)\{\sigma_j=-1\}}
 =\mathrm e^{2y_j}
 \frac{\ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}\left[\mathrm e^{\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}}
      {\ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{-\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}\left[\mathrm e^{-\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}}.
\end{equation*}
Taking half the logarithm, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{arctanh}\left({m_{J,j}}(y)\right)-y_j
 =\frac12\log
 \frac{\ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}\left[\mathrm e^{\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}}
      {\ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{-\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{J^{\setminus j}}(y_{-j})}\left[\mathrm e^{-\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k}\right]}}.
\end{equation*}
Since $|\sum_{k\ne j}J_{jk}\sigma_k|\leq\sum_{k\ne j}|J_{jk}|$,
both expectations in this ratio lie between
$\mathrm e^{-\sum_{k\ne j}|J_{jk}|}$ and
$\mathrm e^{\sum_{k\ne j}|J_{jk}|}$.  Hence
$\left\vert\operatorname{arctanh}\left({m_{J,j}}(y)\right)-y_j\right\vert\leq\sum_{k\ne j}|J_{jk}|$.
We recall that $D(y)=\operatorname{diag}((1-{m_{J,j}}(y)^2)^{-1})_{j=1}^M$.
Also, $|\Sigma_{jk}|\leq1$ by Cauchy--Schwarz and the unit variance bound
for spins.  As proved in \Cref{lem:tap-influence},
$\Sigma_{jk}=(1-m_j^2)\delta_k^{(j)}$ for $k\ne j$.
Since $\delta_k^{(j)}$ is a difference of two probabilities,
$|\delta_k^{(j)}|\leq1$.  Therefore
$|(D\Sigma)_{jk}|\leq1$, also on the diagonal where $(D\Sigma)_{jj}=1$.
We now bound $\mathbf r(y)$, its Jacobian, and $\mathbf b(y)$ independently
of $y$.  By the bound on $\operatorname{arctanh}\left({m_{J,j}}(y)\right)-y_j$
and Cauchy--Schwarz in each row of $J$, we have
$\|\operatorname{arctanh}({m_J}(y))-y\|_2^2
\leq\sum_j(\sum_{k\ne j}|J_{jk}|)^2\leq M\|J\|_{\mathrm F}^2$.
Also $\|{m_J}(y)\|_2\leq\sqrt M$, $\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm F}\leq M$,
and $\|D(y)\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm F}\leq M$.
By the formulas for $\mathbf r$, its Jacobian, and $\mathbf b$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\mathbf r(y)\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\sqrt M(\left\VertJ\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}+\left\VertJ\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}+\ell M),\\
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial\mathbf r(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}
 \leq&~\|D(y)\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm F}+\|J\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm F}
       +3\ell M\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm F}+\sqrt M\\
 \leq&~M(1+\left\VertJ\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}})+3\ell M^2+\sqrt M,\\
 \left\Vert\mathbf b(y)\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\sqrt M(M+1)+3\ell M^2\sqrt M.
\end{align*}
To verify the bound on $\mathbf b(y)$, we use
$(D(y)\Sigma(y)^2D(y))_{jj}=\sum_k(D(y)\Sigma(y))_{jk}^2\leq M$
and $\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma(y)^2)=\sum_{j,k}\Sigma_{jk}(y)^2\leq M^2$.
The three terms in its formula in \Cref{lem:tap-residual-sde} satisfy
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\bigl({m_{J,j}}(y)((D(y)\Sigma(y)^2D(y))_{jj}-1)\bigr)_{j=1}^M\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\sqrt M(M+1),\\
 \left\Vert\ell\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma(y)^2){m_J}(y)\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\ell M^2\sqrt M,\\
 \left\Vert2\ell \Sigma(y)^2{m_J}(y)\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~2\ell\left\Vert\Sigma(y)\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}^2\sqrt M
 \leq2\ell M^2\sqrt M.
\end{align*}
We now justify differentiation under the integral over $y'$.
We fix $J,s>0$ and restrict $\|y\|_2\leq R$.
Every Gaussian center in \cref{eq:tap-smoothing-kernel} has norm
at most $R+s\sqrt M$.  Using
$\|y'-z\|_2^2\geq\|y'\|_2^2/2-\|z\|_2^2$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 p_s(y,y')\leq A_{s,R}\mathrm e^{-\|y'\|_2^2/(4s)},\qquad
 \|\nabla_y p_s(y,y')\|_2
 \leq A_{s,R}\left(\frac{\|y'\|_2+R}{s}+\sqrt M\right)
                  \mathrm e^{-\|y'\|_2^2/(4s)},
\end{equation*}
where $A_{s,R}=(2\pi s)^{-M/2}
\exp((R+s\sqrt M)^2/(2s))$.
Both bounds are integrable in $y'$.  Multiplying by the fixed-$J$
bound on $F$ provides a common integrable bound for the integrand and
its derivatives.  Differentiation under the integral is therefore valid.
The bounds on $\mathbf r$ and $\mathbf b$ above verify this condition
for both functions used in the proof.

We next justify the three operations in
\cref{eq:tap-smoothing-residual-identity} and its derivative.
In the following conditional expectations, we fix $(J,y)$ and average
over $\xi\sim\mu_{J,y}$ and the independent Brownian motion generating $y_s$.
First, the Jacobian bound ensures
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_0^h
 \left\Vert\left.\frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}\right|_{v=y_s}\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^2
 \,\-d s\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\int_0^h
 \left\Vert\left.\frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}\right|_{v=y_s}\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^2
 \,\-d s\mid J,y\right]}
 \leq h\sup_v\left\Vert\frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}\right\Vert_{\mathrm F}^2
 <\infty.
\end{equation*}
For fixed $(J,y)$, the matrix
$\left.\frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}\right|_{v=y_s}$
is a deterministic function of the current external field $y_s$.  It is therefore
measurable with respect to $\sigma(y_u:0\leq u\leq s)$, the filtration
in which $\widetilde B^{\mathrm{loc}}$ is Brownian.
The integrand is continuous and adapted.  Together with square
integrability, this makes the It\^o integral a martingale, so
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_0^h
 \left.\frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}\right|_{v=y_s}
 \,\-d \widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\int_0^h
 \left.\frac{\partial\mathbf r(v)}{\partial v}\right|_{v=y_s}
 \,\-d \widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}\mid J,y\right]}=0.
\end{equation*}

Second, the bound on $\mathbf b$ ensures absolute integrability:
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_0^h\left\Vert\mathbf b(y_s)\right\Vert_2\,\-d s\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\int_0^h\left\Vert\mathbf b(y_s)\right\Vert_2\,\-d s\mid J,y\right]}
 \leq h\sup_v\left\Vert\mathbf b(v)\right\Vert_2<\infty.
\end{equation*}
By Fubini's theorem, applied coordinatewise with $(J,y)$ fixed, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_0^h\mathbf b(y_s)\,\-d s\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\int_0^h\mathbf b(y_s)\,\-d s\mid J,y\right]}
 =\int_0^h\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf b(y_s)\mid J,y\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\mathbf b(y_s)\mid J,y\right]}\,\-d s.
\end{equation*}
Taking conditional expectation in the integrated SDE and using these
two identities proves \cref{eq:tap-smoothing-residual-identity}.

Third, we justify differentiating the time integral with respect to the
initial external field $y$.  We fix $J,M$.  For every $s>0$,
the preceding domination argument proves that $P_s\mathbf b$ is
differentiable.
We apply the previously computed derivative estimate
with $F=\mathbf b$.  Since
$\|\Sigma(y)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\operatorname{Tr}\Sigma(y)\leq M$, we have
\begin{equation*}
 \sup_y\left\|\frac{\partial(P_s\mathbf b)(y)}{\partial y}\right\|_{\mathrm F}
 \leq(s^{-1/2}+\sqrt M)\sup_v\|\mathbf b(v)\|_2.
\end{equation*}
The right side is integrable in $s$, because
$\sup_v\|\mathbf b(v)\|_2<\infty$ and
$\int_0^h(s^{-1/2}+\sqrt M)\,\-d s=2\sqrt h+h\sqrt M$.
Also $\|(P_s\mathbf b)(y)\|_2\leq\sup_v\|\mathbf b(v)\|_2$,
so the function itself is integrable in $s$.
The endpoints $0,h$ do not depend on $y$.
By the mean-value formula, every coordinate difference quotient has
this same integrable bound.  We apply dominated convergence and obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\int_0^h(P_s\mathbf b)(y)\,\-d s
 =\int_0^h\frac{\partial(P_s\mathbf b)(y)}{\partial y}\,\-d s.
\end{equation*}
The differentiation identity above holds for fixed $J,y$.
For the subsequent $L^p$ estimate over random $(J,y)$, we separately use
the moment bounds already obtained from
\Cref{lem:tap-residual-dynamics}:
\begin{equation*}
 \int_0^h
 \left\Vert\frac{\partial(P_s\mathbf b)(y)}{\partial y}\right\Vert_{L^p}\,\-d s
 \leq C_p\sqrt M(2\sqrt h+hM^{1/4})<\infty,
\end{equation*}
which justifies Minkowski's inequality for the time integral under the
joint law of $(J,y)$.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-32: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-recombination-errors}
Under the planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model}, set $J=\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}$.
If $n\geq2$, $n\ell\leq\beta_*^2$, and $t\in[0,T]$, then for every fixed $p\geq2$
there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p,\beta_*,T$, such that
for every deleted coordinate $i$,
\begin{equation*}
 \|\Delta_{\mathrm{ent}}\|_{L^p}+\|\Delta_Q\|_{L^p}
 \leq C_pn^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-32 -->
<!-- lemma-33: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-mean-increments}
Under the planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model}, set $J=\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}$.
If $n\geq2$, $n\ell\leq\beta_*^2$, and $t\geq0$, let $m_b$ be the pinned
mean and $m^{\setminus i}$ the cavity mean for coordinate $i$.
For every fixed $p\geq2$, there is a constant $C_p$, depending only
on $p,\beta_*$, such that for every $i$, $b\in\{\pm1\}$, and $j\ne i$,
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}^{1/p}\leq C_pn^{-1/2},
 \qquad \|m_j-m_j^{\setminus i}\|_{L^p}\leq C_pn^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-33 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
We express the mean difference as an increment along the same path $y_{-i}+a_s$ used for the residual increment.  We first condition on $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},X_i=b)$, so that the interaction $J^{\setminus i}$ and the initial field $y_{-i}$ are fixed.
{Here $M=n-1$, and $\Sigma(v)$ denotes the covariance for interaction matrix $J^{\setminus i}$ and external field $v$.}
With ${m_{J^{\setminus i}}}(v)$ denoting the mean at external field $v$ and interaction $J^{\setminus i}$, we have that
\begin{equation*}
 m_j^{\setminus i}={m_{J^{\setminus i},j}}(y_{-i}),\qquad
 (m_b)_j={m_{J^{\setminus i},j}}(y_{-i}+bJ_{-i,i}).
\end{equation*}
{By the endpoint-law comparison preceding \cref{eq:tap-matched-endpoint-law}, $bJ_{-i,i}$ and $a_\ell$ have the same law given $(\mathcal F^{\setminus i},X_i=b)$.}
{Subtracting the fixed initial mean, we obtain, under this law,}
\begin{equation*}
 (m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}
 \overset{\mathrm d}{=}{m_{J^{\setminus i},j}}(y_{-i}+a_\ell)-{m_{J^{\setminus i},j}}(y_{-i}).
\end{equation*}
Along this path, we apply \Cref{lem:app-wpi-observation-law} in dimension $M$ with base distribution $\mu=\mu^{\setminus i}$ and additional observation $a_s$.
The tilt of the cavity distribution by $v$ has interaction matrix $J^{\setminus i}$ and total field $y_{-i}+v$.
The process $\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}=a_s-\int_0^s {m_{J^{\setminus i}}}(y_{-i}+a_u)\mathrm d u$ is Brownian motion in the filtration generated by $(a_u)_{0\leq u\leq s}$ {under the law above}, and
\begin{equation*}
 \mathrm d {m_{J^{\setminus i},j}}(y_{-i}+a_s)
 =\Sigma(y_{-i}+a_s)_{j,\cdot}\mathrm d\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}.
\end{equation*}
We integrate from $0$ to $\ell$ and use $a_0=0$ to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 {m_{J^{\setminus i},j}}(y_{-i}+a_\ell)-{m_{J^{\setminus i},j}}(y_{-i})
 =\int_0^\ell\Sigma(y_{-i}+a_s)_{j,\cdot}\mathrm d\widetilde B_s^{\mathrm{loc}}.
\end{equation*}
{Thus, after averaging over $\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$ under the conditional law given $X_i=b$, the mean difference has the law of this stochastic integral.}
{The following path norms are under the same conditional law and average jointly over $(J^{\setminus i},y_{-i})$, $\xi$, and $\widetilde B$.}
{For each $s\in[0,\ell]$, by \cref{eq:tap-path-planted-marginal} the pair $(J^{\setminus i},y_{-i}+a_s)$ has the planted distribution in dimension $M=n-1$, with the same parameter $\ell$ and time $t+s$.}
By \Cref{lem:tap-influence}-\textup{(ii)}, we have $\|\Sigma(y_{-i}+a_s)_{j,\cdot}\|_{L^p}\leq C_p$.
We apply BDG and then Minkowski in $L^{p/2}$ to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}^{1/p}
 \leq C_p\left(\int_0^\ell
       \|\Sigma(y_{-i}+a_s)_{j,\cdot}\|_{L^p}^2\mathrm d s\right)^{1/2}
 \leq C_p\sqrt\ell\leq C_pn^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}
Finally, $m_j-m_j^{\setminus i}=\sum_b\pi_b((m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i})$.
The means are $\mathcal F$-measurable, so Jensen's inequality and \cref{eq:tap-posterior-weighted-matching} give
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|m_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|m_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\right]}
 \leq\frac12\sum_b\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
 \leq C_p^p n^{-p/2}.
\end{equation*}
Taking $p$th roots proves the unconditional bound.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-34: body_md -->
\label{lem:tap-inverse-marginals}
Fix $S>0$.
Under the planted model of \Cref{def:tap-planted-model}, set $J=\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}$.
If $n\ell\leq\beta_*^2$ and $t\in[0,S]$, let
$m_j=\ifthenelse{\isempty{\sigma\sim\mu_{J,y}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sigma_j\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{J,y}}\left[\sigma_j\right]}$.  For every fixed $p\geq1$,
there is a constant $C_p$, depending only on $p,\beta_*,S$, such that
\begin{equation*}
 \max_{j\in[n]}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-p}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-p}\right]}\leq C_p.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-34 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
We adapt \cite[Lemma~5.3]{DaviesLeeSandhuShi26}, which is stated for $\beta<1/2$.
We will bound $(1-m_j^2)^{-p}$ by exponential moments of the total external field and the effect of row $j$.
We use the cavity model of \Cref{def:tap-cavity-pinning} at coordinate $j$, with $M=n-1$ and $a=a_j$.
The two unnormalized weights of $\sigma_j=\pm1$ are $\mathrm e^{y_j}\langle \mathrm e^{a\cdot\sigma}\rangle_{\mu^{\setminus j}}$ and $\mathrm e^{-y_j}\langle \mathrm e^{-a\cdot\sigma}\rangle_{\mu^{\setminus j}}$ respectively.
We take half the logarithm of their ratio and obtain
\[
 \operatorname{arctanh}\left(m_j\right)=y_j+\phi(a),\qquad
 \phi(a)=\tfrac12(\log\langle \mathrm e^{a\cdot\sigma}\rangle_{\mu^{\setminus j}}
              -\log\langle \mathrm e^{-a\cdot\sigma}\rangle_{\mu^{\setminus j}}).
\]
Since $(1-m_j^2)^{-p}=\cosh^{2p}(y_j+\phi(a))$, we have
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-p}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-p}\right]}
 \leq \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{2p|y_j|+2p|\phi(a)|}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\mathrm e^{2p|y_j|+2p|\phi(a)|}\right]}
 \leq \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{4p|y_j|}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\mathrm e^{4p|y_j|}\right]}^{1/2}
        \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{4p|\phi(a)|}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\mathrm e^{4p|\phi(a)|}\right]}^{1/2}.
\end{align*}
Thus it suffices to control these two exponential moments.

With $\mathcal F^{\setminus j}$ fixed, we differentiate the two log partition functions and obtain
\[
 \partial_{a_k}\phi(a)=\tfrac12(({m_{+1}})_k+({m_{-1}})_k),
\]
so $|\partial_{a_k}\phi|\le1$ and
$\left\Vert\nabla_a\phi\right\Vert_2\le\sqrt M$.  Also $\phi(-a)=-\phi(a)$.
Since $G$ and $-G$ have the same law,
we obtain
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi(\sqrt\ell G)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\phi(\sqrt\ell G)\right]}
 =-\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi(\sqrt\ell G)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\phi(\sqrt\ell G)\right]}=0.
\]
The function $G\mapsto\phi(\sqrt\ell G)$ has the Lipschitz property
with constant at most $\sqrt{\ell M}\leq\beta_*$.
We apply the Gaussian exponential-moment bound
\cite[Theorem~5.5]{BoucheronLugosiMassart13} and obtain, for $s\ge0$,
\[
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{s|\phi(\sqrt\ell G)|}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\mathrm e^{s|\phi(\sqrt\ell G)|}\right]}
 \le \ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{s\phi(\sqrt\ell G)}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\mathrm e^{s\phi(\sqrt\ell G)}\right]}
     +\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,I_M)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{-s\phi(\sqrt\ell G)}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,I_M)}\left[\mathrm e^{-s\phi(\sqrt\ell G)}\right]}
 \le2\mathrm e^{s^2\beta_*^2/2}.
\]

The actual row is $a=\ell U+\sqrt\ell G$, where $U=X_jX_{-j}$
on the remaining coordinates.  Since
$\left\Vert\ell U\right\Vert_2=\ell\sqrt M$ and $\phi$ has the Lipschitz property
with constant $\sqrt M$, we have
$|\phi(\ell U+\sqrt\ell G)-\phi(\sqrt\ell G)| \le\ell M\le \beta_*^2$.

{We then average the preceding row bound over $(\mathcal F^{\setminus j},X)$ and obtain $\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{s|\phi(a)|}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\mathrm e^{s|\phi(a)|}\right]}\leq2\exp(s\beta_*^2+s^2\beta_*^2/2)$.}
{For the external field, given $X$, we have} $y_j\sim N(tX_j,t)$.

{Given $X$, the absolute value of the mean and the variance of $y_j$ are both $t\leq S$.}
For every $s\geq0$, we use the Gaussian MGF and
$\mathrm e^{s|z|}\leq\mathrm e^{sz}+\mathrm e^{-sz}$ to obtain $\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm e^{s|y_j|}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\mathrm e^{s|y_j|}\right]}\leq2\exp(sS+s^2S/2)$.
Substituting these bounds at $s=4p$ into the initial Cauchy--Schwarz
estimate proves $\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-p}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-p}\right]}
\leq\exp(C\cdot p^2+C\cdot p)$ after increasing $C$ for $p\geq1$.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
We fix $p\geq2$ and a deleted coordinate $i$, and set $M=n-1$.
{We first bound the Taylor remainders in \cref{eq:tap-child-entropy-expansion} using \cref{eq:tap-child-entropy-holder}.}
Under $X_i=b$, the pair $(J^{\setminus i},y_{-i})$ has the planted distribution
at dimension $M=n-1$ and time $t$, and the pair
$(J^{\setminus i},y_{-i}+bJ_{-i,i})$ has that distribution at time
$t+\ell$.  {Both use the same parameter $\ell$ and inverse temperature $\sqrt{M\ell}\leq\beta_*$.}
Since $t\leq T$ and $\ell<1$, both times lie in $[0,T+1]$.
We apply \Cref{lem:tap-inverse-marginals} with $S=T+1$ at order $4p$ to both means and
\Cref{lem:tap-mean-increments} at order $4p$ to their difference.
Substituting into \cref{eq:tap-child-entropy-holder}, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|\rho_j^b|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|\rho_j^b|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}^{1/p}\leq C_pn^{-1}.
\end{equation*}
Minkowski in $L^{p/2}$ under the same conditional law gives
\begin{equation*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\rho^b\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|\rho^b\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}^{2/p}
 \leq\sum_{j\ne i}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|\rho_j^b|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|\rho_j^b|^p\mid X_i=b\right]}^{2/p}
 \leq C_p^2Mn^{-2}\leq C_p^2n^{-1}.
\end{equation*}
Since $\rho^b$ is $\mathcal F$-measurable, Jensen's inequality and
\cref{eq:tap-posterior-weighted-matching} give
\begin{equation*}
 \left\|\sum_b\pi_b\rho^b\right\|_{L^p}
 \leq\left(\frac12\sum_b\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\rho^b\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|\rho^b\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}\right)^{1/p}
 \leq C_pn^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}

For $\overline\rho_j$, we use the full $n$-spin planted law.
{The endpoints are $m_j^{\setminus i}$ and $m_j$, so we apply \Cref{lem:tap-inverse-marginals} with the same choice $S=T+1$ to their inverse spin variances in dimensions $M$ and $n$, both at time $t$.}  H\"older and the bound on $m_j-m_j^{\setminus i}$ at order $4p$ give
\begin{equation*}
 \|\overline\rho_j\|_{L^p}
 \leq\|m_j-m_j^{\setminus i}\|_{L^{4p}}^2
 \left(\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(1-(m_j^{\setminus i})^2)^{-4p}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(1-(m_j^{\setminus i})^2)^{-4p}\right]}^{1/(2p)}
       +\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-4p}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[(1-m_j^2)^{-4p}\right]}^{1/(2p)}\right)
 \leq C_pn^{-1}.
\end{equation*}
Again, Minkowski in $L^{p/2}$ gives
$\|\overline\rho\|_{L^p}^2\leq\sum_{j\ne i}
\|\overline\rho_j\|_{L^p}^2\leq C_p^2n^{-1}$.
We substitute these bounds in \cref{eq:tap-child-entropy-expansion}
to conclude $\|\Delta_{\mathrm{ent}}\|_{L^p}\leq C_pn^{-1/2}$.

{For $\Delta_Q$, we use the algebra preceding \cref{eq:tap-recombination} to write}
\begin{equation*}
 \Delta_Q=\ell(1-m_i^2)m_{-i}
       +\ell\left(\sum_b\pi_b\|m_b\|_2^2m_b-\|m_{-i}\|_2^2m_{-i}\right).
\end{equation*}
To separate the terms that cancel in this weighted difference, we first
write $\|a+u\|_2^2=\|a\|_2^2+2\langle a,u\rangle+\|u\|_2^2$ and multiply by $a+u$:
\begin{align*}
 \|a+u\|_2^2(a+u)
 =&~\|a\|_2^2a+\|a\|_2^2u+2\langle a,u\rangle\,a
       +2\langle a,u\rangle\,u+\|u\|_2^2a+\|u\|_2^2u.
\end{align*}
With $a$ fixed, the term $\|a\|_2^2a$ is constant in $u$, and
$\|a\|_2^2u+2\langle a,u\rangle\,a$ is linear in $u$.
We subtract these terms and denote the remaining quadratic and cubic
terms by
\begin{equation*}
 \rho_Q(a,u)
 \triangleq \|a+u\|_2^2(a+u)-\|a\|_2^2a
       -(\|a\|_2^2u+2\langle a,u\rangle\,a)
 =2\langle a,u\rangle\,u+\|u\|_2^2a+\|u\|_2^2u.
\end{equation*}
If $a,a+u\in[-1,1]^M$, then $\|a\|_2\leq\sqrt M$ and
$\|u\|_2\leq2\sqrt M$.  Thus
$\|\rho_Q(a,u)\|_2\leq5\sqrt M\|u\|_2^2$.
Expanding both cubic terms about $m^{\setminus i}$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \|m_b\|_2^2m_b
 =\|m^{\setminus i}\|_2^2m^{\setminus i}
       +\|m^{\setminus i}\|_2^2(m_b-m^{\setminus i})
 +2\langle m^{\setminus i},m_b-m^{\setminus i}\rangle m^{\setminus i}
       +\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_b-m^{\setminus i}),
\end{align*}
\[
 \|m_{-i}\|_2^2m_{-i}
 =\|m^{\setminus i}\|_2^2m^{\setminus i}
       +\|m^{\setminus i}\|_2^2(m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i})+2\langle m^{\setminus i},m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i}\rangle m^{\setminus i}
       +\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i}).
\]
We multiply the first identity by $\pi_b$, sum over $b$, and subtract
the second identity.  The constant terms cancel because
$\sum_b\pi_b=1$.  For the linear terms, we use
\begin{align*}
 \sum_b\pi_b(m_b-m^{\setminus i})=&~m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i},\\
 \sum_b\pi_b\langle m^{\setminus i},m_b-m^{\setminus i}\rangle
 =&~\langle m^{\setminus i},m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i}\rangle.
\end{align*}
Thus only the remainders remain, and we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_b\pi_b\|m_b\|_2^2m_b-\|m_{-i}\|_2^2m_{-i}
 =\sum_b\pi_b\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_b-m^{\setminus i})
       -\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i}).
\end{equation*}
For the squared vector increments, we apply Minkowski in $L^p$
and \Cref{lem:tap-mean-increments} at order $2p$:
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^{2p}\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|m_b-m^{\setminus i}\|_2^{2p}\mid X_i=b\right]}^{1/p}
 \leq&~\sum_{j\ne i}\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^{2p}\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[|(m_b)_j-m_j^{\setminus i}|^{2p}\mid X_i=b\right]}^{1/p}
 \leq C_p,\\
 \|m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i}\|_{L^{2p}}^2
 \leq&~\sum_{j\ne i}\|m_j-m_j^{\setminus i}\|_{L^{2p}}^2\leq C_p.
\end{align*}
We substitute into $\|\rho_Q(a,u)\|_2\leq5\sqrt M\|u\|_2^2$ and obtain
\begin{align*}
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_b-m^{\setminus i})\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_b-m^{\setminus i})\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}^{1/p}
 \leq&~C_p\sqrt M,\\
 \|\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_{-i}-m^{\setminus i})\|_{L^p}
 \leq&~C_p\sqrt M.
\end{align*}
We apply Jensen's inequality and \cref{eq:tap-posterior-weighted-matching}
to the weighted sum of $\rho_Q(m^{\setminus i},m_b-m^{\setminus i})$,
which are $\mathcal F$-measurable, and use
$\|m_{-i}\|_2\leq\sqrt M$ for the remaining term.  Therefore
\begin{equation*}
 \|\Delta_Q\|_{L^p}\leq\ell\sqrt M+C_p\ell\sqrt M
 \leq C_pn^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*}
{Combining the bounds for $\Delta_{\mathrm{ent}}$ and $\Delta_Q$ proves the lemma.}
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
We fix a deleted coordinate $i$ and a moment order $p\geq2$.
We recall that $\iota_i:\mathbb R^{n-1}\to\mathbb R^n$ inserts a zero
at coordinate $i$, so
$\left\Vert\iota_i\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\right\Vert_2
=\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2$.
It suffices to bound $\left\Vert\mathbf r-\iota_i\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\right\Vert_{L^p}$,
because the reverse triangle inequality bounds the difference of the
two Euclidean norms by this vector difference.

Both $\mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^b$ and $\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$
are functions of $(J,y)$.  We apply Jensen's inequality and
\cref{eq:tap-posterior-weighted-matching} to their difference, then use
\cref{eq:tap-matched-row-increment} to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \left\|\sum_b\pi_b(\mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^b-\mathbf r^{\setminus i})\right\|_{L^p}
 \leq\left(\frac12\sum_b
 \ifthenelse{\isempty{}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^b-\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{}\left[\|\mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^b-\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2^p\mid X_i=b\right]}\right)^{1/p}
 \leq C_pn^{-1/4}.
\end{equation*}

We now use \cref{eq:tap-recombination} on the coordinates different
from $i$.  Coordinate $i$ of
$\mathbf r-\iota_i\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$ equals $r_i$.
We apply the triangle inequality in vector $L^p$ to obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\mathbf r-\iota_i\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\right\Vert_{L^p}
 \leq&~\left\Vertr_i\right\Vert_{L^p}
       +\left\|\sum_{b=\pm1}\pi_b
          (\mathbf r_{\mathrm{pin}}^b-\mathbf r^{\setminus i})
         \right\|_{L^p}
       +\left\Vert\Delta_{\mathrm{ent}}\right\Vert_{L^p}+\left\Vert\Delta_Q\right\Vert_{L^p}\\
 \leq&~C_pn^{-1/2}+C_pn^{-1/4}+C_pn^{-1/2}
 \leq C'_pn^{-1/4}.
\end{align*}
Here we used \Cref{lem:tap-complete-row} at order $p$ for $r_i$,
the weighted-sum estimate above, and
\Cref{lem:tap-recombination-errors} at order $p$ for the two errors.
Their constants do not depend on $i$.

For every fixed integer $p\geq1$, we apply this vector estimate at order
$4p\geq2$ and use the reverse triangle inequality:
\begin{equation*}
 \max_i\bigl\|\,\|\mathbf r\|_2-\|\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\|_2\,\bigr\|_{L^{4p}}
 \leq\max_i\left\Vert\mathbf r-\iota_i\mathbf r^{\setminus i}\right\Vert_{L^{4p}}
 \leq C'_{4p}n^{-1/4}.
\end{equation*}
Renaming the constant proves the claimed bound.
Finally,
$\mathbf r^{\setminus i}
=R_{n-1}^{(Q)}(m^{\setminus i};J^{\setminus i},y_{-i})$,
and $m^{\setminus i}$ is a ratio of finite Gibbs sums determined by
$(J^{\setminus i},y_{-i})$.
Therefore $\mathbf r^{\setminus i}$ is
$\mathcal F^{\setminus i}$-measurable, as asserted.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- lemma-35: body_md -->
\label{prop:fixed-time-amp-tap-input}
Fix $0<\beta<1$ and $s>0$.
There are constants $r,\kappa>0$, depending only on $(\beta,s)$, such that, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there is an integer $K_0=K_0(\beta,s,\varepsilon)$ with the following properties for every fixed $K\geq K_0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item Under $\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}$, with probability tending to one as $n\to\infty$, the pair $(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_s)$ satisfies $\varepsilon$-small TAP residual and $(r,\kappa)$-local strong convexity at time $s$ and AMP depth $K$, as defined in \Cref{def:amp-tap-geometry}.
\item The posterior-mean approximation satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\ifthenelse{\isempty{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[
 \frac{\|m(A,Y_s)-\widehat m_K(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_s)\|_2^2}{n}\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}}\left[
 \frac{\|m(A,Y_s)-\widehat m_K(A^{\mathrm{full}},Y_s)\|_2^2}{n}\right]}
 \leq\varepsilon^2.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-35 -->
<!-- lemma-36: body_md -->
\label{lem:inverse-poly-planted-norm-tail}
Fix $0\leq\beta<1$.  For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, there are $c_{\beta,\varepsilon},
C_{\beta,\varepsilon}>0$ such that
\begin{equation*}
  \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}\left\{
    \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}>2+\varepsilon\right\}
  \leq C_{\beta,\varepsilon}\mathrm{e}^{-c_{\beta,\varepsilon}n}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-36 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
{Under the original augmented law,}
$A^{\mathrm{full}}=G+\left(\beta/n\right)I_n$, where $G$ is a centered GOE matrix.
By \cite[Section~3.3]{BandeiraElAlaouiRodder26}, for every $u\geq0$, we have
$\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim\mathrm{GOE}(n)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left\VertG\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\geq2+u\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{G\sim\mathrm{GOE}(n)}\left[\left\VertG\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\geq2+u\right]}
\leq2\mathrm{e}^{-nu^2/4}$.
For $n\geq2\beta/\varepsilon$, we have
$\left\Vert(\beta/n)I_n\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}=\beta/n\leq\varepsilon/2$. Hence,
\begin{align*}
  \mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}\left\{
    \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}>2+\varepsilon\right\}
  \leq\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim\mathrm{GOE}(n)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}\left[
    \left\VertG\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}>2+\varepsilon/2\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{P}}_{G\sim\mathrm{GOE}(n)}\left[
    \left\VertG\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}>2+\varepsilon/2\right]}
  \leq2\mathrm{e}^{-n\varepsilon^2/16}.
\end{align*}
We set $E=\{\left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}>2+\varepsilon\}$ and apply the forward probability comparison in \Cref{lem:planting-likelihood} to obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}(E)
 \leq(1-\beta^2)^{-1/4}\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}(E)^{1/2}.
\end{equation*}
Combining this with the tail bound under $\mathbb P_{0,n,\beta}^{\mathrm{aug}}$, for $n\geq2\beta/\varepsilon$ we obtain
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb P_{n,\beta}^{\mathrm{pl,aug}}(E)
 \leq(1-\beta^2)^{-1/4}\left(2\mathrm{e}^{-n\varepsilon^2/16}\right)^{1/2}
 =\sqrt2(1-\beta^2)^{-1/4}\mathrm{e}^{-n\varepsilon^2/32}.
\end{equation*}
This finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- lemma-37: body_md -->
\label{lem:inverse-poly-fixed-depth-stability}
Fix $0<\beta_0<\beta_1<1$ and an integer $K\geq0$.
{There is a constant $C=C_{\beta_0,\beta_1,K}$ such that the following bounds hold for every $\beta,\theta\in[\beta_0,\beta_1]$, $s,t\geq0$, symmetric matrices $A^{\mathrm{full}},\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}$ with operator norms at most $4$, and fields $y,\widetilde y\in\mathbb{R}^n$.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The scalar fixed points satisfy
\begin{equation*}
 \left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\theta(s)\right\vert
 \leq\frac{\left\vertt-s\right\vert+2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert}{1-\beta_1^2}.
\end{equation*}
\item The AMP centers and their TAP residuals satisfy
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\widehat m_{K,\beta}\left(A^{\mathrm{full}},y\right)
       -\widehat m_{K,\theta}\left(\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}},\widetilde y\right)\right\Vert_2
 \leq C\left(\sqrt n
   \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
   +\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2+\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert\right),
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
 &~\left\|R_{\mathrm{TAP},\beta}\left(
     \widehat m_{K,\beta}\left(A^{\mathrm{full}},y\right);
     A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t)\right)
   -R_{\mathrm{TAP},\theta}\left(
     \widehat m_{K,\theta}\left(\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}},\widetilde y\right);
     \widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}},\widetilde y,q_\theta(s)\right)
   \right\|_2\\
 \leq&~C\left(\sqrt n
   \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
   +\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2
   +\sqrt n\left(\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert+\left\vertt-s\right\vert\right)\right).
\end{align*}
\item For every $u\in(-1,1)^n$, the TAP Hessians satisfy
\begin{align*}
 &~\left\|\nabla_u^2F_{\mathrm{TAP},\beta}
       \left(u;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t)\right)
   -\nabla_u^2F_{\mathrm{TAP},\theta}
       \left(u;\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}},\widetilde y,q_\theta(s)\right)
       \right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\\
 \leq&~C\left(
   \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
   +\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert+\left\vertt-s\right\vert\right).
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-37 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
For part~\textup{(i)}, recall the scalar channel function
$\psi(\gamma)=\ifthenelse{\isempty{G\sim N(0,1)}}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}\left[\tanh^2\left(\gamma+\sqrt\gamma G\right)\right]}
    {\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim N(0,1)}\left[\tanh^2\left(\gamma+\sqrt\gamma G\right)\right]}$,
which is one-Lipschitz by \cite[Lemma~5.6(a),(b)]{ElAlaouiMontanariSellke25}.
The fixed-point equations are
$q_\beta(t)=\psi\left(t+\beta^2q_\beta(t)\right)$ and
$q_\theta(s)=\psi\left(s+\theta^2q_\theta(s)\right)$.  Hence
\begin{align*}
 \left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\theta(s)\right\vert
 =&~\left\vert\psi\left(t+\beta^2q_\beta(t)\right)
       -\psi\left(s+\theta^2q_\theta(s)\right)\right\vert\\
 \leq&~\left\vertt-s+\beta^2q_\beta(t)-\theta^2q_\theta(s)\right\vert\\
 =&~\left\vertt-s+\beta^2\left(q_\beta(t)-q_\theta(s)\right)
       +(\beta^2-\theta^2)q_\theta(s)\right\vert\\
 \leq&~\left\vertt-s\right\vert+\beta^2\left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\theta(s)\right\vert
       +\left\vert\beta^2-\theta^2\right\vert q_\theta(s).
\end{align*}
Since $0\leq q_\theta(s)\leq1$, $\beta^2\leq\beta_1^2$, and
$\left\vert\beta^2-\theta^2\right\vert=(\beta+\theta)\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
\leq2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\theta(s)\right\vert
 \leq&~\left\vertt-s\right\vert+\beta_1^2\left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\theta(s)\right\vert
       +2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert.
\end{align*}
Moving the term $\beta_1^2\left\vertq_\beta(t)-q_\theta(s)\right\vert$ to the left
and dividing by $1-\beta_1^2>0$ proves part~\textup{(i)}.

{For part~\textup{(ii)}, we extend the recursion argument of \cite[Lemma~5.17]{ElAlaouiMontanariSellke25} to matrix, field, and inverse-temperature perturbations.}  Write $z_k,m_k$ and
$\widetilde z_k,\widetilde m_k$ for the AMP iterates at the two inputs,
with $m_k=\tanh(z_k)$ and $\widetilde m_k=\tanh(\widetilde z_k)$.
Set $Q_k=\left\Vertm_k\right\Vert_2^2/n$ and
$\widetilde Q_k=\left\Vert\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2^2/n$.
Since the coordinates of both vectors lie in $[-1,1]$,
$\left\vertQ_k-\widetilde Q_k\right\vert\sqrt n\leq2\left\Vertm_k-\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2$.
{The matrix and inverse-temperature coefficients satisfy}
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}-\theta\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
 \leq&~\beta_1
   \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
   +4\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert,\\
 \left\vert\beta^2-\theta^2\right\vert\leq&~2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert.
\end{align*}
By definition, we have
\begin{align*}
 z_{k+1}=&~\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}m_k+y-\beta^2(1-Q_k)m_{k-1},\\
 \widetilde z_{k+1}=&~\theta\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\widetilde m_k
       +\widetilde y-\theta^2(1-\widetilde Q_k)\widetilde m_{k-1}.
\end{align*}
Subtracting and separating the changes in the iterates from those in the coefficients, {we obtain}
\begin{align*}
 z_{k+1}-\widetilde z_{k+1}
 =&~\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}(m_k-\widetilde m_k)
    +(\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}-\theta\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}})
       \widetilde m_k+(y-\widetilde y)
 -\beta^2(1-Q_k)(m_{k-1}-\widetilde m_{k-1})\\
 &~-(\beta^2(1-Q_k)-\theta^2(1-\widetilde Q_k))
       \widetilde m_{k-1}.
\end{align*}
For the matrix terms, $\left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq4$
and $\left\Vert\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2\leq\sqrt n$ imply
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vert\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}(m_k-\widetilde m_k)\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~4\beta_1\left\Vertm_k-\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2,\\
 \left\Vert(\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}-\theta\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}})
       \widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~\beta_1\sqrt n
       \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
       +4\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert.
\end{align*}
For the Onsager terms, $0\leq Q_k,\widetilde Q_k\leq1$, so the first
has norm at most $\beta_1^2\left\Vertm_{k-1}-\widetilde m_{k-1}\right\Vert_2$.
The coefficient in the last term satisfies
\begin{align*}
 \left\vert\beta^2(1-Q_k)-\theta^2(1-\widetilde Q_k)\right\vert
 =&~\left\vert(\beta^2-\theta^2)(1-Q_k)
             +\theta^2(\widetilde Q_k-Q_k)\right\vert
 \leq 2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
             +\beta_1^2\left\vertQ_k-\widetilde Q_k\right\vert.
\end{align*}
Since $\left\Vert\widetilde m_{k-1}\right\Vert_2\leq\sqrt n$, the bound on
$\left\vertQ_k-\widetilde Q_k\right\vert$ above bounds the last term by
\begin{equation*}
 2\beta_1\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
 +2\beta_1^2\left\Vertm_k-\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2.
\end{equation*}
The triangle inequality therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vertz_{k+1}-\widetilde z_{k+1}\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~(4\beta_1+2\beta_1^2)\left\Vertm_k-\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2
       +\beta_1^2\left\Vertm_{k-1}-\widetilde m_{k-1}\right\Vert_2\\
 &~+\beta_1\sqrt n
       \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
       +(4+2\beta_1)\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
       +\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2.
\end{align*}
Absorbing the fixed coefficients into $C$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vertz_{k+1}-\widetilde z_{k+1}\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~C\left(\left\Vertm_k-\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2
       +\left\Vertm_{k-1}-\widetilde m_{k-1}\right\Vert_2
       +\sqrt n\left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}
                         -\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
       +\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert\right)
       +\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2.
\end{align*}
Take $C\geq1$ in this recursion.  To make the induction explicit, set
$c_{-1}=c_0=0$ and $c_{k+1}=C(c_k+c_{k-1}+1)$.
We claim that, for every $k\geq0$,
\begin{equation*}
 \left\Vertz_k-\widetilde z_k\right\Vert_2
 \leq c_k\left(\sqrt n
 \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
 +\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2+\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert\right).
\end{equation*}
At $k=0$, both vectors are zero.  Also $m_{-1}=\widetilde m_{-1}=0$.
Since $\tanh$ is one-Lipschitz,
$\left\Vertm_k-\widetilde m_k\right\Vert_2\leq\left\Vertz_k-\widetilde z_k\right\Vert_2$.
If the above bound holds at steps $k$ and $k-1$, substituting
these bounds into the recursion gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\Vertz_{k+1}-\widetilde z_{k+1}\right\Vert_2
 \leq&~C(c_k+c_{k-1}+1)
 \left(\sqrt n
 \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
 +\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2+\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert\right).
\end{align*}
For $k=0$, the same calculation uses the zero initial $m_{-1}$ vectors.
The coefficient is precisely $c_{k+1}$, completing the induction.
{At the fixed depth $K$, $c_K$ depends only on the inverse-temperature interval and $K$.}  The bound for $m_K-\widetilde m_K$ follows by
applying the same one-Lipschitz property of $\tanh$.


Recall that
$R_{\mathrm{TAP},\beta}(u;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q)
=\operatorname{arctanh}(u)-y-\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}u
+\beta^2(1-q)u$.
To control the residuals, subtract their defining expressions:
\begin{align*}
 &~R_{\mathrm{TAP},\beta}\left(m_K;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t)\right)
   -R_{\mathrm{TAP},\theta}\left(\widetilde m_K;
        \widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}},\widetilde y,q_\theta(s)\right)\\
 =&~z_K-\widetilde z_K-(y-\widetilde y)
       -\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}(m_K-\widetilde m_K)
       -(\beta A^{\mathrm{full}}-\theta\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}})
           \widetilde m_K\\
 &~+\beta^2(1-q_\beta(t))(m_K-\widetilde m_K)
       +(\beta^2(1-q_\beta(t))-\theta^2(1-q_\theta(s)))
           \widetilde m_K.
\end{align*}
{For the last term, we first separate the inverse-temperature and fixed-point perturbations, then apply part~\textup{(i)}:}
\begin{align*}
 \left\vert\beta^2(1-q_\beta(t))-\theta^2(1-q_\theta(s))\right\vert
 =&~\left\vert(\beta^2-\theta^2)(1-q_\beta(t))
            +\theta^2(q_\theta(s)-q_\beta(t))\right\vert\\
 \leq&~2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
             +\beta_1^2\left\vertq_\theta(s)-q_\beta(t)\right\vert\\
 \leq&~2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
       +\frac{\beta_1^2}{1-\beta_1^2}
          \left(\left\vertt-s\right\vert+2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert\right)\\
 =&~\frac{2\beta_1}{1-\beta_1^2}\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
       +\frac{\beta_1^2}{1-\beta_1^2}\left\vertt-s\right\vert.
\end{align*}

Taking norms in the residual decomposition and using
$\left\Vert\widetilde m_K\right\Vert_2\leq\sqrt n$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 &~\left\|R_{\mathrm{TAP},\beta}\left(m_K;A^{\mathrm{full}},y,q_\beta(t)\right)
   -R_{\mathrm{TAP},\theta}\left(\widetilde m_K;
        \widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}},\widetilde y,q_\theta(s)\right)\right\|_2\\
 \leq&~\left\Vertz_K-\widetilde z_K\right\Vert_2+\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2
       +(4\beta_1+\beta_1^2)\left\Vertm_K-\widetilde m_K\right\Vert_2\\
 &~+\sqrt n\left(
       \beta_1\left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
       +4\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert
       +\frac{2\beta_1\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert+\beta_1^2\left\vertt-s\right\vert}
                    {1-\beta_1^2}\right)\\
 \leq&~((1+4\beta_1+\beta_1^2)c_K+1)
       \left(\sqrt n
       \left\VertA^{\mathrm{full}}-\widetilde A^{\mathrm{full}}\right\Vert_{\mathrm{op}}
       +\left\Verty-\widetilde y\right\Vert_2+\sqrt n\left\vert\beta-\theta\right\vert\right)\\
 &~+\sqrt n\left(
